A Domain-Theoretic Approach to Statistical Programming Languages
Jean Goubault-Larrecq, Xiaodong Jia, Clément Théron

Abstract
We give a domain-theoretic semantics to a statistical programming language, using the plain old category of dcpos, in contrast to some more sophisticated recent proposals. Remarkably, our monad of minimal valuations is commutative, which allows for program transformations that permute the order of independent random draws, as one would expect. A similar property is not known for Jones and Plotkin’ s monad of continuous valuations. Instead of working with true real numbers, we work with exact real arithmetic, providing a bridge towards possible implementations. (Implementations by themselves are not addressed here.) Rather remarkably, we show that restricting ourselves to minimal valuations does not restrict us much: all measures on the real line can be modeled by minimal valuations on the domain 𝐈 ​ ℝ ⊥ \mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot} of exact real arithmetic. We give three operational semantics for our language, and we show that they are all adequate with respect to the denotational semantics. We also explore quite a few examples in order to demonstrate that our semantics computes exactly as one would expect, and in order to debunk the myth that a semantics based on continuous maps would not be expressive enough to encode measures with non-compact support using only measures with compact support, or to encode measures via non-continuous density functions, for instance. Our examples also include some useful, non-trivial cases of distributions on higher-order objects.



1
Introduction

The purpose of this paper is to give a simple, domain-theoretic semantics
to statistical programming languages.

Statistical programming languages such as Church
[
Goodman et al., 2008
]
,
Anglican
[
Wood et al., 2014
]
, WebPPL
[
Goodman and Stuhlmüller, 2014
]
or Venture

[
Mansinghka et al., 2014
]
, were introduced as a convenient means to describe
and implement so-called stochastic generative processes. Those are
randomized programs that describe probability distributions.

Initial proposals focused on implementations. One of the first
proposals for a formal semantics of such a language, SFPC, is due to
Vákár et al.
[
Vákár et al., 2019
]
, and is based on

quasi-Borel predomains
, a notion that expands on the clever
notion of
quasi-Borel spaces

[
Heunen et al., 2017
]
, with
additional domain-theoretic structure.

The constructions of
[
Vákár et al., 2019
]
are rather involved, and one may
wonder there would exist simpler denotational semantics for such
languages. We will give one, based on domain theory alone. Domain
theory is probably one of the oldest mathematical basis for
denotational semantics
[
Scott, 1969
]
. It is a common belief that it
would be inadequate for giving semantics to probabilistic languages.
This may be due to a superficial reading of a famous paper by A. Jung
and R. Tix
[
Jung and Tix, 1998
]
. And indeed, there are several purely
domain-theoretic semantics of probabilistic languages

[
Jones, 1990
,
Goubault-Larrecq, 2015
,
Goubault-Larrecq, 2019
]
.

However, it is true that statistical probabilistic languages present
additional challenges to the semanticist. Those are caused by several
additional features that one must take into account: a native type of
real numbers, continuous distributions, and perhaps most importantly,

soft constraints

[
Staton et al., 2016
]
. The latter is a
convenient way of implementing the computation of conditional
distributions, or smoothed versions therefore, and is implemented by a
primitive called
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}
in SFPC.

One difference between the quasi-Borel semantics of SFPC

[
Vákár et al., 2019
]
, or that of PCFSS
[
Dal Lago and Hoshino, 2019
]
, or that of
PPCF
[
Ehrhard et al., 2018
]
, with our domain-theoretic semantics does not lie
in probabilities or soft constraints, but with the way we handle real
numbers. SFPC, PCFSS, and PPCF rely on
true
real numbers, that
is, values of type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
are interpreted as elements of
\mathbb{R}
.
We interpret values of type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
as so-called
exact
real
numbers, namely as elements of a dcpo
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
of interval
approximations of real numbers, as in RealPCF and other proposals for
so-called exact real arithmetic

[
Boehm et al., 1986
,
Lester, 1992
,
Sünderhauf, 1995
,
Escardó, 1996
,
Escardó, 1996
,
Edalat et al., 1997
,
Plume, 1998
,
Marcial-Romero, 2004
,
Ho, 2013
]
.
This is the most natural choice with domain-theoretic semantics, as

\mathbb{R}
itself is not a dcpo (more precisely, the topology of
\mathbb{R}

cannot be obtained as a Scott topology), but embeds naturally in a
domain of interval approximations. This is also a natural bridge to
implementations; although we will not pursue this topic in depth, the
final section of this paper will give hints.

Contributions.

The main contribution of this paper is, therefore, a simple, purely
domain-theoretic denotational semantics for a statistical programming
language with exact real arithmetic, continuous distributions and soft
constraints, featuring full recursion. Additionally, and as in
previous proposals such as
[
Vákár et al., 2019
]
, our monad of so-called

minimal valuations
implementing probabilistic choice is
commutative. This is crucial in establishing the correctness of
run-of-the-mill program transformations such as permuting the order in
which two independent random variables are drawn. In domain-theoretic
semantics of probabilistic programming languages based on Jones and
Plotkin’s continuous valuations
[
Jones and Plotkin, 1989
,
Jones, 1990
]
, it was not
known how to achieve this, at least until recent work by Jia,
Lindenhovius, Mislove and Zamdzhiev
[
Jia et al., 2021
]
. (We made
that discovery at the same time that they did: see the final related
work section for details.) For the moment, let us just say that there
are two differences between our work and theirs. The less significant
one is probably that we work with (minimal) valuations that are
unbounded, as required to give semantics to
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}
, while they work
with subprobability valuations. The more significant one is that they
do not handle
continuous
distributions. It is a priori unclear
which continuous distributions on
\mathbb{R}
can be represented as

minimal
valuations. Being minimal, indeed, is a genuine
restriction: as we will see, Lebesgue measure is a continuous
valuation on
\mathbb{R}
that is not minimal. In spite of this, and this
is perhaps the most pleasing aspect of the current work,
all

measures on
\mathbb{R}
—including Lebesgue measure—can be realized as
minimal valuations on
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
, as we will show in
Section
6
. We will also provide an
extensive list of examples in Section
9
, by which we
hope to demonstrate that our approach is capable of defining a rich
set of distributions, including distributions on higher-order objects.

Outline.

We give some preliminary definitions in
Section
2
, where we also describe some of the
challenges in more detail. In Section
3
, we
recapitulate the construction and basic properties of the monad
\mathbf{V}

of continuous valuations. The commutativity of that monad is
equivalent to a form of the Fubini-Tonelli theorem, which in its most
basic form expresses an interchange property for double integrals.
Rather subtly, and perhaps paradoxically, such a Fubini-Tonelli
theorem is easy to obtain for the
\mathbf{V}
monad on the category
{\mathbf{Top}}

of topological spaces, but is an open problem on the subcategory

{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
of dcpos. We will explain the issue in
Section
4
, where we will see that
everything boils down to the fact that products in
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
are in
general different from products in the larger category
{\mathbf{Top}}
.

In order to obtain a Fubini-Tonelli theorem on
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
, we may opt to
restrict to, say, continuous dcpos, but this runs into some trouble,
as we will have seen in Section
2
. Our solution
is much simpler: we restrict continuous valuations to a submonad

\mathbf{V}_{m}
of so-called
minimal
valuations, and we show that

\mathbf{V}_{m}
is a commutative monad on
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
in
Section
5
. Now, since we will restrict our
valuations to be minimal, doesn’t this exclude some interesting
continuous distributions? And indeed that will seem to be the case:
as we have already mentioned, we will show that Lebesgue measure, seen
as a continuous valuation on
\mathbb{R}
, is not minimal. But (and again,
the shift is subtle), it
is
minimal on the domain
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}

that serves to do exact real arithmetic. In fact, as we show in
Section
6
,
every
measure on

\mathbb{R}
gives rise to a minimal valuation on
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
. We will even
give a simple, explicit description of the corresponding minimal
valuation as a supremum of a countable chain of simple valuations.

Having done all this preliminary work, we introduce a higher-order
statistical programming language, ISPCF, with continuous distributions
and soft constraints, as well as full recursion, in
Section
7
. Its denotational semantics is
somehow straightforward, considering our preparatory steps, and is
given in Section
8
. In order to get a grasp of what
we can express in ISPCF, and more importantly, how one can reason
about ISPCF programs using that semantics, we provide a rather
extensive list of examples in Section
9
. This
culminates with non-trivial examples of distributions on some
higher-order data types.

Finally, we will explore a few operational semantics for ISPCF, paving
the way for formally verified implementations. (We will not address
implementations per se in this paper.) The first operational
semantics for ISPCF we give is similar to some other earlier proposals

[
Vákár et al., 2019
,
Dal Lago and Hoshino, 2019
,
Ehrhard et al., 2018
]
, except that our real numbers
are exact reals, not true reals, and that our transition function is
continuous, not just measurable; it is given in
Section
10
. It is not too hard to give
another operational semantics which works with
true
reals, and
which is therefore even closer to the operational semantics given in

[
Vákár et al., 2019
,
Dal Lago and Hoshino, 2019
,
Ehrhard et al., 2018
]
; this is the
precise

operational semantics of Section
11
. Finally,
we give a sampling-based operational semantics in
Section
12
. The name “sampling-based” is by
analogy with early work by
[
Park et al., 2008
]
, and with one of the
semantics of
[
Dal Lago and Hoshino, 2019
]
. That semantics is meant to be one
step closer to an implementation: instead of drawing real numbers at
random with respect to arbitrary measures on
\mathbb{R}
, the
sampling-based semantics draws bits independently at random, and
derives certain continuous distributions from those bits. In each
case, we show that our operational semantics are sound and adequate
with respect to our denotational semantics.

We finish by reviewing related work in Section
13
,
and by concluding in Section
14
.

2
Preliminaries, Challenges

We refer to
[
Billingsley, 1986
]
for basics of measure theory, and to

[
Abramsky and Jung, 1994
,
Gierz et al., 2003
,
Goubault-Larrecq, 2013
]
for basics of domain theory and
topology.

2.1
Measure theory

A
\sigma
-algebra
on a set
X
is a collection of subsets
closed under countable unions and complements. A
measurable
space

X
is a set with a
\sigma
-algebra
\Sigma_{X}
. The
elements of
\Sigma_{X}
are usually called the
measurable
subsets
of
X
.

A
measure

\mu
on
X
is a
\sigma
-additive map from

\Sigma_{X}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, where
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is the set of extended
non-negative real numbers
\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{+\infty\}
. We will agree
that
0.(+\infty)=0
. (This makes multiplication on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

Scott-continuous, see below.) The property of

\sigma
-additivity
means that, for every countable family of
pairwise disjoint sets
E_{n}
,

\mu(\bigcup_{n}E_{n})=\sum_{n}\mu(E_{n})
. (Here
n
ranges over any
subset of
\mathbb{N}
, possibly empty.)

A
measurable map

f\colon X\to Y
between measurable spaces
is a map such that
f^{-1}(E)\in\Sigma_{X}
for every

E\in\Sigma_{Y}
. The
image measure

f[\mu]
of a measure

\mu
on
X
is defined by
f[\mu](E)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mu(f^{-1}(E))
.

The
\sigma
-algebra
\Sigma(A)

generated by
a family
A
of
subsets of
X
is the the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing
A
.
The
Borel
\sigma
-algebra
on a topological space is the

\sigma
-algebra generated by its topology. The
standard
topology
on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is generated by the intervals
[0,b[
,

]a,b[
and
]a,+\infty]
, with
0<a<b<+\infty
. Its Borel

\sigma
-algebra is also generated by just the intervals

]a,+\infty]
(the Scott-open subsets, see below). Hence a
measurable map
h\colon X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is a map such that

h^{-1}(]t,+\infty])\in\Sigma_{X}
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Its

Lebesgue integral
can be defined elegantly through

Choquet’s formula
:

\int_{X}hd\mu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\mu(h^{-1}(]t,+\infty]))dt
, where the right-hand integral is an ordinary Riemann integral.

This formula makes the following
change-of-variables formula
an
easy observation: for every measurable map
f\colon X\to Y
, for
every measurable map
h\colon Y\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,

\int_{Y}hdf[\mu]=\int_{X}(h\circ f)d\mu
.

There is a unique measure
\lambda
on
\mathbb{R}
such that

\lambda(]a,b[)=b-a
for every open bounded interval
]a,b[
.
This measure is called
Lebesgue measure
.

A measure
\mu
on
X
is
bounded
if and only if

\mu(X)<+\infty
. A measure
\mu
is
\sigma
-finite
if
there is a sequence

E_{0}\subseteq E_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq E_{n}\subseteq\cdots
of
measurable subsets of
X
whose union is
X
and such that

\mu(E_{n})<+\infty
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. A
\pi
-system

\Pi
on a set
X
is a family of sets closed under finite
intersections. If
X
is a measurable space such that

\Sigma_{X}=\Sigma(\Pi)
, any two
\sigma
-finite measures that agree
on
\Pi
also agree on
\Sigma_{X}
. In particular, Lebesgue measure on

\mathbb{R}
is uniquely defined by the specification

\lambda(]a,b[)=b-a
.

2.2
Domain theory and topology

A
dcpo
is a poset in which every directed family
D
has a
supremum
\sup D
. A prime example is
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, with the usual
ordering. Another example is
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
, the poset of closed intervals

[a,b]
with
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
and
a\leq b
, ordered by reverse
inclusion
\supseteq
. Every directed family

{([a_{i},b_{i}])}_{i\in I}
in
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
has a supremum

\bigcap_{i\in I}[a_{i},b_{i}]=[\sup_{i\in I}a_{i},\inf_{i\in I}b_{i}]
.
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
is the
lift
of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
, namely the dcpo
obtained by adding a fresh element
\bot
below all others. In
general, we define the lift
X_{\bot}
of a dcpo
X
similarly. In the
case of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
, we may equate
\bot
with the whole set
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}

itself, so that
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
is still ordered by reverse inclusion.

\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
will be the domain of interpretation of the type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}

of exact real numbers. Among them, we find the
total
numbers

a\in\mathbb{R}
, which we may equate with the maximal elements
[a,a]

of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
.

\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
is an example of a
pointed
dcpo, namely one that has
a least element, which we will always write as
\bot
, and which we read
as
bottom
.

The
standard topology
on
\mathbb{R}
is generated by the open
intervals
]a,b[
, with
a<b
. The map
i\colon a\mapsto[a,a]

is then a topological embedding of
\mathbb{R}
, with its standard
topology, into
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
(or
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
) with its Scott topology. In
other words,
i
is continuous, and every open subset
U
of
\mathbb{R}

is the inverse image of some Scott-open subset of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
(resp.,

\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
) by
i
. Explicitly,
]a,b[
is the inverse image of the
Scott-open subset of intervals
[c,d]
such that
a<c\leq d<b
.
This allows us to consider
\mathbb{R}
as a subspace of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
, resp.
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
.

We will also write
\leq
for the ordering on any poset. In the
example of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
,
\leq
is
\supseteq
. The

upward closure

\mathop{\uparrow}A
of a subset
A
of a poset
X
is

\{y\in X\mid\exists x\in A,x\leq y\}
. The
downward
closure

\mathop{\downarrow}A
is defined similarly. A set
A
is
upwards
closed
if and only if
A=\mathop{\uparrow}A
, and
downwards closed
if
and only if
A=\mathop{\downarrow}A
. A subset
U
of a poset
X
is

Scott-open
if and only if it is upwards closed and, for every
directed family
D
such that
\sup D
exists and is in
U
, some
element of
D
is in
U
already. The Scott-open subsets of a poset

X
form its
Scott topology
.

The
way-below
relation
\ll
on a poset
X
is defined by

x\ll y
if and only if, for every directed family
D
with a
supremum
z
, if
y\leq z
, then
x
is less than or equal to some
element of
D
already. We write
\hbox to0.0pt{$\uparrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\uparrow$}x
for

\{y\in X\mid x\ll y\}
, and
\hbox to0.0pt{$\downarrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\downarrow$}y
for

\{x\in X\mid x\ll y\}
. A poset
X
is
continuous
if and
only if
\hbox to0.0pt{$\downarrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\downarrow$}x
is directed and has
x
as supremum for every

x\in X
. A
basis

B
of a poset
X
is a subset of
X
such
that
\hbox to0.0pt{$\downarrow$\hss}\raise 2.15277pt\hbox{$\downarrow$}x\cap B
is directed and has
x
as supremum for every

x\in X
. A poset
X
is continuous if and only if it has a basis
(namely,
X
itself). A poset is
\omega
-continuous
if and
only if it has a countable basis. Examples include
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, with any
countable dense subset (with respect to its standard topology), such
as the rational numbers, or such as the
dyadic numbers

k/2^{n}

(
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
); or
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
, with the basis of
intervals
[a,b]
where
a
and
b
are both dyadic or both rational.

We write
{\mathcal{O}}X
for the lattice of open subsets of a topological
space. This applies to dcpos
X
as well, which will always be
considered with their Scott topology. The continuous maps

f\colon X\to Y
between two dcpos coincide with the

Scott-continuous
maps, namely the monotonic (order-preserving)
maps that preserve all directed suprema. We write
\mathcal{L}X
for the
space of continuous maps from a topological space
X
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, the
latter with its Scott topology, as usual. Such maps are usually
called
lower semicontinuous
, or
lsc
, in the mathematical
literature. Note that
\mathcal{L}X
, with the pointwise ordering, is a
dcpo.

There are several ways in which one can model probabilistic choice.
The most classical one is through measures. A popular alternative
used in domain theory is given by
continuous valuations

[
Jones and Plotkin, 1989
,
Jones, 1990
]
. A continuous valuation is a Scott-continuous
map
\nu\colon{\mathcal{O}}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
such that
\nu(\emptyset)=0

(
strictness
) and, for all
U,V\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,

\nu(U\cup V)+\nu(U\cap V)=\nu(U)+\nu(V)

(
modularity
).
Canonical examples of continuous valuations on
X
are
Dirac
valuations

\delta_{x}
for
x\in X
, where for each open subset
U

of
X
,
\delta_{x}(U)=1
if
x\in U
and
\delta_{x}(U)=0
, otherwise.
The set of all continuous valuations on
X

is denoted by
\mathbf{V}X
. We order
\mathbf{V}X
by the
stochastic
order
defined as
\nu_{1}\leq\nu_{2}
if and only if

\nu_{1}(U)\leq\nu_{2}(U)
for all opens of
X
. The set

\mathbf{V}X
is a dcpo in the stochastic order.

There is a notion of integral

\int_{x\in X}h(x)d\nu
, or briefly
\int hd\nu
, for every

h\in\mathcal{L}X
, which can again be defined by a Choquet formula.
Tix
[
Tix, 1995
, Satz 4.4]
showed that the integral is a
Scott-continuous bilinear form, namely:

•

for every
\nu\in\mathbf{V}X
, the map
h\in\mathcal{L}X\mapsto\int hd\nu
is Scott-continuous and linear, in the sense that

\int\alpha hd\nu=\alpha\int hd\nu
for every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
\int(h+h^{\prime})d\nu=\int hd\nu+\int h^{\prime}d\nu
, for all

h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}X
;

•

for every
h\in\mathcal{L}X
, the map
\nu\in\mathbf{V}X\mapsto\int hd\nu
is Scott-continuous and linear, in a similar sense.

More generally, a
linear
map
G\colon\mathcal{L}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

satisfies
G(h+h^{\prime})=G(h)+G(h^{\prime})
and
G(\alpha.h)=\alpha.G(h)

for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}X
. Conversely, any
Scott-continuous linear map
G\colon\mathcal{L}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is of the
form
h\mapsto\int hd\nu
for a unique continuous valuation
\nu
,
given by
\nu(U)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}G(\chi_{U})
, where
\chi_{U}
is the
characteristic map of
U
(
\chi_{U}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}1
if
x\in U
,
0

otherwise).

Continuous valuations and measures are pretty much the same thing on

\omega
-continuous dcpos, namely on continuous dcpos with a countable
basis. This holds more generally on de Brecht’s quasi-Polish spaces

[
de Brecht, 2013
]
, a class of spaces that contains not only the

\omega
-continuous dcpos from domain theory but also the Polish
spaces from topological measure theory.

One can see this as follows. In one direction, Adamski’s theorem
states that every measure
\mu
on a hereditarily Lindelöf space
X

is
\tau
-smooth
[
Adamski, 1977
, Theorem 3.1]
, meaning that
its restriction to the lattice of open subsets of
X
is a continuous
valuation. A hereditarily Lindelöf space is a space whose subspaces
are all Lindelöf, or equivalently a space in which every family of
open sets contains a countable subfamily with the same union. Every
second-countable space is hereditarily Lindelöf, and that includes
all quasi-Polish spaces. In the other direction, every continuous
valuation on an LCS-complete space extends to a Borel measure

[
de Brecht et al., 2019
, Theorem 1.1]
. An
LCS-complete
space is a
space that is homeomorphic to a
G_{\delta}
subset of a locally compact
sober space. Every quasi-Polish space is LCS-complete; in fact, the
quasi-Polish spaces are exactly the second-countable LCS-complete
spaces
[
de Brecht et al., 2019
, Theorem 9.5]
.

2.3
Is there any trouble with the probabilistic powerdomain?

The probabilistic powerdomain, namely the dcpo
\mathbf{V}X
of all
continuous valuations on a space
X
, ordered pointwise, is known to
have its problems
[
Jung and Tix, 1998
]
. Precisely, there is no known
Cartesian-closed category of continuous dcpos that is closed under the

\mathbf{V}
functor. It is sometimes believed that this means that domain
theory cannot be used to give semantics to higher-order probabilistic
languages. This would be wrong: the category
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
of
all

dcpos, not just the continuous dcpos,
is
Cartesian-closed and
closed under the
\mathbf{V}
functor
[
Jones and Plotkin, 1989
,
Jones, 1990
]
.

Continuity is not required to prove, say, soundness and adequacy
theorems using logical relations, as one realizes by reading the
relevant parts of
[
Streicher, 2002
]
, and as we will do in
Section
10.2
. But it is required to obtain a form of
the Fubini-Tonelli theorem, or, in categorical terms, to turn
\mathbf{V}

into a
commutative
monad. (See

[
Kock, 2012
, Theorem 9.2]
for the relation between the two
notions.) Commutativity is important in applications, as we will
briefly discuss in Remark
8.1
, and is a key ingredient of
the semantics of
[
Vákár et al., 2019
]
. We
do
obtain a commutative
monad, without any need for continuity, by a simple trick based on
inductive closures (Definition
5.1
,
Section
5
). This trick was found
independently by at least one other group of researchers

[
Jia et al., 2021
]
, but on different, and incomparable, monads.

2.4
Scoring, and density functions

Compared to ordinary probabilistic languages, statistical programming
languages aim to offer the possibility of computing conditional
distributions. This runs into questions on non-computability

[
Ackerman et al., 2011
]
. At this point, we note that there are
well-established theories of computable probability distributions,
notably on computable metric spaces
[
Galatolo et al., 2010
]
, on more
general computable topological spaces
[
Roy, 2011
]
, and also based
on Weihrauch’s type two theory of effectivity, also known as

TTE

[
Weihrauch, 1987
]
, see

[
Weihrauch, 1999
,
Schröder and Simpson, 2006
]
. In the absence of probabilities,
Schulz
[
Schulz, 2000
]
shows that the functions from

[0,1]^{n}
to
[0,1]
that are computable in the sense of TTE and of
RealPCF coincide.

In practice, a number of algorithms are implemented to compute certain
special conditional distributions, with a fallback strategy based on
one form or another of rejection sampling, or with a so-called

scoring
mechanism, which allows one to give more or less
importance to specific outcomes.

This scoring mechanism is typically implemented through a primitive
called
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}

[
Staton et al., 2016
,
Vákár et al., 2019
,
Dal Lago and Hoshino, 2019
,
Ehrhard et al., 2018
]
. Roughly,
the effect of
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;M
, where
M\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
evaluates to a
non-negative real number
\alpha
, is to multiply the ‘probability’ of
the current computation branch by
\alpha
—making it a measure
rather than a probability, whence the quotes.

As a consequence,
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}
can be used to build new measures

g\cdot\mu
from a measure
\mu
and a density function
g
. The
measure
g\cdot\mu
is defined by

(g\cdot\mu)(E)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{x}\chi_{E}(x)g(x)d\mu
, and is
sometimes written as
gd\mu
. Indeed, in case
x
is drawn at random
with respect to some measure
\mu
, writing
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;g(x);M(x)
will
have the effect of executing
M(x)
as though
x
had been drawn with
probability multiplied by
g(x)
, namely as though it had been drawn
at random with respect to
g\cdot\mu
. This interpretation conforms
to intuition if
g
really is a density function, namely if

\int_{x}g(x)d\mu=1
, in which case
g\cdot\mu
is a probability
distribution. In general, however,
g\cdot\mu
will be a measure.
As a simple, but extreme example,
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;0
annihilates the effect
of the current computation. If
x
is drawn with a measure whose
total mass is, say,
\pi/4
, then
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}(4/\pi)
will renormalize
the measure to a probability distribution. Other uses of
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}

include soft conditioning and Bayesian fitting, as illustrated in

[
Vákár et al., 2019
]
.

3
Monads of continuous valuations

We will describe probabilistic effects by following Moggi’s seminal
work on monads
[
Moggi, 1989
,
Moggi, 1991
]
. We use Manes’ presentation of
monads
[
Manes, 1976
]
: a monad
(T,\eta,\_^{\dagger})
on a
category
{\mathbf{C}}
is a function
T
mapping objects of
{\mathbf{C}}
to
objects of
{\mathbf{C}}
, a collection of morphisms

\eta_{X}\colon X\to TX
, one for each object
X
of
{\mathbf{C}}
, and
called the
unit
, and for every morphism
f\colon X\to TY
, a
morphism
f^{\dagger}\colon TX\to TY
called the
extension
of

f
; those are required to satisfy the axioms:

1.

f^{\dagger}\circ\eta_{X}=f
;

2.

\eta_{X}^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}_{TX}
;

3.

(g^{\dagger}\circ f)^{\dagger}=g^{\dagger}\circ f^{\dagger}
.

Then
T
extends to an endofunctor, acting on morphisms through

Tf=(\eta_{Y}\circ f)^{\dagger}
. Proposition
3.2

below is due to Jones
[
Jones, 1990
, Theorem 4.5]
. Her definition of
the integral was different, and she implicitly restricted valuations
to subprobability valuations. A similar statement is due to Kirch

[
Kirch, 1993
, Satz 6.1]
, for continuous dcpos instead of general
dcpos. Tix
[
Tix, 1995
]
was probably the first to use the Choquet
formula in this context. Her study was also restricted to continuous
dcpos.

We define
\eta_{X}\colon X\to\mathbf{V}X
as mapping
x\in X
to

\delta_{x}
. For every Scott-continuous map
f\colon X\to\mathbf{V}Y
,
for every
\mu\in\mathbf{V}X
, for every Scott-open subset
V
of
Y
, we
define:

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}(\mu)(V)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{x\in X}f(x)(V)d\mu.

(1)

The following lemma is proved exactly as in most of the references we
have just cited.

Lemma 3.1
.

For all dcpos
X
and
Y
, for every Scott-continuous map

f\colon X\to\mathbf{V}Y
,

(i)

the map
\eta_{X}
is Scott-continuous;

(ii)

the map
f^{\dagger}
is Scott-continuous from
\mathbf{V}X
to
\mathbf{V}Y
;

(iii)

for every
\mu\in\mathbf{V}X
, for every
g\in\mathcal{L}Y
,

\displaystyle\int_{y\in Y}g(y)df^{\dagger}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in X}\left(\int_{y\in Y}g(y)df(x)\right)d\mu.

(2)

(iv)

For every

h\in\mathcal{L}X
,
\int_{x^{\prime}\in X}h(x^{\prime})d\delta_{x}=h(x)
.

Proof.

(i) If
x\leq y
, then for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
, if

\delta_{x}(U)=1
then
x
is in
U
, so
y
is in
U
as well, and
therefore
\delta_{y}(U)=1
. It follows that

\delta_{x}\leq\delta_{y}
. Let
D
be any directed family in
X
,
and
x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sup D
. For every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
,
\delta_{x}(U)=1
if
and only if
x\in U
, if and only if some element
y\in D
is in

U
, by definition of Scott-open sets; and that is equivalent to

\sup_{y\in D}\delta_{y}(U)=1
.

(ii) We first verify that
f^{\dagger}(\mu)
is a continuous
valuation, for every
\mu\in\mathbf{V}X
. Strictness and modularity
follow easily from the fact that
f(x)\in\mathbf{V}Y
for every
x\in X
. Scott-continuity follows from the fact that the integral is a
(bilinear) form that is Scott-continuous in its function argument.
The integral is also Scott-continuous in its valuation argument, so

f^{\dagger}
is itself Scott-continuous.

(iii) We write
g
as the directed supremum of the maps
g_{K}
,
defined by
g_{K}(y)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{k=1}^{K2^{K}}\frac{1}{2^{K}}\chi_{g^{-1}(]\frac{k}{2^{K}},+\infty])}
,
K\in\mathbb{N}
. Then:

\displaystyle\int_{y\in Y}g_{K}(y)df^{\dagger}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K2^{K}}\frac{1}{2^{K}}\int_{y\in Y}\chi_{g^{-1}(]\frac{k}{2^{K}},+\infty])}(y)df^{\dagger}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K2^{K}}\frac{1}{2^{K}}f^{\dagger}(\mu)(g^{-1}(]\frac{k}{2^{K}},+\infty])

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{K2^{K}}\frac{1}{2^{K}}\int_{x\in X}f(x)(g^{-1}(]\frac{k}{2^{K}},+\infty])d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in X}\sum_{k=1}^{K2^{K}}\frac{1}{2^{K}}f(x)(g^{-1}(]\frac{k}{2^{K}},+\infty])d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in X}\left(\int_{y\in Y}g_{K}(y)df(x)\right)d\mu,

and the result follows by Scott-continuity of the integral in its
function argument and taking suprema, as
K
tends to
+\infty
.

(iv) The Choquet formula for the integral yields

\int_{x^{\prime}\in X}h(x^{\prime})d\delta_{x}=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\delta_{x}(h^{-1}(]t,+\infty]))dt=\int_{0}^{h(x)}1dt=h(x)
.
∎

Proposition 3.2
.

The triple
(\mathbf{V},\eta,\_^{\dagger})
is a monad on the category

{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
of dcpos and Scott-continuous maps. For every
Scott-continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
, for every
\mu\in\mathbf{V}X
,

\mathbf{V}f(\mu)
is the image valuation
f[\mu]
, defined by

f[\mu](V)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mu(f^{-1}(V))
, for every
V\in{\mathcal{O}}Y
.

Proof.

In light of Lemma
3.1
, the first part will be proved
once we have verified the three axioms given by Manes:

1.

f^{\dagger}\circ\eta_{X}
maps
x
to the continuous valuation

\nu
defined by
\nu(V)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{x\in X}f(x)(V)d\delta_{x}=f(x)(V)
; so
\nu=f(x)
, and therefore
f^{\dagger}\circ\eta_{X}=f
.

2.

\eta_{X}^{\dagger}
satisfies
\eta_{X}^{\dagger}(\mu)(U)=\int_{x\in X}\eta_{X}(x)(U)d\mu=\int_{x\in X}\chi_{U}(x)d\mu=\mu(U)
, so
\eta_{X}^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{V}X}
.

3.

For all
f\colon X\to\mathbf{V}Y
,
g\colon Y\to\mathbf{V}Z
, and

W\in{\mathcal{O}}Z
,

\displaystyle(g^{\dagger}\circ f)^{\dagger}(\mu)(W)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in X}g^{\dagger}(f(x))(W)d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in X}\left(\int_{y\in Y}g(y)(W)df(x)\right)d\mu,

while:

\displaystyle g^{\dagger}(f^{\dagger}(\mu))(W)

\displaystyle=\int_{y\in Y}g(y)(W)df^{\dagger}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in X}\left(\int_{y\in Y}g(y)(W)df(x)\right)d\mu,

by (
2
).

∎

4
The Fubini-Tonelli theorem, and what goes wrong with
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}

Jones proved a form of Fubini’s (more accurately, Tonelli’s) theorem
for (subprobability) continuous valuations, on continuous dcpos

[
Jones, 1990
]
. This actually generalizes to continuous valuations on
arbitrary topological spaces, and the proof is not that complicated,
as we demonstrate. (Another purpose we have in giving that proof is in
order to fix a gap in Jones’ proof, who actually does not show the
existence of the product valuation.)

As we will see in more detail later, Tonelli’s theorem turns
\mathbf{V}

into a commutative monad, and that is a basic requirement for being
able to say that drawing two objects at random independently can be
done in any order.

Proposition 4.1

(Fubini-Tonelli for continuous valuations on
{\mathbf{Top}}
)
.

Let
X
and
Y
be two spaces,
\mu\in\mathbf{V}X
,
\nu\in\mathbf{V}Y
.
There is a unique so-called product valuation
\mu\times\nu
on
X\times Y
such that, for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
and for every
V\in{\mathcal{O}}Y
,
(\mu\times\nu)(U\times V)=\mu(U).\nu(V)
. For
every
f\in\mathcal{L}(X\times Y)
,

\displaystyle\int_{(x,y)\in X\times Y}f(x,y)d(\mu\times\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in X}\left(\int_{y\in Y}f(x,y)d\nu\right)d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{y\in Y}\left(\int_{x\in X}f(x,y)d\mu\right)d\nu.

Proof.

(Sketch.) We first deal with uniqueness. We assume any
continuous valuation
\xi
on
X\times Y
such that

\xi(U\times V)=\mu(U).\nu(V)
for all
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
and

V\in{\mathcal{O}}Y
. We call any such product
U\times V
an

open rectangle
. The value of
\xi
on finite unions

W\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcup_{i\in J}U_{i}\times V_{i}
of open rectangles is
determined uniquely: either
\mu(U_{i}).\nu(U_{i})=+\infty
for
some
i\in J
, and then
\xi(W)
must be equal to
+\infty
, by
monotonicity; or
\xi(W)
must be equal to

\sum_{K\neq\emptyset,K\subseteq J}(-1)^{|K|+1}\xi(\bigcap_{i\in K}U_{i}\times V_{i})
by the so-called inclusion-exclusion
formula (an easy consequence of modularity), showing that
\xi(W)

is again determined uniquely. Finally, by definition of the product
topology, every open subset
W
of
X\times Y
is a union

\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}\times V_{i}
of open rectangles, hence a
directed union of finite unions
\bigcup_{i\in J}U_{i}\times V_{i}

(where
J
ranges over the finite subsets of
I
);
\xi(W)
is then
determined uniquely, since
\xi
is Scott-continuous.

For the existence part, the easiest route is to consider the two
maps
G,G^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{L}(X\times Y)\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
defined by:

\displaystyle G(f)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{x\in X}\left(\int_{y\in Y}f(x,y)d\nu\right)d\mu

\displaystyle G^{\prime}(f)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{y\in Y}\left(\int_{x\in X}f(x,y)d\mu\right)d\nu.

We check that
G
and
G^{\prime}
are Scott-continuous, linear maps, and
are therefore integral functionals for unique continuous valuations

\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
on
X\times Y
, respectively. For every open
rectangle
U\times V
,
\xi(U\times V)=G(\chi_{U\times V})

is equal to
\mu(U).\nu(V)
, and similarly for
\xi^{\prime}(U\times V)
. By the uniqueness part,
\xi=\xi^{\prime}
, so
G=G^{\prime}
. We write
\mu\times\nu
for
\xi
, and the theorem is proved.
∎

This proof is the core of several similar proofs. One of the closest
is due to Vickers
[
Vickers, 2011
]
, who proves a similar
theorem on the category of locales instead of
{\mathbf{Top}}
. Although
localic theorems usually generalize purely topological theorems, this
one does not, because locale products do not coincide with products in

{\mathbf{Top}}
in general
[
Isbell, 1981
, Theorem 2]
.

Since every dcpo can be seen as a topological space with its Scott
topology, it would seem that we would obtain a Fubini-Tonelli theorem
for Scott-continuous maps and continuous valuations on arbitrary
dcpos, as a special case of Proposition
4.1
.

This is not the case, but the reason is subtle. As with locales,
products in
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
do not usually coincide with products in
{\mathbf{Top}}
.
Explicitly, let us write
X_{\sigma}
for the topological space obtained
by equipping a dcpo
X
with its Scott topology. Then the topology on

(X\times Y)_{\sigma}
(where
\times
is dcpo product) is finer, and
in general strictly finer, than the product topology on

X_{\sigma}\times Y_{\sigma}
. (See Exercise 5.2.16 of

[
Goubault-Larrecq, 2013
]
for an example where it is strictly finer.) As a
consequence, there are more, and generally, strictly more
Scott-continuous maps from
X\times Y
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
than continuous
maps from the topological product
X_{\sigma}\times Y_{\sigma}
to

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. The Fubini-Tonelli formula holds for functions of the
second, smaller class, but it is unknown whether it holds for the
first kind of functions. One exception is when either
X
or
Y
is

core-compact
(the Scott-opens form a continuous lattice),
in which case the Scott and product topologies
coincide on
X\times Y

[
Gierz et al., 2003
, Theorem II-4.13]
; then
we retrieve Jones’ version of the Fubini-Tonelli theorem.

One may blame
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
for this state of affairs. Another possibility
is to consider that we are considering too general a notion of
continuous valuation. We will explore this avenue in the next
section, by restricting to so-called minimal valuations.

5
Minimal valuations, and Fubini-Tonelli again

A
simple valuation
is any finite linear combination

\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
of Dirac masses, with coefficients

r_{i}
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Definition 5.1

(Minimal valuations)
.

Let
\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{f}}X
be the poset of simple valuations on
X
, and

\mathbf{V}_{m}X
be the inductive closure of
\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{f}}X
in
\mathbf{V}X
.
The elements of
\mathbf{V}_{m}X
are called the
minimal valuations

on
X
.

The
inductive closure
of a subset
A
of a dcpo
Z
is the
smallest subset of
Z
that contains
A
and is closed under directed
suprema. It is obtained by taking all directed suprema of elements of

A
, all directed suprema of elements obtained in this fashion, and
proceeding this way transfinitely.

Remark 5.2
.

On a continuous dcpo
X
, every continuous valuation is a directed
supremum of simple valuations. In fact,
\mathbf{V}X
is a continuous
dcpo with a basis of simple valuations, as showed by Jones

[
Jones, 1990
]
, at least in the case of subprobability valuations.
(The general theorem can be found as Theorem IV-9.16 of

[
Gierz et al., 2003
]
.) Hence, in particular, continuous valuations and
minimal valuations agree on every continuous dcpo. One may wonder
whether all continuous valuations on a dcpo are minimal. We will
give a counterexample to this claim in a forthcoming paper.

5.1
The monad
\mathbf{V}_{m}
of minimal valuations

We will now show that
\mathbf{V}_{m}
defines a submonad of
\mathbf{V}
. To
this end, we need to know more about inductive closures. A

d-closed subset
of a dcpo
Z
is a subset
C
such that the
supremum of every directed family of elements of
C
, taken in
Z
, is
in
C
. The d-closed subsets form the closed subsets of a topology
called the
d-topology

[
Keimel and Lawson, 2008
, Section 5]
, and the
inductive closure of a subset
A
coincides with its
d-closure

cl_{d}(A)
, namely its closure in the d-topology.

We note that every Scott-continuous map is continuous with respect to
the underlying d-topologies. This is easily checked, or see

[
Keimel and Lawson, 2008
, Lemma 5.3]
. In particular:

Fact 5.3
.

For every Scott-continuous map
f\colon\mathbf{V}X\to\mathbf{V}Y
, for
every
A\subseteq\mathbf{V}X
,
f(cl_{d}(A))\subseteq cl_{d}(f(A))
.

Lemma 5.4
.

For every space
X
,
\mathbf{V}_{m}X
is closed under addition and
multiplication by elements of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, as computed in the larger
space
\mathbf{V}X
.

Proof.

Let us deal with addition. Multiplication is similar.

For every simple valuation
\mu
, the map

f_{\mu}\colon\nu\in\mathbf{V}X\mapsto\mu+\nu
is Scott-continuous,
and maps simple valuations to simple valuations. By
Fact
5.3
with
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{f}}X
,
f_{\mu}
maps all
elements of
cl_{d}(A)=\mathbf{V}_{m}X
to

cl_{d}(f_{\mu}(A))\subseteq cl_{d}(\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{f}}X)=\mathbf{V}_{m}X
.

It follows that for every minimal valuation
\nu
, the map

g\colon\mu\in\mathbf{V}X\mapsto\mu+\nu=f_{\mu}(\nu)
maps simple
valuations to minimal valuations. We observe that
g
is also
Scott-continuous. By Fact
5.3
with the same
A
as
above,
g
maps all elements of
cl_{d}(A)=\mathbf{V}_{m}X
to

cl_{d}(g(A))\subseteq cl_{d}(\mathbf{V}_{m}X)=\mathbf{V}_{m}X
. Hence, for
every
\nu\in\mathbf{V}_{m}X
, for every
\mu\in\mathbf{V}_{m}X
,
\mu+\nu

is in
\mathbf{V}_{m}X
.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

For any Scott-continuous map
f\colon X\to\mathbf{V}_{m}Y
,
f^{\dagger}

is a Scott-continuous map from
\mathbf{V}_{m}X
to
\mathbf{V}_{m}Y
.
Similarly, for every Scott-continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
,
\mathbf{V}f
is a Scott-continuous map from
\mathbf{V}_{m}X
to
\mathbf{V}_{m}Y
.

Proof.

For the first part, the only challenge is to show that, for every

\nu\in\mathbf{V}_{m}X
,
f^{\dagger}(\nu)
is in
\mathbf{V}_{m}Y
.
Scott-continuity follows from the fact that
f^{\dagger}
is
Scott-continuous from
\mathbf{V}X
to
\mathbf{V}Y
.

For every
\nu\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
in

\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{f}}X
,
f^{\dagger}(\nu)
is the continuous valuation

\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}f(x_{i})
: for every
V\in{\mathcal{O}}Y
,
f^{\dagger}(\nu)(V)=\int_{x\in X}f(x)(V)d\nu=\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}f(x_{i})(V)
.
By Lemma
5.4
, and since
f(x_{i})
is in
\mathbf{V}_{m}Y
for each
i
,
f^{\dagger}(\nu)
is in
\mathbf{V}_{m}Y
as well.

Hence
f^{\dagger}
maps
\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{f}}X
to
\mathbf{V}_{m}Y
. Using
Fact
5.3
with
A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{f}}X
,

f^{\dagger}(cl_{d}(A))=f^{\dagger}(\mathbf{V}_{m}X)
is included in

cl_{d}(f^{\dagger}(A))\subseteq cl_{d}(\mathbf{V}_{m}Y)=\mathbf{V}_{m}Y
.

The second part follows from the first part and the equation
\mathbf{V}f=(\eta_{Y}\circ f)^{\dagger}
.
∎

We observe that
\eta_{X}(x)=\delta_{x}
is in

\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{f}}X\subseteq\mathbf{V}_{m}X
for every dcpo
X
, and every

x\in X
, whence the following.

Proposition 5.6
.

The triple
(\mathbf{V}_{m},\eta,\_^{\dagger})
is a monad on the category
of dcpos and Scott-continuous maps.

5.2
Tensorial strengths

A tensorial strength for a monad
(T,\eta,\_^{\dagger})
is a
collection
t
of morphisms

t_{X,Y}\colon X\times TY\to T(X\times Y)
, natural in
X
and

Y
, satisfying certain coherence conditions (which we omit, see

[
Moggi, 1991
]
.) We then say that
(T,\eta,\_^{\dagger},t)
is a

strong
monad. We will satisfy ourselves with the following
result. By
[
Moggi, 1991
, Proposition 3.4]
, in a category with finite
products and enough points, if one can find morphisms

t_{X,Y}\colon X\times TY\to T(X\times Y)
for all objects
X

and
Y
such that

t_{X,Y}\circ\langle x,\nu\rangle=T(\langle x\circ!,\mathrm{id}_{Y}\rangle)\circ\nu
, then the collection of those morphisms is the
unique tensorial strength. A category with a terminal object
1
has

enough points
if and only if, for any two morphisms

f,g\colon X\to Y
,
f=g
if and only if for every

x\colon 1\to X
,
f\circ x=g\circ x
.

The category
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
of dcpos has finite products, and has enough
points. Specializing the above to
T\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathbf{V}
, the formula for

t_{X,Y}
reads: for every
x\in X
, for every
\nu\in\mathbf{V}Y
, for
every
W\in{\mathcal{O}}(X\times Y)
,

t_{X,Y}(x,\nu)(W)=\nu(\{y\in Y\mid(x,y)\in W\}
.
Rewriting this as

t_{X,Y}(x,\nu)(W)=\int_{y\in Y}\chi_{W}(x,y)d\nu
, hence

t_{X,Y}(x,\nu)=\mathbf{V}(\lambda y\in Y.(x,y))(\nu)
, we
retrieve formulae already given by Jones
[
Jones, 1990
, Section 4.3]
,
and which show immediately that the map
t_{X,Y}
is well-defined and
Scott-continuous.

It also follows from Lemma
5.5
that, if
\nu
is a
minimal valuation, namely an element of
\mathbf{V}_{m}X
, then

t_{X,Y}(x,\nu)
is an element of
\mathbf{V}_{m}(X\times Y)
. It
follows:

Proposition 5.7
.

(\mathbf{V},\eta,\_^{\dagger},t)
and

(\mathbf{V}_{m},\eta,\_^{\dagger},t)
are strong monads on
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
.

5.3
Minimal valuations form a commutative monad on
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
, or
Fubini-Tonelli again

We now show that
\mathbf{V}_{m}
is a
commutative
monad on
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
.
The corresponding result is unknown for
\mathbf{V}
. Equivalently, the
Fubini-Tonelli theorem holds for minimal valuations. In order to
prove it, we use the following simple lemma.

Lemma 5.8
.

Two morphisms
f,g\colon X\to Y
in
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
that coincide on

A\subseteq X
also coincide on
cl_{d}(A)
.

Proof.

Let
B\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{x\in X\mid f(x)=g(x)\}
. Since
f
and
g

preserve directed suprema,
B
is d-closed. By assumption,
A
is
included in
B
, so
B
also contains
cl_{d}(A)
.
∎

Theorem 5.9

(Fubini-Tonelli for minimal valuations on
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
)
.

Let
X
,
Y
be two dcpos,
f\in\mathcal{L}(X\times Y)
,
\mu\in\mathbf{V}X
, and
\nu\in\mathbf{V}Y
. If
\mu
or
\nu
is minimal, then:

\displaystyle\int_{x\in X}\left(\int_{y\in Y}f(x,y)d\nu\right)d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{y\in Y}\left(\int_{x\in X}f(x,y)d\mu\right)d\nu.

(3)

Proof.

When
\mu
is a simple valuation
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}
, it
is clear that the two sides of the equation coincide. Hence the two
Scott-continuous functions:

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\mapsto\int_{x\in X}\left(\int_{y\in Y}f(x,y)d\nu\right)d\mu

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\mapsto\int_{y\in Y}\left(\int_{x\in X}f(x,y)d\mu\right)d\nu

coincide on
\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{f}}X
. By Lemma
5.8
, they coincide
on
cl_{d}(\mathbf{V}_{\mathrm{f}}X)=\mathbf{V}_{m}X
. This finishes the case where

\mu
is minimal. The case where
\nu
is minimal is symmetric.
∎

Given a tensorial strength
t
for a monad
T
on a category with
finite products, there is a dual tensorial strength
t^{\prime}
, where

t^{\prime}_{X,Y}\colon TX\times Y\to T(X\times Y)
, obtained by swapping
the two arguments, applying
t_{X,Y}
, and then swapping back the
roles of
X
and
Y
. Here

t^{\prime}_{X,Y}(\mu,y)(W)=\int_{x\in X}\chi_{W}(x,y)d\mu
. We can
then define two morphisms from
TX\times TY
to
T(X\times Y)
,
namely
{t^{\prime}}^{\dagger}_{X,Y}\circ t_{TX,Y}
and

t^{\dagger}_{X,Y}\circ t^{\prime}_{X,TY}
. The monad
T
is

commutative
when they coincide. The connection between
commutative monads and Fubini-Tonelli-like theorems is made explicit
by Kock
[
Kock, 2012
]
. For completeness, we prove the
following explicitly.

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
X
,
Y
be two dcpos. The maps

t^{\dagger}_{X,Y}\circ t^{\prime}_{X,\mathbf{V}Y}
and

{t^{\prime}}^{\dagger}_{X,Y}\circ t_{\mathbf{V}X,Y}
coincide on those pairs

(\mu,\nu)\in\mathbf{V}X\times\mathbf{V}Y
such that

\mu\in\mathbf{V}_{m}X
or
\nu\in\mathbf{V}_{m}Y
.

Proof.

We have already seen that

t_{X,Y}(x,\nu)=\mathbf{V}(\lambda y\in Y.(x,y))(\nu)
, for all

x
and
\nu
. In other words,

t_{X,Y}(x,\nu)=(\lambda y\in Y.\delta_{(x,y)})^{\dagger}(\nu)
. Similarly,

t^{\prime}_{X,Z}(\mu,z)=(\lambda x\in X.\delta_{(x,z)})^{\dagger}(\mu)
.

For every
W\in{\mathcal{O}}(X\times Y)
,

\displaystyle t^{\dagger}_{X,Y}(t^{\prime}_{X,\mathbf{V}Y}(\mu,\nu))(W)

\displaystyle=\int_{(x^{\prime},\nu)\in X\times\mathbf{V}Y}t_{X,Y}(x^{\prime},\nu)(W)dt^{\prime}_{X,\mathbf{V}Y}(\mu,\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{(x^{\prime},\nu)\in X\times\mathbf{V}Y}t_{X,Y}(x^{\prime},\nu)(W)d(\lambda x\in X.\delta_{(x,\nu)})^{\dagger}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in X}\left(\int_{(x^{\prime},\nu)\in X\times\mathbf{V}Y}t_{X,Y}(x^{\prime},\nu)(W)d\delta_{(x,\nu)}\right)d\mu

by (
2
)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in X}t_{X,Y}(x,\nu)(W)d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in X}\left(\int_{y\in Y}\chi_{W}(x,y)d\nu\right)d\mu.

Symmetrically,

\displaystyle{t^{\prime}}^{\dagger}_{X,Y}(t_{\mathbf{V}X,Y}(\mu,\nu))(W)

\displaystyle=\int_{y\in Y}\left(\int_{x\in X}\chi_{W}(x,y)d\mu\right)d\nu.

The result then follows from Theorem
5.9
.
∎

Corollary 5.11
.

(\mathbf{V}_{m},\eta,\_^{\dagger},t)
is a commutative monad on
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
.

Remark 5.12
.

Strictly speaking, the Fubini-Tonelli theorem is more general, and
states the existence of a product measure. Here we obtain a minimal
product valuation, as follows. We write
\otimes
for the morphism

{t^{\prime}}^{\dagger}_{X,Y}\circ t_{\mathbf{V}X,Y}

(
=t^{\dagger}_{X,Y}\circ t^{\prime}_{X,\mathbf{V}Y}
) from

\mathbf{V}_{m}X\times\mathbf{V}_{m}Y
to
\mathbf{V}_{m}(X\times Y)
, as with any
commutative monad
[
Kock, 2012
, Section 5]
. Then, for all

\mu\in\mathbf{V}_{m}X
and
\nu\in\mathbf{V}_{m}Y
,
\otimes(\mu,\nu)
,
which we prefer to write as
\mu\otimes\nu
, is in

\mathbf{V}_{m}(X\times Y)
. Looking back at the computations we have
done during the proof of Proposition
5.10
,

\displaystyle(\mu\otimes\nu)(W)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in X}\left(\int_{y\in Y}\chi_{W}(x,y)d\nu\right)d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{y\in Y}\left(\int_{x\in X}\chi_{W}(x,y)d\mu\right)d\nu

for every
W\in{\mathcal{O}}(X\times Y)
. An easy computation, based on
(
2
), yields that, for every

f\in\mathcal{L}(X\times Y)
,

\int_{(x,y)\in X\times Y}f(x,y)d(\mu\otimes\nu)
is equal
to any of the double integrals of (
3
).

As an additional benefit of the categorical approach, we obtain that
the map
\otimes\colon(\mu,\nu)\mapsto\mu\otimes\nu
is
Scott-continuous from
\mathbf{V}_{m}X\times\mathbf{V}_{m}Y
to

\mathbf{V}_{m}(X\times Y)
.

6
Measures on
\mathbb{R}
, minimal valuations on
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}

All this is good, but aren’t we restricting continuous valuations too
much by only considering minimal valuations? And indeed, Lebesgue
measure is not minimal on
\mathbb{R}
, as we will soon see. But it

is
minimal on
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
and on
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
. In fact, we will see
that
every
measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
is represented by a minimal
valuation on
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
and on
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
.

To be more precise, since
\mathbb{R}
is second-countable hence
hereditarily Lindelöf, every measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
restricts to a
continuous valuation on
\mathbb{R}
. Its image valuation
i[\mu]
by the
embedding
i\colon x\mapsto[x,x]
of
\mathbb{R}
into
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
(or

\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
) is then a continuous valuation on
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
(resp.,

\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
). While the former is rarely minimal, we will see that the
latter always is.

Let us call
valuation
on
X
any strict, modular, monotonic map
from
{\mathcal{O}}X
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
; namely, we forgo the continuity
requirement. A valuation
\nu
on
X
is
point-continuous
if
and only if for every open subset
U
of
X
, for every
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}

such that
r<\nu(U)
, there is a finite subset
A
of
U
such
that, for every open neighborhood
V
of
A
,
\nu(V)>r
. The
notion is due to Heckmann
[
Heckmann, 1996
]
. His main achievement was
to show that the space
\mathbf{V}_{p}X
of point-continuous valuations over

X
, with the so-called weak topology, is a sobrification of the space
of simple valuations, also with the weak topology. This can be used
to show that there is also a commutative monad

(\mathbf{V}_{p},\eta,\_^{\dagger},t)
on
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
, but we will not show this
here. We will use the following results by Heckmann: every
point-continuous valuation is continuous; every simple valuation is
point-continuous;
\mathbf{V}_{p}X
is sober, in particular it is a dcpo
under the pointwise ordering; in particular, every minimal valuation
is point-continuous.

Let
\lambda
be the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
, or ambiguously, its
restriction to the open subsets of
\mathbb{R}
, in which case we call it
the
Lebesgue valuation
.

Lemma 6.1
.

The Lebesgue valuation
\lambda
on
\mathbb{R}
is not point-continuous,
hence not minimal.

Proof.

Let
U
be any non-empty open subset of
\mathbb{R}
. Then

\lambda(U)>0
. Let us pick any
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

r<\lambda(U)
. For every finite subset
A
of
U
, say of
cardinality
n
, the open set

V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcup_{x\in A}]x-\epsilon,x+\epsilon[
, where

\epsilon>0
is chosen so that
2n\epsilon<r
, is an open
neighborhood of
A
but

\lambda(V)\leq\sum_{x\in A}\lambda(]x-\epsilon,x+\epsilon[)=2n\epsilon<r
.
∎

Proposition 6.2
.

For every measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
, and writing again
\mu
for the
valuation it induces by restriction to
{\mathcal{O}}\mathbb{R}
, the image
valuation
i[\mu]
is a minimal valuation on
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
(resp.,

\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
).

Proof.

\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
(resp.,
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
) is a continuous dcpo. Hence
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R})

(resp.,
\mathbf{V}(\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot})
) is also a continuous dcpo, with a basis
of simple valuations
[
Gierz et al., 2003
, Theorem IV-9.16]
. In
particular,
i[\mu]
is a directed supremum of simple valuations,
hence a minimal valuation.
∎

Remark 6.3
.

The proof of Proposition
6.2
shows, more generally,
that, given any hereditarily Lindelöf space
X
with a topological
embedding
i
into some continuous dcpo
P
, for every Borel measure

\mu
on
X
,
i[\mu]
is a minimal valuation on
P
. Lawson
showed that every Polish space
X
, not just
\mathbb{R}
, has this
property
[
Lawson, 1997
]
.

\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
are even
\omega
-continuous, namely, they have a
countable basis. One can then show that
i[\mu]
is the supremum of
a countable chain of simple valuations (Exercise IV-9.29 of

[
Gierz et al., 2003
]
helps). It is interesting to see that we can obtain
such an explicit countable chain by elementary means. The following
construction, which has some common points with the so-called
Riemann-Stieltjes integral, is uniform, and only requires the
knowledge of
\mu(]a,b])
for intervals
]a,b]
with rational or
dyadic endpoints.

Definition 6.4
.

A
partition

P
(of
\mathbb{R}
) is a finite non-empty subset of

\mathbb{R}
. We write
P
as
\{a_{1}<\cdots<a_{n}\}
in order to make
both the elements and their order manifest, with
n\geq 1
. Every
such partition induces a finite subset
E_{P}
of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
defined by

E_{P}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{[a_{1},a_{2}],\cdots,[a_{n-1},a_{n}]\}
. If
n=1
, then

E_{P}
is empty.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the set of all partitions of
\mathbb{R}
. A
partition
Q

refines

P
if and only if
P\subseteq Q
.

Given any measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
, and any partition

P\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{a_{1}<\cdots<a_{n}\}
of
\mathbb{R}
, we let
\mu_{P}
be the
simple valuation

\sum_{i=2}^{n}\mu(]a_{i-1},a_{i}]).\delta_{[a_{i-1},a_{i}]}
on

\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
(resp.,
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
).

Remark 6.5
.

The coefficient of
\delta_{[a_{i-1},a_{i}]}
in
\mu_{P}
is

\mu(]a_{i-1},a_{i}])
, not
\mu([a_{i-1},a_{i}])
. The point is
that
\mu(]a_{i-1},a_{i}])+\mu(]a_{i},a_{i+1}])=\mu(]a_{i-1},a_{i+1}])
. Choosing the coefficient to be
\mu([a_{i-1},a_{i}[)

would work equally well.

Remark 6.6
.

If
\mu
is a probability valuation on
\mathbb{R}
given by its
cumulative distribution function
F
(namely,

F(t)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mu(]-\infty,t])
), then
\mu_{P}
is equal to

\sum_{i=2}^{n}(F(a_{i})-F(a_{i-1})).\delta_{[a_{i-1},a_{i}]}
,
and represents the process of picking the interval
[a_{i-1},a_{i}]

with probability
F(a_{i})-F(a_{i-1})
.

Theorem 6.7
.

Let
\mu
be any measure on
\mathbb{R}
. Then:

1.

for all partitions
P
and
Q
of
\mathbb{R}
, if
P\subseteq Q

then
\mu_{P}\leq\mu_{Q}
;

2.

i[\mu]
is the directed supremum of
{(\mu_{P})}_{P\in\mathcal{P}}
, and is therefore a minimal valuation;

3.

for every dense subset
D
of
\mathbb{R}
,
i[\mu]
is also the
directed supremum of
{(\mu_{P})}_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{D}}
, where

\mathcal{P}_{D}
is the collection of partitions
P\subseteq D
.

Proof.

1. Since
Q
refines
P
,
Q
is obtained by adding some points to

P
, and it suffices to show the claim when we add just one point.
Let us write
P
as
\{a_{1}<\cdots<a_{n}\}
, and let

Q\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}P\cup\{a\}
, where
a\not\in P
. If
a<a_{1}
, then

\mu_{Q}
is equal to

\mu_{P}+\mu(]a,a_{1}]).\delta_{[a,a_{1}]}\geq\mu_{P}
; similarly
if
a>a_{n}
. Otherwise, let
j
be the unique index such that

a_{j-1}<a<a_{j}
,
2\leq j\leq n
. Then:

\displaystyle\mu_{P}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=2\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{n}\mu(]a_{i-1},a_{i}]).\delta_{[a_{i-1},a_{i}]}+\mu(]a_{j-1},a_{j}]).\delta_{[a_{j-1},a_{j}]}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=2\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{n}\mu(]a_{i-1},a_{i}]).\delta_{[a_{i-1},a_{i}]}+\mu(]a_{j-1},a]).\delta_{[a_{j-1},a_{j}]}+\mu(]a,a_{j}]).\delta_{[a_{j-1},a_{j}]}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=2\\
i\neq j\end{subarray}}^{n}\mu(]a_{i-1},a_{i}]).\delta_{[a_{i-1},a_{i}]}+\mu(]a_{j-1},a]).\delta_{[a_{j-1},a]}+\mu(]a,a_{j}]).\delta_{[a,a_{j}]}=\mu_{Q},

where the latter inequality is justified by the fact that

[a_{j-1},a]
and
[a,a_{j}]
are both larger than or equal to

[a_{j-1},a_{j}]
, and that
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\delta_{\mathbf{x}}
is
monotonic.

Item 2 is a special case of item 3, with
D\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathbb{R}
. Hence we
prove item 3 directly. Let
D
be any dense subset of
\mathbb{R}
.

The family
\mathcal{P}_{D}
is directed under inclusion, since

\{d\}\in\mathcal{P}
for any given
d\in D
, and since for all

P,Q\in\mathcal{P}_{D}
,
P\cup Q
is in
\mathcal{P}_{D}
. By
item 1,
{(\mu_{P})}_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{D}}
is therefore directed.

Let us fix an arbitrary Scott-open subset
\mathcal{U}
of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}

(resp.,
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
), and let
U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}i^{-1}(\mathcal{U})
.

For every
P\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{a_{1}<\cdots<a_{n}\}\in\mathcal{P}_{D}
,

\mu_{P}(\mathcal{U})\leq i[\mu](\mathcal{U})
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\mu_{P}(\mathcal{U})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}2\leq i\leq n\\
[a_{i-1},a_{i}]\in\mathcal{U}\end{subarray}}\mu(]a_{i-1},a_{i}])

\displaystyle=\mu\left(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}2\leq i\leq n\\
[a_{i-1},a_{i}]\in\mathcal{U}\end{subarray}}]a_{i-1},a_{i}]\right)

\displaystyle\text{since the sets }]a_{i-1},a_{i}]\text{ are pairwise
disjoint}

\displaystyle\leq\mu(U)=i[\mu](\mathcal{U}).

The last inequality is justified by the fact that for every
i
with

2\leq i\leq n
such that
[a_{i-1},a_{i}]\in\mathcal{U}
,

[a_{i-1},a_{i}]
is included in
U
: for every

x\in[a_{i-1},a_{i}]
,
[a_{i-1},a_{i}]
is below
[x,x]
in
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}

(resp.,
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
), so
[x,x]\in\mathcal{U}
, meaning that

x\in U
.

In order to show that

\sup_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{D}}\mu_{P}(\mathcal{U})\geq i[\mu](\mathcal{U})
, we consider any
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that

r<i[\mu](\mathcal{U})=\mu(U)
, and we will show that there is
a partition
P\in\mathcal{P}_{D}
such that
\mu_{P}(\mathcal{U})>r
.
For every
x\in U
,
[x,x]
is in
\mathcal{U}
and is the supremum
of the chain of elements
[x-\epsilon,x+\epsilon]
,
\epsilon>0
.
Therefore there is a positive real number
\epsilon_{x}
such that

[x-\epsilon_{x},x+\epsilon_{x}]
is in
\mathcal{U}
. Since
D
is
dense in
\mathbb{R}
, we can find a subinterval
[a_{x},b_{x}]
such that

a_{x}
and
b_{x}
are in
D
, and
a_{x}<x<b_{x}
. We note that

[a_{x},b_{x}]
is also in
\mathcal{U}
. In particular,
[a_{x},b_{x}]
,
hence also
]a_{x},b_{x}[
, is included in
U
for every
x\in U
, so

U=\bigcup_{x\in U}]a_{x},b_{x}[
. We write the latter as the
directed union of the open sets

U_{E}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcup_{x\in E}]a_{x},b_{x}[
, where
E
ranges over the
finite subsets of
U
. Since
\mu
restricted to the open sets of

\mathbb{R}
is a continuous valuation, there is a finite subset
E
of

U
such that
r<\mu(U_{E})
.

We let
P
be the collection of elements
a_{x}
and
b_{x}
,
x\in E
.
This is an element of
\mathcal{P}_{D}
. Let us write
P
as

\{c_{1}<\cdots<c_{n}\}
. For every
x\in E
,
[a_{x},b_{x}]
is an
element of
\mathcal{U}
, and is equal to
[c_{i_{x}},c_{j_{x}}]
for
some indices
i_{x}
and
j_{x}
such that
1\leq i_{x}<j_{x}\leq n
.
Then
[c_{i-1},c_{i}]
is larger than or equal to
[a_{x},b_{x}]
for
every
i
with
i_{x}+1\leq i\leq j_{x}
, hence is also in

\mathcal{U}
. It follows that

\mu_{P}(\mathcal{U})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}2\leq i\leq n\\
{}[c_{i-1},c_{i}]\in\mathcal{U}\end{subarray}}\mu(]c_{i-1},c_{i}])\geq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in E\\
i_{x}+1\leq i\leq j_{x}\end{subarray}}\mu(]c_{i-1},c_{i}])
. The latter is equal to

\mu(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in E\\
i_{x}+1\leq i\leq j_{x}\end{subarray}}]c_{i-1},c_{i}])
, since the intervals
]c_{i-1},c_{i}]
are pairwise disjoint.
By definition of
i_{x}
and
j_{x}
, this is larger than or equal to

\mu(\bigcup_{x\in E}]a_{x},b_{x}])
, hence to

\mu(\bigcup_{x\in E}]a_{x},b_{x}[)=\mu(U_{E})
. Therefore

r<\mu_{P}(\mathcal{U})
, as desired.
∎

Corollary 6.8
.

Let
\mu
be any measure on
\mathbb{R}
. For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
the partition
P_{n}
consist of all integer multiples of
1/2^{n}

between
-n
and
n
. The minimal valuation
i[\mu]
is the
supremum of the countable chain of simple valuations
\mu_{P_{n}}
,

n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

The family
{(P_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a subfamily of
\mathcal{P}_{D}
,
where
D
is the set of dyadic numbers. It is in fact cofinal:
every element
P
of
\mathcal{P}_{D}
is refined by
P_{n}
for some

n\in\mathbb{N}
, namely for any
n
larger than every element of
P
,
every opposite of an element of
P
, and such that every element of

P
is an integer multiple of
1/2^{n}
. It follows that

{(\mu_{P})}_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{D}}
and
{(\mu_{P_{n}})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

have the same supremum, and that is
i[\mu]
by
Theorem
6.7
, item 3.
∎

7
The ISPCF Calculus

We now come to the description of our calculus. This is a variant of
SFPC, a statistical variant of Fiore and Plotkin’s Fixed Point
Calculus
[
Fiore and Plotkin, 1994
]
due to Vákár, Kammar, and Staton

[
Vákár et al., 2019
]
. It is also very close to the calculus PCFSS of

[
Dal Lago and Hoshino, 2019
]
and to PPCF
[
Ehrhard et al., 2018
]
. Just like the
latter, but contrarily to the other languages we have mentioned, our
calculus ISPCF (for
Interval
statistical PCF) is a call-by-name
language.

Its algebra of types is as follows. One may naturally consider
additional types, such as more complex, recursively defined data
types.

\displaystyle\sigma,\tau,\ldots

\displaystyle::=\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}\mid\boldsymbol{\mathtt{void}}\mid\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\mid\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\mid\sigma+\tau\mid\sigma\times\tau\mid\sigma\to\tau\mid D\tau

The types of the form
D\tau
are called
distribution types
.
The function arrow
\to
associates to the right, so that

\sigma\to\tau\to\lambda
abbreviates

\sigma\to(\tau\to\lambda)
. Compared to the algebra of types of
SFPC, first, we keep a type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
of real numbers—exact reals
instead of true reals, though.
Second, we do not require the presence of recursive types as SFPC, so
as to simplify the presentation. Accommodating such type
constructions is not central to our work, and can be handled through
bilimit constructions
[
Abramsky and Jung, 1994
, Section 5]
. (Showing
adequacy, as we will do in Section
10.2
, in the presence
of recursive types, is much more challenging, though. See

[
Jia et al., 2021
]
for adequacy in the presence of recursive types
and discrete probabilistic choice, and
[
Pitts, 1993
]
for a general
technique for proving adequacy in the presence of recursive types.)
Third, and finally, we choose to have an explicit type of
distributions former
D
, in the style of Moggi’s Simple Metalanguage

[
Moggi, 1991
]
: arrow types
\sigma\to\tau
in SFPC are typically
encoded as
\sigma\to D\tau
in ISPCF.

\begin{array}[]{cc}{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 16.89268pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=33.78537pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash x_{\sigma}\colon\sigma$}}}}&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 7.78532pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\underline{f}\in\Sigma$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=37.23729pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\underline{f}\colon typ(\underline{f})$}}}}\qquad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash M\colon\sigma\to\sigma$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=52.77509pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 1.73201pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}\;M\colon\sigma$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 41.77728pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=83.55457pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]\colon D\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}$}}}}&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 15.97295pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash M\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=76.0985pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}M\colon D\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 18.69827pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash M\colon\tau$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=72.55948pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\lambda x_{\sigma}.M\colon\sigma\to\tau$}}}}&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash M\colon\sigma\to\tau\quad\vdash N\colon\sigma$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=93.49364pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 24.60286pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash MN\colon\tau$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 9.01462pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash M\colon\tau$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=53.19217pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits M\colon D\tau$}}}}&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash M\colon D\sigma\quad\vdash N\colon D\tau$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=94.33797pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 2.82195pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\sigma}\leftarrow M};N\colon D\tau$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash M\colon\sigma\quad\vdash N\colon\tau$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=77.22408pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 8.88875pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\langle M,N\rangle\colon\sigma\times\tau$}}}}&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash M\colon\sigma\times\tau$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=53.0992pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 3.20363pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\pi_{1}M\colon\sigma$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\vdash M\colon\sigma\times\tau$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=53.0992pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 3.87492pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\pi_{2}M\colon\tau$}}}}\\
\\
{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 12.865pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\vdash M\colon\sigma\end{array}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=61.1247pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\iota_{1}M\colon\sigma+\tau$}}}}\quad{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 13.5363pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\begin{array}[]{c}\\
\vdash M\colon\tau\end{array}$}}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=61.1247pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\vdash\iota_{2}M\colon\sigma+\tau$}}}}&{\vbox{\hbox spread0.0pt{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\penalty\begin{array}[]{c}\vdash M\colon\sigma+\tau\\
\vdash N\colon\sigma\to\upsilon\quad\vdash P\colon\tau\to\upsilon\end{array}$}\hskip 0.0pt}\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule height=2.75002pt,depth=-2.24998pt,width=85.60312pt\hbox{}}}\hbox{\kern 13.15405pt\hbox{$\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathtt{case}}MNP\colon\upsilon$}}}}\end{array}

Figure 1:
The typing rules of ISPCF

Just like SFPC, ISPCF is a typed higher-order functional language.
Its terms are given by the following grammar:

\displaystyle M,N,P,\ldots

\displaystyle::=x_{\sigma}\mid\underline{f}\mid\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]\mid\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}M

\displaystyle\quad\mid\lambda x.M\mid MN\mid\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}\;M\mid\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits M\mid\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x\leftarrow M};N

\displaystyle\quad\mid\langle M,N\rangle\mid\pi_{1}M\mid\pi_{2}M\mid\iota_{1}M\mid\iota_{2}M\mid\boldsymbol{\mathtt{case}}MNP

where
x_{\sigma}
(and also
y_{\tau}
,
z_{\upsilon}
, etc.) range over
variables, whose type appears explicitly as a subscript (which we will
often omit for clarity),
\underline{f}
ranges over a fixed set

\Sigma
of constants, and
\mu
ranges over a fixed set
\mathcal{M}

of measures on
\mathbb{R}
. We assume a countably infinite supply of
variables of each type.

We assume a function
typ
from
\Sigma
to the set of ISPCF types:

typ(\underline{f})
is the type of
\underline{f}
, by definition.

Amongst those we require a constant
\underline{*}
of type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}
,
and constants
\underline{n}
, one for each integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, with

typ(\underline{n})=\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}
.

We may include constants
\underline{q}
, one for each rational number

q
, with
typ(\underline{q})=\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
. We may also include
constants for non-rational numbers, such as
\underline{\pi}
; and
standard numerical primitives such as
\underline{\sin}
,

\underline{\mathrm{exp}}
or
\underline{\log}
, with

typ(\underline{\sin})=typ(\underline{\mathrm{exp}})=typ(\underline{\log})=\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
, meant to implement the
sine, exponential and logarithm functions respectively, as in SFPC.

We will see later that we can define a type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}
of Booleans,
namely
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{void}}+\boldsymbol{\mathtt{void}}
, and two terms
\underline{\mathrm{true}}
and
\underline{\mathrm{false}}
of type

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}
. Given that, we may include a sign test

\underline{\text{pos}}\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}
, with the intention that
\underline{\text{pos}}\;M

evaluates to the value
\mathrm{true}
of
\underline{\mathrm{true}}
if
M
evaluates to a
positive real number, and to the value
\mathrm{false}
of
\underline{\mathrm{false}}
if
M

evaluates to a negative real number (we will discuss semantics soon).

The constructs
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits
and
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits
are standard monadic constructs;

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}
is a fixed-point combinator, and we assume the usual

\alpha
-renaming conventions. Crucially, we keep the two iconic
primitives of SFPC:
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}
(or rather,
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]
) and

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}
. The typing rules are given in Figure
1
.

We also write
M;N
for

\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x\leftarrow M};N
, where
x
is a fresh variable—one that
is not free in
N
.

While SFPC is call-by-value, ISPCF is a call-by-name language, just
like Hakaru
[
Shan and Ramsey, 2017
]
or PPCF
[
Ehrhard et al., 2018
]
. (Hakaru,
however, lacks higher-order functions and recursion.) This slightly
simplifies the denotational semantics, and does not preclude us from
using a call-by-value style of programming if we so desire. Namely,
call-by-value application
M@N
of
M\colon\sigma\to D\tau
to

N\colon D\sigma
is defined as
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x\leftarrow N};Mx
, where

x
is a fresh variable. We will return to this point in
Section
9.1
.

8
The Denotational Semantics of ISPCF

The semantics
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
of types
\tau
is given by induction on
\tau
:

\begin{array}[]{c}\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}}\right\rrbracket\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{*,\bot\}\quad\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{void}}}\right\rrbracket\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\bot\}\quad\quad\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}}\right\rrbracket\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathbb{Z}_{\bot}\quad\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}\\
\left\llbracket{\sigma\to\tau}\right\rrbracket\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[\left\llbracket{\sigma}\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket]\quad\left\llbracket{D\tau}\right\rrbracket\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathbf{V}_{m}(\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket)\\
\left\llbracket{\sigma\times\tau}\right\rrbracket\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\left\llbracket{\sigma}\right\rrbracket\times\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket\quad\left\llbracket{\sigma+\tau}\right\rrbracket\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(\left\llbracket{\sigma}\right\rrbracket+\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket)_{\bot}.\end{array}

In
\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}}\right\rrbracket
, we have
\bot<*
.
\mathbb{Z}_{\bot}
is
\mathbb{Z}
plus a
fresh element
\bot
, ordered so that
\bot
is least and all integers
are pairwise incomparable.
[X\to Y]
denotes the dcpo of
Scott-continuous maps from the dcpo
X
to the dcpo
Y
, ordered
pointwise. Given any two pointed dcpos
X
and
Y
,
X\times Y
is
their ordinary product, ordered pointwise.
X+Y
is their
coproduct, and consists of elements
(1,x)
with
x\in X
,
(2,y)

with
y\in Y
, so that
(1,x)\leq(1,x^{\prime})
if and only if

x\leq x^{\prime}
,
(2,y)\leq(2,y^{\prime})
if and only if
y\leq y^{\prime}
, and

(1,x)
and
(2,y)
are incomparable.

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{x_{\sigma}}\right\rrbracket\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\rho(x_{\sigma})

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\underline{f}}\right\rrbracket\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}f

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]}\right\rrbracket\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}i[\mu]

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}M}\right\rrbracket\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}|\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho|.\delta_{*}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\lambda x_{\sigma}.M}\right\rrbracket\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda V\in\left\llbracket{\sigma}\right\rrbracket.\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(\rho[x_{\sigma}\mapsto V])\mskip-180.0mu

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{MN}\right\rrbracket\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho(\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho)\mskip-20.0mu

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}\;M}\right\rrbracket\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathop{\text{lfp}}(\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits M}\right\rrbracket\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\delta_{\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\sigma}\leftarrow M};N}\right\rrbracket\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(\left\llbracket{\lambda x_{\sigma}.N}\right\rrbracket\rho)^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\langle M,N\rangle}\right\rrbracket\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho,\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\pi_{i}M}\right\rrbracket\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda(V_{1},V_{2}).V_{i}

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{case}}MNP}\right\rrbracket\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho(a)&\text{if }\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho=(1,a)\\
\left\llbracket{P}\right\rrbracket\rho(b)&\text{if }\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho=(2,b)\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\mskip-80.0mu

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\iota_{i}M}\right\rrbracket\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(i,\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho)

Figure 2:
The semantics of ISPCF

The denotational semantics
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho
of each term
M
, in the
environment
\rho
, is given in Figure
2
. Environments
map variables
x_{\sigma}
to elements of the corresponding dcpo

\left\llbracket{\sigma}\right\rrbracket
. The notation
\rho[x\mapsto V]
stands for the
environment that maps
x
to
V
and every other variable
y
to

\rho(y)
. The least fixed point operator
\mathop{\text{lfp}}
on every pointed
dcpo
X
is such that

\mathop{\text{lfp}}(f)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f^{n}(\bot)
for every

f\in[X\to X]
. In the denotation of
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{case}}MNP
,

\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho(a)
is the application of
\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho
, which is a
function, to the value
a
.

Let us make a few comments. We assume an element
f\in\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket

for each constant
\underline{f}\in\Sigma
of type
\tau
. Hence,
for example, we have elements

\pi\in\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket
,

\sin,\mathrm{exp},\log\in\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket
, etc.
The case of sign tests
\underline{\text{pos}}
is interesting: by an easy argument
based on the fact that the real line is connected, there is no
Scott-continuous function from
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
to
\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}}\right\rrbracket
, where

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}+\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}
, which would map the elements
[a,a]

with
a\geq 0
to
\mathrm{true}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(1,*)
, and those with
a<0
to
