\mathrm{false}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(2,*)
(see
[
Edalat and Escardó, 2000
, Proposition 2.4]
for a
generalization of this observation). And indeed, such tests are not
computable. One reasonable choice for the semantics of
\underline{\text{pos}}
, if
we decide to include it, is to define
\text{pos}([a,b])
as
\mathrm{true}

if
a>0
,
\mathrm{false}
if
b<0
, and
\bot
otherwise.

The semantics of
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]
, namely
i[\mu]
, is the minimal
valuation associated with the measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
, see
Theorem
6.7
.

For every
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}^{\star}
, the notation
|\mathbf{a}|
denotes

0
if
\mathbf{a}=\bot
or if
\mathbf{a}=[a,b]
with

a\leq 0\leq b
,
a
if
\mathbf{a}=[a,b]
with
a\geq 0
, and

-b
if
\mathbf{a}=[a,b]
with
b\leq 0
.

This, as well as the semantics of
\underline{\text{pos}}
, is an instance of a more
general, well-known domain-theoretic construction. A
bc-domain

is a continuous dcpo in which every subset with an upper bound has a
least upper bound. The bc-domains with their Scott topology are
exactly the
densely injective

T_{0}
topological spaces, namely
the
T_{0}
spaces
Z
such that, for every dense subset
X
of any
space
Y
, every continuous map
f\colon X\to Z
extends to a
continuous map from the whole of
Y
to
Z

[
Gierz et al., 2003
, Proposition II-3.11]
. There is even a pointwise
largest such extension
f^{*}
, defined by

f^{*}(y)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sup_{U\in{\mathcal{O}}Y,y\in U}\inf_{x\in U\cap X}f(x)

[
Gierz et al., 2003
, Proposition II-3.9]
. The latter formula makes
sense and defines a continuous map
f^{*}
even when
f
is not
continuous, but in that case
f^{*}
will fail to extend
f
.

One can check that
\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}}\right\rrbracket
,
\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{void}}}\right\rrbracket
,

\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}}\right\rrbracket
,
\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket
, and in fact
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
for any
type
\tau
that does not contain the
D
operator, are bc-domains.
In particular, every continuous map
g\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
induces
a continuous map

\hat{g}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(i\circ g)^{*}\colon\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}\to\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
that
extends
i\circ g
, namely:
\hat{g}([x,x])=[g(x),g(x)]
for
every
x\in\mathbb{R}
. In doing so, we silently equate
\mathbb{R}
with a
subspace of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
through
i
, and it is easy to realize that

\mathbb{R}
is indeed dense in
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
.

This applies to
\sin
,
\cos
,
\exp
, notably. The case of
\log
is
slightly different, since it is only defined on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus\{0\}
.
In that case, the map
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
that sends every
positive real number
x
to
i(\log x)=[\log x,\log x]
, and all
other numbers to
\bot
, is continuous, and it is natural to define

\log\colon\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}\to\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
as its largest continuous
extension. The notation
|\mathbf{a}|
, as used in the semantics of

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;M
(where
\mathbf{a}
is
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho
), is equal to
f^{*}

where
f
is the absolute value map from
\mathbb{R}
to
\mathbb{R}
, and
therefore extends it to
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
. The semantics
pos
of
\underline{\text{pos}}

is
f^{*}
, where
f
is the non-continuous map that sends every
positive number to
\mathrm{true}
and all other numbers to
\mathrm{false}
.
Hence
f^{*}
does not extend
f
, but we still have

f^{*}([a,a])=f(a)
if
a\neq 0
.

It is clear that for each derivable judgment
\vdash M\colon\tau
,
for every environment
\rho
,
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho
is a well-defined element
of
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
. In particular, the least fixed point

\mathop{\text{lfp}}(\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho)
is well-defined in the definition of

\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}\;M}\right\rrbracket\rho
, because
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
is a pointed dcpo; this
is shown by induction on
\tau
, and the least element of

\left\llbracket{D\tau}\right\rrbracket
is the constant zero valuation. We also mention the
following case, which shows where tensorial strengths are
required. There is a morphism:

\textstyle{TX\times[X\to TY]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}_{X,Y}}
\textstyle{T(X\times[X\to TY])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{App}^{\dagger}}
\textstyle{TY}

where
X\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\left\llbracket{\sigma}\right\rrbracket
,
Y\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
,
T
is the

\mathbf{V}_{m}
monad,
t^{\prime}
is its dual tensorial strength, and
\mathrm{App}
is
application. Applying this to

(\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho,\left\llbracket{\lambda x_{\sigma}.N}\right\rrbracket\rho)
yields the
semantic value
\left\llbracket{\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\sigma}\leftarrow M};N}\right\rrbracket\rho
.

Remark 8.1
.

By standard category-theoretic arguments, since
\mathbf{V}_{m}
is
commutative, the following equation holds when
x
is not free in

N
and
y
is not free in
M
:

\mskip-40.0mu\left\llbracket{\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x\leftarrow M};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{y\leftarrow N};P}\right\rrbracket\rho=\left\llbracket{\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{y\leftarrow N};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x\leftarrow M};P}\right\rrbracket\rho.

It was already argued
[
Vákár et al., 2019
, Comments after Corollary 6.11]

that this semantic equation is crucial in program transformation
techniques such as disintegration-based Bayesian inference

[
Shan and Ramsey, 2017
]
as implemented in the Hakaru system

[
Narayanan et al., 2016
]
. As in
[
Vákár et al., 2019
]
, this equation is valid
at all types in our semantics.

9
Examples

9.1
The call-by-value versus call-by-name question

A recurring paradox in the theory of higher-order probabilistic
programming languages is the following: what should the value of

(\lambda x.x=x)(\mathtt{rand}())
be, where
\mathtt{rand}()

returns a random number? Since
x=x
is universally true, one expects
this program to always return true. In call-by-name, one also expects

\beta
-reduction to be a sound reduction rule, so that this value
should also be equal to
\mathtt{rand}()=\mathtt{rand}()
.
Unfortunately, the latter draws
two
independent random numbers,
and may return false.

This problem is avoided in
call-by-value
languages, which force

\mathtt{rand}()
to be evaluated first, and only once.

This need not be a problem in call-by-name languages such as ISPCF.
This is well-known, but we would like this point to be clear. In the
call-by-name language PPCF, the issue is solved by using a

\mathtt{let}(x,M,N)
construct that evaluates
M
, binds the
resulting value to
x
and then evaluates
N

[
Ehrhard et al., 2018
, Example 3.10]
. In ISPCF,
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits
may serve a similar
purpose, as we now demonstrate.

Let us build a simple uniform random number generator

\mathtt{randbool}
on
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}
. We recall that

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}+\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}
. We define
\underline{\mathrm{true}}
as

\iota_{1}\underline{*}
; its semantics is
\mathrm{true}=(1,*)
; and

\underline{\mathrm{false}}
as
\iota_{2}\underline{*}
, whose semantics is

\mathrm{false}=(2,*)
. If
M
has type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}
, we also define

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{if}}MNP
as an abbreviation for

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{case}}M(\lambda x_{1}.\allowbreak N)(\lambda x_{2}.P)
, where

x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are fresh variables.

In order to implement
\mathtt{randbool}
, we assume we have a term

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]
, where
\lambda_{1}
is the uniform measure on

[0,1]
, namely: for every measurable subset
E
of
\mathbb{R}
,

\lambda_{1}(E)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda(E\cap[0,1])
. We also assume the

\underline{\text{pos}}
primitive, and subtraction
-
. We define

\mathtt{randbool}
as follows.

\displaystyle\mathtt{randbool}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits(\underline{\text{pos}}(x-\underline{0.5}))

For every environment
\rho
,
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{randbool}}\right\rrbracket\rho
is the
continuous valuation

\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mathrm{true}}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mathrm{false}}
.

Let

\mathtt{eqbool}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda x.\lambda y.\boldsymbol{\mathtt{if}}\;x\;{(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{if}}\;y\;\underline{\mathrm{true}}\;\underline{\mathrm{false}})}\;{(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{if}}\;y\;\underline{\mathrm{false}}\;\underline{\mathrm{true}})}
, and we
abbreviate
\mathtt{eqbool}\;MN
as
M=N
, for all

M,N\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}
. One cannot write

(\lambda x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}}.x=x)(\mathtt{randbool})
directly, because

\mathtt{randbool}
has type
D\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}
, not
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}
. Sampling from
a distribution must be done through
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits
, and we have (at least)
two choices. The term:

\displaystyle\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{b_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}}\leftarrow\mathtt{randbool}};\;{\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits((\lambda x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}}.x=x)b)}

first samples, obtaining a random Boolean value
b
, which is then
passed to
\lambda x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}}.x=x
. This term has semantics

\delta_{\mathrm{true}}
; in other words, it is a probabilistic process that
returns
\mathrm{true}
with probability one. Or we may defer sampling
as follows:

\displaystyle(\lambda x_{D\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}}.\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{b_{1}\leftarrow x};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{b_{2}\leftarrow x};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits(b_{1}=b_{2}))\mathtt{randbool}.

The semantics of that term is
\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mathrm{true}}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\mathrm{false}}
: the probability that the two independent Boolean
values
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
are equal is only
1/2
.

9.2
Rejection sampling

Let us imagine that you have a probability measure
\mu
on a space

X
, let
A
be a measurable subset of
X
such that
\mu(A)\neq 0
.
Then there is a conditional probability measure
\mu_{A}
, defined by

\mu_{A}(E)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mu(E)/\mu(A)
for every measurable subset
E

of
A
. Rejection sampling allows one to draw an element at random in

A
with probability
\mu_{A}
, by drawing an element in
X
with
probability
\mu
until we find one in
A
. This terminates with
probability
1
, and in fact in
1/\mu(A)
trials on average.

Let
\tau
be any type. We consider the following ISPCF term:

\displaystyle\mathtt{rej}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda p_{D\tau}.\lambda sel_{\tau\to D\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}}.

\displaystyle\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}(\lambda r_{D\tau}.\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\tau}\leftarrow p};

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{b_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}}\leftarrow sel\;x};

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{if}}\;{b}\;{(\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits x)}\;{r}).

Intuitively,
\mathtt{rej}
takes a distribution
p
as input, a
selection function
sel
, and draws
x
at random with probability
p

until
sel\;x
returns
\mathrm{true}
. We allow
sel
to return an object
of type
D\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}
, not
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}
, in order to allow
sel
to do some
random computations as well.

For every environment
\rho
,
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rej}}\right\rrbracket\rho
is a
function that takes a minimal valuation
\mu
(bound to the variable

p
) and a selection function
s\in\left\llbracket{\tau\to D\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}}\right\rrbracket
(bound
to
sel
) to a minimal valuation
\nu_{|sel}
, which we now elucidate.
By definition,
\nu_{|sel}
is the least minimal valuation such that

\nu_{|sel}=\Phi(\nu_{|sel})
, where:

\displaystyle\Phi(\nu)

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket{\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x\leftarrow p};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{b\leftarrow sel\;x};\boldsymbol{\mathtt{if}}\;b\;{(\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits x)}\;r}\right\rrbracket\rho[p\mapsto\mu,sel\mapsto s,r\mapsto\nu]

For every selection function
s\in\left\llbracket{\tau\to D\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}}\right\rrbracket
, for
every
x\in\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
, we may write
s(x)
as

s_{\mathrm{true}}(x).\delta_{\mathrm{true}}+s_{\mathrm{false}}(x).\delta_{\mathrm{false}}+s_{(1,\bot)}(x).\delta_{(1,\bot)}+s_{(2,\bot)}.\delta_{(2,\bot)}+s_{\bot}(x).\delta_{\bot}
. For
simplicity, let us assume that

s_{\bot}(x)=s_{(1,\bot)}(x)=s_{(2,\bot)}(x)=0
. In other words, the
probability of
s(x)
returning a non-terminating Boolean is zero.
Expanding the semantics shows that
\Phi(\nu)
is the continuous
valuation such that, for every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}{\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket}
,

\displaystyle\Phi(\nu)(U)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket}(s_{\mathrm{true}}(x).\chi_{U}(x)+s_{\mathrm{false}}(x).\nu(U))d\mu.

Given any space
X
, any continuous valuation
\nu
on
X
, and any
continuous map
g\colon X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, there is a continuous
valuation
g\cdot\nu
on
X
defined by

(g\cdot\nu)(U)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{x\in X}g(x)\chi_{U}(x)d\nu
, for
every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}X
. This formula characterizes
g\cdot\nu
as
the continuous valuation obtained from
\nu
by applying the

density function

g
; a notation that is sometimes used for

g\cdot\nu
is
gd\nu
.

This abbreviation allows us to rewrite
\Phi(\nu)(U)
as follows.
The value
(s_{\mathrm{false}}\cdot\mu)(\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket)
is the total mass

\int_{x\in\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket}s_{\mathrm{false}}(x)d\mu
of

s_{\mathrm{false}}\cdot\mu
on
\left\llbracket{\mu}\right\rrbracket
.

\displaystyle\Phi(\nu)

\displaystyle=s_{\mathrm{true}}\cdot\mu+(s_{\mathrm{false}}\cdot\mu)(\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket).\nu.

Let us solve the equation
\nu=\Phi(\nu)
for
\nu
. One may
compute the least solution as
\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\Phi^{n}(0)
, where

0
denotes the zero valuation, but it is easier to solve the equation
directly.

If
(s_{\mathrm{false}}\cdot\mu)(\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket)<1
, then there are at most
two solutions to the equation
\nu=\Phi(\nu)
. The smaller one is:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-(s_{\mathrm{false}}\cdot\mu)(\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket)}s_{\mathrm{true}}\cdot\mu,

and this is the value of
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rej}}\right\rrbracket\rho(\mu)(s)
. When

0<(s_{\mathrm{false}}\cdot\mu)(\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket)<1
, there is another
solution, and that is the continuous valuation that maps every
non-empty open subset of
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
to
+\infty
, the
largest

continuous valuation on
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
. (Yes, that is a minimal
valuation, since it is simply the directed supremum of all simple
valuations whatsoever.)

If
(s_{\mathrm{false}}\cdot\mu)(\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket)=1
, then we are typically
in the case of sampling with respect to a set of measure zero. The
sitation is more complicated in general, since

s_{\mathrm{false}}+s_{\mathrm{true}}
may not be equal to
1
: the condition

(s_{\mathrm{false}}\cdot\mu)(\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket)=1
imposes no constraint on

s_{\mathrm{true}}\cdot\mu
.

The general case where
(s_{\mathrm{false}}\cdot\mu)(\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket)\geq 1

is probably even less intuitive. Let

A\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(s_{\mathrm{false}}\cdot\mu)(\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket)
. The equation

\nu=\Phi(\nu)
reduces to
\nu=s_{\mathrm{true}}\cdot\mu+A.\nu
.
The largest continuous valuation is always a solution, but there is
smaller one. For every open subset
U
of
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
, the possible
values
x
for
\nu(U)
satisfy

x=(s_{\mathrm{true}}\cdot\mu)(U)+A.x
: if

(s_{\mathrm{true}}\cdot\mu)(U)=0
, then the least value for
x
is
0

(this is the only possible value if
A>1
; if
A=1
, every element
of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
is a possible value); otherwise, the only possible value
for
x
is
+\infty
.
This suggests that the least solution
\nu
to

\nu=\Phi(\nu)
is defined by:

\displaystyle\nu(U)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\text{if }(s_{\mathrm{true}}\cdot\mu)(U)=0\\
+\infty&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

This is just the continuous valuation

(+\infty).(s_{\mathrm{true}}\cdot\mu)
. It is a minimal valuation by
Lemma
5.4
. Hence the desired semantics

\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rej}}\right\rrbracket\rho
is
(+\infty).(s_{\mathrm{true}}\cdot\mu)
if

(s_{\mathrm{false}}\cdot\mu)(\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket)\geq 1
.

In the special case where
s
is deterministic, namely that

s_{\mathrm{true}}=\chi_{U}
and
s_{\mathrm{false}}=\chi_{V}
for two disjoint
open subsets
U
and
V
of
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
, then
s_{\mathrm{true}}\cdot\mu

maps every open set
W
to
\mu(U\cap W)
, and is therefore equal to
the restriction
\mu_{|U}
of
\mu
to
U
, as introduced by Heckmann

[
Heckmann, 1996
]
. In that case,
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rej}}\right\rrbracket\rho
is equal
to
\frac{1}{1-\mu(V)}\mu_{|U}
if
\mu(V)<1
, and to

(+\infty).\mu_{|U}
otherwise.

9.3
Using
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}

Using
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}
instead of recursion, we can implement rejection
sampling by the following ISPCF term:

\displaystyle\mathtt{rej}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda p_{D\tau}.\lambda sel_{\tau\to D\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}}.

\displaystyle\qquad\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\tau}\leftarrow p};

\displaystyle\qquad\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{b_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}}\leftarrow sel\;x};

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{if}}\;b\;\underline{1.0}\;\underline{0.0});

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\;x.

Let
\mu\in\left\llbracket{D\tau}\right\rrbracket
,
s\in\left\llbracket{\tau\to D\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}}\right\rrbracket
. We
again assume that
s(x)
as

s_{\mathrm{true}}(x).\delta_{\mathrm{true}}+s_{\mathrm{false}}(x).\delta_{\mathrm{false}}
for every
x\in\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
. Then, for every

W\in{\mathcal{O}}{\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket}
,

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rej}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket\rho(\mu)(s)(W)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in X}s_{\mathrm{true}}(x)\chi_{W}(x)d\mu,

so that
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rej}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket\rho(\mu)(s)
is simply

s_{\mathrm{true}}\cdot\mu
.

In other words,
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rej}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket\rho(\mu)(s)
computes the
valuation obtained from
\mu
by applying the density function

s_{\mathrm{true}}
. When
s_{\mathrm{true}}=\chi_{U}
, this is
\mu_{|U}
.
Hence we retrieve the same result as for
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rej}}\right\rrbracket\rho
,
up to a renormalization factor, which may be infinite.

It is practical to abbreviate the form

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{if}}\;b\;\underline{1.0}\;\underline{0.0})
, where

b\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}
, as
\mathtt{observe}\;b
. This is a familiar
operation, implemented in Anglican
[
Wood et al., 2014
]
, as
a derived operator in Hakaru
[
Shan and Ramsey, 2017
]
, or
as the third argument of
\mathtt{lex\text{-}query}

clauses in Church
[
Goodman et al., 2008
]
.

9.4
Generating normal distributions

Let us imagine that we do not have a term
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mathcal{N}(0,1)]
for the normal (Gaussian) distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
with
mean
0
and standard deviation
1
. We will give several different
possible implementations in ISPCF of well-known algorithms.

For every measure map
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
\mathcal{N}(0,1)

is such that:

\displaystyle\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}}f(x)d\mathcal{N}(0,1)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}}f(x)\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}}dx.

This is a measure defined from Lebesgue measure
\lambda
on
\mathbb{R}

by applying the density map

x\mapsto\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}}
. As such, we
can implement it as follows, assuming a term
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda]
, as
well as primitives for multiplication, division, exponential
\underline{\text{exp}}
,
square root
\underline{\text{sqrt}}
, and various numerical constants, all with their
intended semantics. We agree that product is written with an infix

\times
, and we remember that
M;N
abbreviates
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x\leftarrow M};N
, where
x
is a fresh variable.

\displaystyle\mathtt{normal}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda]};

\displaystyle\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}(\underline{\text{sqrt}}(\underline{0.5}/\underline{\pi})\times\underline{\text{exp}}(\underline{-0.5}\times x\times x));\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits x.

This is the original purpose of
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}
: to define a distribution
from another one through a density function. We check that this gives
us the intended result. For every environment
\rho
, for every open
subset
W
of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
,

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\mathtt{normal}}\right\rrbracket\rho(W)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}}|\overline{g}(\mathbf{x})|.\delta_{\mathbf{x}}(W)di[\lambda]

where

\overline{g}(\mathbf{x})\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\left\llbracket{\underline{\text{sqrt}}(\underline{0.5}/\underline{\pi})\times\underline{\text{exp}}(\underline{-0.5}\times x\times x)}\right\rrbracket\rho[x\mapsto\mathbf{x}]
; we check that

\overline{g}([x,x])=[g(x),g(x)]
where

g(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sqrt{\frac{1}{2\pi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}}
. By the
change-of-variables formula, and the fact that
\delta_{[x,x]}(W)=\chi_{i^{-1}(W)}(x)
,

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\mathtt{normal}}\right\rrbracket\rho(W)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}}g(x).\chi_{i^{-1}(W)}(x)d\lambda=\mathcal{N}(0,1)(i^{-1}(W)).

Therefore
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{normal}}\right\rrbracket\rho=i[\mathcal{N}(0,1)]
.

Another way of implementing
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
is the

Box-Muller algorithm

[
Box and Muller, 1958
]
, which produces pairs
of two independent normally distributed variables, from two
independent uniformly distributed variables in
[0,1]
.

In order to implement this in ISPCF, we assume a term

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]
, where
\lambda_{1}
is the uniform measure on

[0,1]
. We also assume we have primitives
\underline{\sin}
,

\underline{\cos}
,
\underline{\log}
, and
\underline{\text{sqrt}}
, as well as
addition, subtraction, opposite, and multiplication, with the expected
semantics. We consider the following ISPCF term of type

D(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\times\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}})
. We use the shorthand
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}\;{x=u}\;\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}\;{v}
for

(\lambda x.v)u
.

\displaystyle\mathtt{box\_muller}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]};

\displaystyle\qquad\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{y_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]};

\displaystyle\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}\;{c_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}=\underline{\cos}(\underline{2.0}\times\underline{\pi}\times y)}\;\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}\;{}

\displaystyle\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}\;{s_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}=\underline{\sin}(\underline{2.0}\times\underline{\pi}\times y)}\;\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}\;{}

\displaystyle\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}\;{m_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}=\underline{\text{sqrt}}(\underline{-2.0}\times\underline{\log}\;x)}\;\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}\;{}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits(\langle m\times c,m\times s\rangle).

This is a non-recursive definition, and as a consequence,

\left\llbracket{\mathtt{box\_muller}}\right\rrbracket\rho
is a probability distribution on

\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}\times\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
that is actually concentrated on the
subspace
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
. Explicitly, for every open subset
W

of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}\times\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
,

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\mathtt{box\_muller}}\right\rrbracket\rho(W)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}}\left(\int_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}}\delta_{f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}(W)di[\lambda_{1}]\right)di[\lambda_{1}],

where
f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is

\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}\;{c_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}=\underline{\cos}(\underline{2.0}\times\underline{\pi}\times y)}\;\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}\;{\cdots\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}\;\langle m\times c,m\times s\rangle}}\right\rrbracket\rho[x\mapsto\mathbf{x},y\mapsto\mathbf{y}]
. By the
change-of-variables formula, we have:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\mathtt{box\_muller}}\right\rrbracket\rho(W)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\delta_{f([x,x],[y,y])}(W)d\lambda_{1}\right)d\lambda_{1},

and
f([x,x],[y,y])=[g(x,y),g(x,y)]
, where

g(x,y)=(\sqrt{-2\log x}\cos(2\pi y),\allowbreak\sqrt{-2\log x}\sin(2\pi y))
. It follows that
f([x,x],[y,y])\in W
if and only if
g(x,y)\in i^{-1}(W)
, so that:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\mathtt{box\_muller}}\right\rrbracket\rho(W)

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\left(\int_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\chi_{i^{-1}(W)}(g(x,y))d\lambda_{1}\right)d\lambda_{1},

We now use the familiar argument subtending the Box-Muller algorithm,
which we recapitulate for completeness. For every measurable map

h\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
(the integrals are ordinary Lebesgue
integrals),

\displaystyle\int_{(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(s,t)d\mathcal{N}(0,1)\otimes\mathcal{N}(0,1)

\displaystyle=\int_{(s,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{2\pi}h(s,t)e^{-\frac{1}{2}(s^{2}+t^{2})}ds\;dt

\displaystyle=\int_{r\in\mathbb{R}_{+},\theta\in[0,2\pi]}\frac{1}{2\pi}h(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta)e^{-\frac{1}{2}r^{2}}r\;dr\;d\theta

using the change of variables
s=r\cos\theta
,

t=r\sin\theta

\displaystyle=\int_{x,y\in[0,1]}h(\sqrt{-2\log x}\cos(2\pi y),\sqrt{-2\log x}\sin(2\pi y))\;dx\;dy

\displaystyle\text{by letting $x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}r^{2}}$, $y\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\theta/(2\pi)$}.

By taking
h\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\chi_{i^{-1}(W)}
, it follows that

(\mathcal{N}(0,1)\otimes\mathcal{N}(0,1))(i^{-1}(W))=\left\llbracket{\mathtt{box\_muller}}\right\rrbracket\rho(W)
. Therefore
\mathtt{box\_muller}

implements the product
\mathcal{N}(0,1)\otimes\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
in the sense that:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\mathtt{box\_muller}}\right\rrbracket\rho

\displaystyle=i[\mathcal{N}(0,1)\otimes\mathcal{N}(0,1)].

Remark 9.1
.

The valuation
\lambda_{1}
has compact support
[0,1]
. Since only
continuous maps can be defined in ISPCF, it might seem intuitive
that any continuous valuation defined by ISPCF terms that only use

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]
as sampler should also have compact support,
enforcing a necessary limitation on the kind of distributions that
one can define. The example of the term
\mathtt{box\_muller}
shows
that this is not the case. We will see another example in
Section
9.5
.

A more efficient variant of this algorithm combines it with rejection
sampling, and is due to Marsaglia and Bray
[
Marsaglia and Bray, 1964
]
:

\displaystyle\mathtt{box\_muller^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{\langle x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}},y_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\rangle\leftarrow\mathtt{rej}\;(\mathtt{prod}_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}},\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\;\mathtt{L}\;\mathtt{L})\;\mathtt{discp}};

\displaystyle\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}\;{u_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}=x\times x+y\times y}\;\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}\;{}

\displaystyle\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}\;{m_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}=\underline{\text{sqrt}}(\underline{-2.0}\times\underline{\log}\;u/u)}\;\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}\;{}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\langle m\times x,m\times y\rangle,

where
\mathtt{prod}_{\sigma,\tau}
implements the product of two
minimal valuations on
\left\llbracket{\sigma}\right\rrbracket
and
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
,
\mathtt{L}

implements the uniform measure on
[-1,1]
(namely, half of the
restriction of Lebesgue measure to
[-1,1]
), and
\mathtt{discp}

tests whether a point lies on the unit disc:

\displaystyle\mathtt{prod}_{\sigma,\tau}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda p_{D\sigma}.\lambda q_{D\tau}.

\displaystyle\qquad\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\sigma}\leftarrow p};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{y_{\tau}\leftarrow q};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\langle x,y\rangle

\displaystyle\mathtt{L}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits(\underline{2.0}\times x-\underline{1.0})

\displaystyle\mathtt{discp}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda\langle x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}},y_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\rangle.\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits(\underline{\text{pos}}(\underline{1.0}-x\times x-y\times y).

We also use the convenient abbreviation

\lambda\langle x_{\sigma},y_{\tau}\rangle.M
for

\lambda p_{\sigma\times\tau}.\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}\;{x_{\sigma}=\pi_{1}p}\;\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}\;{}\allowbreak\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}\;{y_{\tau}=\pi_{2}p}\;\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}\;{M}
, where
p
is not free in

M
, and similarly for

\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{\langle x_{\sigma},y_{\tau}\rangle\leftarrow M};N
.

Remark 9.2
.

We could have defined
\mathtt{prod}_{\sigma,\tau}
as

\lambda p_{D\sigma}.\lambda q_{D\tau}.\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{y_{\tau}\leftarrow q};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\sigma}\leftarrow p};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\langle x,y\rangle
instead. Since the monad of minimal valuations is
commutative, this would not make any semantical difference.

We let the reader check that
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{box\_muller^{\prime}}}\right\rrbracket\rho
is
also equal to
i[\mathcal{N}(0,1),\mathcal{N}(0,1)]
. We only
give a sketch of an argument. The semantics of

\mathtt{rej}\;(\mathtt{prod}_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}},\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\;\mathtt{L}\;\mathtt{L})\;\mathtt{discp}

is
\frac{1}{1-i[\mu](\mathcal{V})}i[\mu]_{|\mathcal{U}}
, where:

•

\mu
is the uniform measure on the square

[-1,1]\times[-1,1]
;

•

\mathcal{U}
is the Scott-open set of pairs

([a,b],[c,d])\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

[x,y]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[a,b]\times[a,b]+[c,d]\times[c,d]
satisfies

y<1
; in particular,
U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}i^{-1}(\mathcal{U})
is the open
unit disc
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid x^{2}+y^{2}<1\}
;

•

\mathcal{V}
is a similar Scott-open set, with the property that

V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}i^{-1}(\mathcal{V})
is the complement of the closed unit
disc, namely
V=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\mid x^{2}+y^{2}>1\}
.

Then
i[\mu](\mathcal{V})=\mu(V)=1-\pi/4=1-\mu(U)
, so
that the semantics of

\mathtt{rej}\;(\mathtt{prod}_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}},\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\;\mathtt{L}\;\mathtt{L})\;\mathtt{discp}

is
i[\mu^{\prime}]
, where
\mu^{\prime}
is the uniform measure on the (open) unit
disc. The rest of the argument follows similar lines as with the
original Box-Muller algorithm.

Here is a fourth and final implementation of
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Let

\mathtt{box\_muller^{\prime}}^{\prime}
be obtained from
\mathtt{box\_muller^{\prime}}
by
replacing
\mathtt{rej}
by
\mathtt{rej}^{\prime}
. The semantics of

\mathtt{rej}^{\prime}\;(\mathtt{prod}_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}},\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\;\mathtt{L}\;\mathtt{L})\;\mathtt{discp}

is equal to
1-i[\mu](\mathcal{V})=\pi/4
times the semantics of

\mathtt{rej}\;(\mathtt{prod}_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}},\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\;\mathtt{L}\;\mathtt{L})\;\mathtt{discp}
.
It follows that

\left\llbracket{\mathtt{box\_muller^{\prime}}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket\rho=\frac{\pi}{4}\;i[\mathcal{N}(0,1),\mathcal{N}(0,1)]=i\left[\frac{\pi}{4}\mathcal{N}(0,1),\frac{\pi}{4}\mathcal{N}(0,1)\right]
. This yields the same result as
before, up to a renormalization factor.

9.5
Generating Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}

We have noticed that one can define measures with unbounded support
from just
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]
, although
\lambda_{1}
has bounded
support (see Remark
9.1
). We now show that we can
define measures with unbounded support and whose total mass is
infinite as well. This requires the use of
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}
. What may be
intriguing is that
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}
multiplies the current ‘probability’ by a
real number different from
+\infty
. Still, this can be used to
increase the total mass from
1
to
+\infty
.

Hence, we imagine that the term
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]
is available,
where
\lambda_{1}
is Lebesgue measure on
[0,1]
, but that

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda]
is not, where
\lambda
is Lebesgue measure on

\mathbb{R}
. Assuming constants
\underline{\pi}
,
\underline{1.0}
,

\underline{2.0}
,
\underline{\tan}
,
\underline{\arctan}
, and some
constants for addition, subtraction, multiplication and division, we
define:

\displaystyle\mathtt{uniform}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda a_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}.\lambda b_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}.\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits{(a+(b-a)\times x)}

\displaystyle\mathtt{lebesgue}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{z_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\leftarrow\mathtt{uniform}(-\underline{\pi}/\underline{2.0})(\underline{\pi}/\underline{2.0})};

\displaystyle\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}(\underline{1.0}/(\underline{1.0}+z\times z));

\displaystyle\qquad\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits(\underline{\arctan}\;z).

We claim that
\mathtt{lebesgue}
fits the bill. Let

\upsilon_{[a,b]}
denote the uniform probability measure on the
interval
[a,b]
; up to the embedding
i\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
,
this is what
\mathtt{uniform}
applied to
a
and
b
computes. Then
we use a monotonic, differentiable homeomorphism
g
from
\mathbb{R}
to
the open interval
]a,b[
in order to transport that to the whole of

\mathbb{R}
. (One may observe that
]a,b[
differs from
[a,b]
, but
that will not matter, since the complement of
]a,b[
in
[a,b]
has

\upsilon_{[a,b]}
-measure
0
.) We have chosen

g(u)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\arctan u
,
a=-\pi/2
,
b=\pi/2
here.

Let us check our claim formally; this is a paper on semantics, and we
need to check that our semantics produces the intended result. First,
for every environment
\rho
, for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
such that

a<b
,
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{uniform}}\right\rrbracket\rho(a)(b)
is the minimal
valuation
(\lambda x.\delta_{a+(b-a)x)})^{\dagger}(i[\lambda_{1}])
.
For every open subset
U
of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
, therefore,

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\mathtt{uniform}}\right\rrbracket\rho(a)(b)(U)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}}\delta_{i(a)+(i(b)-i(a))\times\mathbf{u})}(U)di[\lambda_{1}]

\displaystyle=\int_{u\in\mathbb{R}}\delta_{(i(a)+(i(b)-i(a))\times i(u))}(U)d\lambda_{1}

by the change-of-variables formula

\displaystyle=\int_{u\in\mathbb{R}}\delta_{i(a+(b-a)u)}(U)d\lambda_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\chi_{i^{-1}(U)}(a+(b-a)u)du

\displaystyle\text{using the identity }\delta_{c}(U)=\chi_{U}(c)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{b-a}\int_{a}^{b}\chi_{i^{-1}(U)}(v)dv

\displaystyle\text{by letting }v\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}a+(b-a)u

\displaystyle=\upsilon_{[a,b]}(i^{-1}(U)).

Therefore
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{uniform}}\right\rrbracket\rho(a)(b)=i[\upsilon_{[a,b]}]
.

Next, we evaluate
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{lebesgue}}\right\rrbracket\rho(U)
for every open
subset
U
of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
as follows. Let us abbreviate the term

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}(\underline{1.0}/(\underline{1.0}+z\times z));\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits(\underline{\arctan}\;z)
as
M(z)
. For every
u\in\mathbb{R}
, for
every open subset
U
of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
, letting

\nu_{u}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\left\llbracket{M(z)}\right\rrbracket\rho[z\mapsto i(u)]
, we have the
following, where
g(u)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\arctan u
, and therefore
g^{\prime}(u)=1/(1+u^{2})
:

\displaystyle\nu_{u}(U)

\displaystyle=(\lambda\_.\delta_{g(u)})^{\dagger}(g^{\prime}(u)\delta_{*})(U)

\displaystyle=\int_{\_\in\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}}\right\rrbracket}\delta_{g(u)}(U)dg^{\prime}(u)\delta_{*}

\displaystyle=g^{\prime}(u)\chi_{i^{-1}(U)}(g(u)).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\mathtt{lebesgue}}\right\rrbracket\rho(U)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}}\left\llbracket{M(z)}\right\rrbracket\rho[z\mapsto\mathbf{u}](U)di[\upsilon_{[-\pi/2,\pi/2]}]

\displaystyle=\int_{u\in\mathbb{R}}\left\llbracket{M(z)}\right\rrbracket\rho[z\mapsto i(u)](U)d\upsilon_{[-\pi/2,\pi/2]}

by the change-of-variables formula

\displaystyle=\int_{u\in\mathbb{R}}\nu_{u}(U)d\upsilon_{[-\pi/2,\pi/2]}

\displaystyle=\int_{u\in\mathbb{R}}g^{\prime}(u)\chi_{i^{-1}(U)}(g(u))d\upsilon_{[-\pi/2,\pi/2]}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}g^{\prime}(u)\chi_{i^{-1}(U)}(g(u))du

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\chi_{i^{-1}(U)}(t)dt

\displaystyle\text{by letting }t\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}g(u)

\displaystyle=\lambda(i^{-1}(U)).

Therefore, as promised,
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{lebesgue}}\right\rrbracket\rho
is the image
measure
i[\lambda]
of Lebesgue measure
\lambda
on
\mathbb{R}
.

9.6
Generating exponential distributions

Generating an exponential distribution is another classical example.
The novelty is that this is defined from Lebesgue measure
\lambda
by
a density function that is
not
continuous. Although ISPCF can
only express continuous functions, we show that this will not prevent
us from defining the exponential distribution.

For simplicity, we consider the exponential distribution with
parameter
1
. The density map is:

\displaystyle g(x)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}e^{-x}&\text{if }x\geq 0\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

We note that
g
is not continuous at
0
. However, we have the
following candidate ISPCF term, assuming the relevant primitives:

\displaystyle\mathtt{exp\_density}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}.\boldsymbol{\mathtt{if}}\;{(\underline{\text{pos}}\;x)}\;(\underline{\exp}(-x))\;\underline{0.0}.

For every environment
\rho
, we see that

\overline{g}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\left\llbracket{\mathtt{exp\_density}}\right\rrbracket
maps
\bot
to

\bot
, and every interval
[a,b]
to
[e^{-b},e^{-a}]
if
a>0
,
to
[0,0]
if
b<0
, and to
\bot
otherwise. In particular,

\left\llbracket{\mathtt{exp\_density}}\right\rrbracket(i(a))
coincides with
i(g(a))
for
all points of continuity of
g
, namely for every
a\neq 0
.

The most obvious way to implement the exponential distribution is by
using
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}
. We assume a term
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda]
, where
\lambda

is Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
.

\displaystyle\mathtt{expo}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda]};\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}(\mathtt{exp\_density}\;x);\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits x.

For every open subset
W
of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
, we compute:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\mathtt{expo}}\right\rrbracket\rho(W)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}}|\overline{g}(\mathbf{x})|.\delta_{\mathbf{x}}(W)di[\lambda]

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\overline{g}(i(x))|.\delta_{i(x)}(W)d\lambda

by the change-of-variables formula

\displaystyle=\int_{x\in\mathbb{R}}g(x).\chi_{i^{-1}(W)}(x)d\lambda.

The last line is justified by the fact that

\delta_{i(x)}(W)=\chi_{i^{-1}(W)}(x)
, and more importantly, by
the fact that
|\overline{g}(i(x))|
and
g(x)
coincide for every

x\in\mathbb{R}
except on a set of Lebesgue measure
0
. (Namely, when

x=0
, in which case
g(x)=1
and
|\overline{g}(i(x))|=0
.)

It follows that
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{expo}}\right\rrbracket\rho
is exactly

i[g\cdot\lambda]
, the image by
i
of the exponential
distribution
g\cdot\lambda
.

Another way of implementing the exponential distribution is as:

\displaystyle\mathtt{expo}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits(-\underline{\log}\;x),

assuming a term
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]
for sampling along the Lebesgue
measure
\lambda_{1}
on
[0,1]
. We check that this also gives the
correct result. For every open subset
W
of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
,

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\mathtt{expo}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket\rho(W)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}}\delta_{-\left\llbracket{\underline{\log}}\right\rrbracket\rho(\mathbf{x})}(W)di[\lambda_{1}]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\chi_{W}(-\log x)dx,

by the change-of-variables formula, the fact that integrating along

\lambda_{1}
means integrating on
[0,1]
, and the equality

\delta_{a}(W)=\chi_{W}(a)
. We now let
x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}e^{-t}
.
The latter integral is then equal to
\int_{0}^{+\infty}\chi_{W}(t)e^{-t}dt=(g\cdot\lambda)(W)
. Therefore
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{expo}^{\prime}}\right\rrbracket\rho=\left\llbracket{\mathtt{expo}}\right\rrbracket\rho=i[g\cdot\lambda]
.

A final implementation of the exponential distribution, due to von
Neumann, consists in simulating a distribution with density

(1-x)+(\frac{x^{2}}{2!}-\frac{x^{3}}{3!})+\cdots+(\frac{x^{2n}}{(2n)!}-\frac{x^{2n+1}}{(2n+1)!})+\cdots=e^{-x}
. We
will leave the verification that the term
\mathtt{von\_neumann}

below again has the same semantics
i[g\cdot\lambda]
as

\mathtt{expo}
and
\mathtt{expo}^{\prime}
, as an exercise. We first define:

\displaystyle\mathtt{longest\_decreasing\_run}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}(\lambda f_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to D\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}}.\lambda x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}.\lambda n_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}}.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{u\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]};

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{if}}\;{(\underline{\text{pos}}(u-x))}\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits n

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad(f\;u\;(n+\underline{1})))

Given any
x\in[0,1]
,

\left\llbracket{\mathtt{longest\_decreasing\_run}}\right\rrbracket\rho(x)(0)
returns the
largest
n
such that
x>u_{1}>u_{2}>\cdots>u_{n}
(
\leq u_{n+1}
)
for randomly uniformly distributed real numbers
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
, …,
in
[0,1]
. It is a standard exercice to show that

\left\llbracket{\mathtt{longest\_decreasing\_run}}\right\rrbracket\rho(x)(0)
is the
distribution on
\mathbb{Z}_{\bot}
giving probability

\frac{x^{n}}{n!}-\frac{x^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}
to each
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
and probability
0
to all other elements. Drawing a value
n
at
random with respect to that distribution, the probability that
n
is
even is

\sum_{n\text{ even}}(\frac{x^{n}}{n!}-\frac{x^{n+1}}{(n+1)!})=e^{-x}
. Assuming a term

\underline{\text{odd}}\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}
testing whether its
argument is odd, one then defines:

\displaystyle\mathtt{von\_neumann}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]};

\displaystyle\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}(\lambda f_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to D\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}.\lambda\ell_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{if}}\;(\underline{\text{odd}}(\mathtt{longest\_decreasing\_run}\;x\;0))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad f\;(\ell+\underline{1.0})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\ell)x

The
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}
expression in the middle maps every real number
\ell

to
\ell+m
, where
m
is the average number of calls to

\mathtt{longest\_decreasing\_run}\;x\;0
it takes it to return an even
number.

We direct the reader to
[
Fan et al., 2019
]
for an explanation and some
faster algorithms based on the same principle. See also

[
Forsythe, 1972
]
for a generalization to densities of the form

e^{-G(x)}
, including normal distributions.

9.7
Distributions on higher-order objects

All our previous examples were about building distributions on simple
types such as
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
or
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\times\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
. One may be
interested in generating distributions on functions, or even on types
of distributions themselves.

Let us start with the following problem. We are given a countably
infinite family of distributions on some type
\tau
, as the value of
a parameter
F\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to D\tau
, and we wish to find a random
function
f
of type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\tau
, such that
f(0)
,
f(1)
,
…, are independently distributed according to the distributions

F(0)
,
F(1)
, …, respectively. Formally, we look for a
distribution of type
D(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\tau)
over those functions.

At first sight, this looks like an infinite-dimensional generalization
of the product distribution term
\mathtt{prod}_{\sigma,\tau}

introduced earlier. For example, we may think of writing:

\displaystyle\mathtt{wrong\_infinite\_prod}_{\tau}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}(\lambda P_{(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to D\tau)\to D(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\tau)}.\lambda F_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to D\tau}.

\displaystyle\qquad\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\tau}\leftarrow\underline{\mathrm{head}}\;F};

\displaystyle\qquad\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{rest_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\tau}\leftarrow P(\underline{\mathrm{tail}}\;F)};

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits(x::rest)),

where
\underline{\mathrm{head}}F\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}F(\underline{0})
extracts the first element
of the sequence encoded by
F
,

\underline{\mathrm{tail}}F\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda n_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}}.F(n+\underline{1})
extracts
the remaining elements, and

x::rest\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda n_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}}.\boldsymbol{\mathtt{if}}{(n=\underline{0})}\;x\;(rest\;(n-\underline{1}))
adds
x
to the front of
rest
. However,
and as the lack of base case for the recursion may hint of, this is
wrong: the semantics of
\mathtt{wrong\_infinite\_prod}_{\tau}
is
merely the zero valuation; that program never terminates.

In fact, our problem has no solution. The reason is that the total
mass of what we want to compute is undefined (and this undefinedness
does not just apply to our domain-theoretic semantics, but to all the
semantics we know of). We are given infinitely many distributions

\mu_{0}
,
\mu_{1}
, …,
\mu_{n}
, … on the same space

X\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
, as input. Their infinite product,
if it exists, has total mass
\prod_{n=0}^{+\infty}a_{n}
, where

a_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mu_{n}(X)
is the total mass of
\mu_{n}
. That only makes
sense if the infinite product
\prod_{n=0}^{+\infty}a_{n}
converges.
For example, it makes non sense if
a_{n}=1/2
for
n
odd and
a_{n}=2

for
n
even. Such cases are easy to build using
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}
.

Let us consider a slightly easier problem, where all the distributions

\mu_{n}
are constrained to be the same distribution
\mu
; we wish to
compute the infinite product
\mu^{\infty}
of
\mu
with itself. Now
this simplified problem still does not have any solution (unless

\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
is empty), and this is now particular to our
domain-theoretic semantics. Indeed, let us imagine that the map

f\colon\mu\mapsto\mu^{\infty}
were computable, hence
Scott-continuous, and let
x
be any fixed element of
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
.
Then the map
a\in[0,1]\mapsto(a\delta_{x})^{\infty}(\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket)

would be Scott-continuous. But that function maps
1
to
1
, and all
other elements of
[0,1]
to
0
, and is therefore not
Scott-continuous.

Instead, we will show how one can answer our problem in special cases.
The first special case we consider is to build the product

\lambda_{1}^{\infty}
of a countably infinite number of copies of
Lebesgue measure
\lambda_{1}
on
[0,1]
. By definition,

\lambda_{1}^{\infty}
is the unique continuous valuation on the countable
topological product
[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
whose image valuation

\pi_{S}[\lambda_{1}^{\infty}]
onto
[0,1]^{S}
is the (finite) product
valuation
\prod_{n\in S}\lambda_{1}
, for every finite subset
S
of

\mathbb{N}
. We write
\pi_{S}\colon[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}\to[0,1]^{S}
for
projection onto the coordinates in
S
. This exists and is unique by
general theorems
[
Goubault-Larrecq, 2021
, Theorem 5.3]
.

We embed
[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
, and more generally
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
, into

\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket=[\mathbb{Z}_{\bot}\to\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}]
as follows. There
is a map
j\colon\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket
defined by

j(\vec{x})(n)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}i(x_{n})
for every

\vec{x}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{(x_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
and every

n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
i
is the usual embedding of
\mathbb{R}
into

\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
, restricted to
[0,1]
; and
j(\vec{x})(n)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\bot

for every
n\in\{-1,-2,\cdots\}\cup\{\bot\}
.

Lemma 9.3
.

The Scott topology of
\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket
has a subbase of
open sets of the form

[n\in V]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{f\in[\mathbb{Z}_{\bot}\to\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}]\mid f(n)\in V\}
, where
n
ranges over
\mathbb{Z}_{\bot}
and
V
ranges over the open
subsets of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
.

The map
j
is a topological embedding of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
into

\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket
.

Proof.

\mathbb{Z}_{\bot}
and
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
are bc-domains, and therefore the Scott
topology on
[\mathbb{Z}_{\bot}\to\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}]
coincides with the topology of
pointwise convergence
[
Goubault-Larrecq, 2015
, Proposition 11.2]
. The
latter is the topology generated by the indicated subbasic open sets

[n\in V]
, by definition. This can also be deduced from
Proposition II-4.6 of
[
Gierz et al., 2003
]
.

The inverse image
j^{-1}([n\in V])
is equal to the subbasic open
set
\pi_{n}^{-1}(U)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\vec{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid x_{n}\in U\}

(where
U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}i^{-1}(V)
) if
n\in\mathbb{N}
, is empty if

n\not\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bot\not\in V
, and is the whole of

\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
otherwise. Therefore
j
is continuous.

Finally, every subbasic open set
\pi_{n}^{-1}(U)
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
U

open in
\mathbb{R}
) is equal to
j^{-1}([n\in V])
, where
V
is any
open subset of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
such that
i^{-1}(V)=U
(which exists
since
i
is an embedding). Hence
j
is a topological embedding.
∎

We will build a term of type
D(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}})
whose semantics is

j[\lambda_{1}^{\infty}]
—a continuous valuation that now makes sense;
this will be stated in Theorem
9.10
.

The idea is simple. We draw a real number at random using

\lambda_{1}
, we write it in binary, cut (or dice) its sequence of bits
into a countably infinite partition of subsequences of bits, and we
reassemble (or splice) each subsequence into a new real number in

[0,1]
. The dicing operation is far from being continuous, but this
will not matter; the reason why will be given in
Remark
9.5
.

Let us introduce the mathematical objects we will need. Let

\upsilon
be the uniform measure on infinite sequences of bits; this
is the unique Borel measure on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
(where
\{0,1\}
has
the discrete topology) such that

\upsilon(\{s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid s_{0}=b_{0},\cdots,s_{k-1}=b_{k-1}\})=1/2^{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and elements

b_{0},\ldots,b_{k-1}\in\{0,1\}
. The easiest way to show its
existence is as the image measure of Lebesgue measure by a suitable
map.

Lemma 9.4
.

The function
\mathrm{bin}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
that maps
every number in
[0,1[
to the string of bits in its binary
expansion, every negative number to the all zero string
0^{\omega}
,
and every number larger than or equal to
1
to the all one string

1^{\omega}
is measurable. The image measure
\mathrm{bin}[\lambda_{1}]
is
the unique measure
\upsilon
on
[0,1]
such that

\upsilon(\{s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mid s_{0}=b_{0},\cdots,s_{k-1}=b_{k-1}\})=1/2^{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and elements

b_{0},\ldots,b_{k-1}\in\{0,1\}
.

Proof.

For every finite string
b\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(b_{0},b_{1},\cdots,b_{k-1})
of
bits, let
U_{b}
be the set of infinite strings of bits having
s
as
a prefix. The sets
U_{b}
form a countable base of the topology on

\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and
\mathrm{bin}^{-1}(U_{b})
is the interval

[\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}b_{i}/2^{i+1},\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}b_{i}/2^{i+1}+1/2^{k}[
if not all bits
b_{i}
are equal to
0
or to
1
,

]-\infty,1/2^{k}[
if all the bits
b_{i}
are equal to
0
, and to

[1-1/2^{k},+\infty[
otherwise. Every interval is in the Borel

\sigma
-algebra, and every open subset of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is a
countable union of sets
U_{s}
, so the inverse image of any open
subset of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
by
\mathrm{bin}
is Borel. It follows that

\mathrm{bin}
is measurable. Then

\mathrm{bin}[\lambda_{1}](U_{b})=\lambda_{1}(\mathrm{bin}^{-1}(U_{b}))=\lambda(\mathrm{bin}^{-1}(U_{s})\cap[0,1])
is equal to
1/2^{k}
. Finally, the
sets
U_{b}
form a
\pi
-system, whence the claim of uniqueness.
∎

The function
\mathrm{bin}
is not continuous on the whole of
\mathbb{R}
, hence
not implementable. Its restriction to the set of non-dyadic numbers
in
[0,1]
, is, though, and we make this clearer by defining a
continuous map
\mathrm{bin}^{\prime}\colon\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}\to\{0,1\}_{\bot}^{\mathbb{N}}
that
implements
\mathrm{bin}
on the non-dyadic numbers in
[0,1]
. Roughly,

\mathrm{bin}^{\prime}
is implemented as follows. Given an argument value of type

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
, if the value is smaller than
1/2
(which one can test by
using
pos
), then emit a zero bit and multiply the value by
2
;
if that value is larger than
1/2
, then emit a one bit, subtract

1/2
from the value and multiply the result by
2
; then collect all
the emitted bits into a sequence, which may be infinite, or finite (if
computation ever gets stuck). Formally, we define

\mathrm{bin}^{\prime}(\underline{a})_{k}
by induction on
k
as follows. If

\underline{a}=\bot
, then
\mathrm{bin}^{\prime}(\underline{a})_{k}
is
\bot
. If

\underline{a}=[a,b]
and
k=0
, then
\mathrm{bin}^{\prime}([a,b])_{0}
is defined
as
0
if
b<1/2
,
1
if
a>1/2
, and as
\bot
otherwise. If

\underline{a}=[a,b]
and
k\geq 1
, then
\mathrm{bin}^{\prime}([a,b])_{k}
is
defined as
\mathrm{bin}^{\prime}(\underline{c})_{k-1}
where
\underline{c}
is equal
to
[2a,2b]
if
b<1/2
,
[2a-1,2b-1]
if
a>1/2
, and
\bot

otherwise. One checks easily that
\mathrm{bin}^{\prime}
is continuous.

Remark 9.5
.

Let
E_{0}
be the collection of all points that are either dyadic or
outside
[0,1]
. This is a set of
\lambda_{1}
-measure zero,
because there are only countably many dyadic numbers and
\lambda_{1}

is supported on
[0,1]
. Since
\mathrm{bin}
and
\mathrm{bin}^{\prime}
coincide outside

E_{0}
, the image measures of
\lambda_{1}
by each of those two
functions is the same. (Formally,
j^{\prime}[\mathrm{bin}[\lambda_{1}]]
and

\mathrm{bin}^{\prime}[i[\lambda_{1}]]
coincide, for any environment
\rho
, and
where
j^{\prime}
is the obvious topological embedding

\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\{0,1\}_{\bot}^{\mathbb{N}}
.)

Lemma 9.6
.

The map
\mathrm{num}\colon\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{R}
that sends
s
to

\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}s_{i}/2^{i+1}
is continuous, and the image measure

\mathrm{num}[\upsilon]
is equal to
\lambda_{1}
.

Proof.

Let
U
be any open subset of
\mathbb{R}
, and
s\in\mathrm{num}^{-1}(U)
.
For some
\epsilon>0
, the interval

]\mathrm{num}(s)-\epsilon,\mathrm{num}(s)+\infty[
is included in
U
. We pick

k\in\mathbb{N}
so that
1/2^{k}<\epsilon
. Then the set of elements

t\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
t_{0}=s_{0}
, …,
t_{k}=s_{k}
is an
open neighborhood of
s
that is included in
\mathrm{num}^{-1}(U)
,
showing that
\mathrm{num}^{-1}(U)
is open. Therefore
\mathrm{num}
is
continuous.

It is easy to see that
\mathrm{num}(\mathrm{bin}(x))=x
for every

x\in[0,1]
, so that
(\mathrm{num}\circ\mathrm{bin})^{-1}(E)
and
E
have
the same intersection with
[0,1]
, for every subset
E
of

\mathbb{R}
. Hence, for every Borel measurable subset
E
of
\mathbb{R}
,
and using Lemma
9.4
,

\mathrm{num}[\upsilon](E)=\mathrm{num}[\mathrm{bin}[\lambda_{1}]](E)=\lambda((\mathrm{num}\circ\mathrm{bin})^{-1}(E)\cap[0,1])=\lambda(E\cap[0,1])=\lambda_{1}(E)
.
∎

We assume an bijective computable pairing map

\langle\_,\_\rangle\colon\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}
. A practical
one is the map that interleaves the two binary representations of the
numbers in argument, namely

\langle\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}a_{i}2^{i},\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}b_{j}2^{j}\rangle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}a_{i}2^{2i}+\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}b_{j}2^{2j+1}
.

Given a random string of bits
s
, the strings

s[m]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{(s_{\langle m,n\rangle})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
are themselves random and independent. We express this by the
following well-known lemma. In order to state it, for
every
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, let
\mathop{\uparrow}s
(“shift
s
”) be the
string such that

(\mathop{\uparrow}s)_{\langle m,n\rangle}=s_{\langle m+1,n\rangle}
for all

m,n\in\mathbb{N}
. In other words,
(\mathop{\uparrow}s)[m]=s[m+1]
for every

m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Computing
s[m]
from
s
and
m
is easy, considering that pairing

\langle\_,\_,\rangle
is computable.

The spaces
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
(\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}})^{2}
are compact
Hausdorff, and therefore locally compact and sober, in particular
LCS-complete. We recall that, in that case, every continuous
valuation on those spaces extends to a Borel measure

[
de Brecht et al., 2019
, Theorem 1.1]
. The extension is unique for bounded
continuous valuations such as
\upsilon
or

\upsilon\otimes\upsilon
, because the open sets form a
\pi
-system
that generates the Borel
\sigma
-algebra. The spaces
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}

and
(\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}})^{2}
are also second-countable, and then by
Adamski’s theorem
[
Adamski, 1977
, Theorem 3.1]
every measure
restricts to a continuous valuation on the open sets. In other words,
bounded measures and bounded continuous valuations are in bijective
correspondence on those spaces. Hence we can trade freely between the
notions of (bounded) continuous valuations and (bounded) measures on
those spaces.

Lemma 9.7
.

The map
split\colon s\mapsto(s[0],\mathop{\uparrow}s)
is a homeomorphism
of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
onto
(\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}})^{2}
. The image continuous
valuation (resp., measure)
split[\upsilon]
is the product
valuation (resp., product measure)
\upsilon\otimes\upsilon
.

Proof.

The first part is a direct consequence of the fact that the pairing
map is bijective. For the second part, we reason on continuous
valuations, since that will be slightly easier. For every pair of
disjoint finite subsets
I
and
J
of
\mathbb{N}
, let
U_{I,J}
be the
set of elements
s
of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
such that
s_{m}=0
for every

m\in I
and
s_{n}=1
for every
n\in J
. We see that those sets
form a base of the topology on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and that

\upsilon(U_{I,J})=1/2^{|I|+|J|}
, where
|\_|
denotes
cardinality, using the last part of Lemma
9.4
, A
base of the product topology on
(\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}})^{2}
is given by the
sets
U_{I,J}\times U_{I^{\prime},J^{\prime}}
, and:

\displaystyle split[\upsilon](U_{I,J}\times U_{I^{\prime},J^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\upsilon(split^{-1}(U_{I,J}\times U_{I^{\prime},J^{\prime}}))

\displaystyle=\upsilon(U_{I^{\prime\prime},J^{\prime\prime}}),

where

I^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\langle 0,k\rangle\mid k\in I\}\cup\{\langle m^{\prime}+1,k\rangle\mid m^{\prime},k\in\mathbb{N},\langle m^{\prime},k\rangle\in I^{\prime}\}
and

J^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{\langle 0,k\rangle\mid k\in J\}\cup\{\langle n^{\prime}+1,k\rangle\mid n^{\prime},k\in\mathbb{N},\langle n^{\prime},k\rangle\in J^{\prime}\}
.
Hence

split[\upsilon](U_{I,J}\times U_{I^{\prime},J^{\prime}})=1/2^{|I^{\prime\prime}|+|J^{\prime\prime}|}=1/2^{|I|+|I^{\prime}|+|J|+|J^{\prime}|}
. We also have:

\displaystyle(\upsilon\otimes\upsilon)(U_{I,J}\times U_{I^{\prime},J^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\upsilon(U_{I,J}).\upsilon(U_{I^{\prime},J^{\prime}})=1/2^{|I|+|J|}.1/2^{|I^{\prime}|+|J^{\prime}|},

so
split[\upsilon]
and
\upsilon\otimes\upsilon
coincide on
the basic open sets
U_{I,J}
. Using modularity, they must coincide
on any finite disjoint union of basic open sets, and by
Scott-continuity, they must coincide on any arbitrary disjoint union
of basic open sets. (This is an argument we have sketched in the
proof of Theorem
4.1
already.) Since any two
basic open sets
U_{I,J}
are either comparable or disjoint, every
open subset of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
is such a disjoint union, and this
concludes the proof.
∎

Corollary 9.8
.

For every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

1.

the map

split_{N}\colon s\mapsto(s[0],s[1],\cdots,s[N-1],\mathop{\uparrow}^{N}s)

is a homeomorphism of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
onto
(\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}})^{N+1}
;

2.

the image measure
split_{N}[\upsilon]
is the product measure

\underbrace{\upsilon\otimes\upsilon\otimes\cdots\otimes\upsilon}_{N+1}
;

3.

the map

\varpi_{N}\colon s\mapsto(s[0],s[1],\cdots,s[N-1])
is
continuous from
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
to
(\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}})^{N}
, and

\varpi_{N}[\upsilon]
is the product measure

\underbrace{\upsilon\otimes\upsilon\otimes\cdots\otimes\upsilon}_{N}
.

Proof.

We show 1 and 2 by induction on
N
. This is obvious if
N=0
, and is by
Lemma
9.7
if
N=1
. If
N\geq 2
, we note that

split_{N}=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}}\times split_{N-1})\circ split
.
Hence
split_{N}
is a homeomorphism, using the induction hypothesis,
and

split_{N}[\upsilon]=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}}\times split_{N-1})[split[\upsilon]]=(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{R}}\times split_{N-1})[\upsilon\otimes\upsilon]
(by Lemma
9.7
)

=\upsilon\otimes split_{N-1}[\upsilon]=\upsilon\otimes\underbrace{\upsilon\otimes\upsilon\otimes\cdots\otimes\upsilon}_{N}
, by induction hypothesis.

Item 3 follows from 1 and 2 by composing with the appropriate
projection map.
∎

Remark 9.9
.

It is also true that the image
measure

split[\upsilon]
is
the product
measure
of
\upsilon
by itself. The proof is
similar, and it suffices to observe that the sets
U_{I,J}
form a

\pi
-system that generates the
\sigma
-algebra on
(\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}})^{2}
.
A subtle point with that approach is that the latter is a product of

\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
with itself, certainly, but in which category? In
Lemma
9.7
, the product is taken in
{\mathbf{Top}}
. The
measure-theoretic approach requires to work with the product taken
in the category of measurable spaces and measurable maps. In
general, those two products differ. The problem is similar to the
issue discussed in Section
4
.
Fortunately, there is no such problem here, because the Borel

\sigma
-algebra of the topological product of two Polish spaces
coincides with the product of the two
\sigma
-algebras.

We assume an ISPCF constant

\underline{\text{mux}}\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
whose semantics
mux

satisfies the following: for every
x\in\mathbb{R}
, for every

m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\text{mux}(i(x))(m)=i(\mathrm{num}^{\prime}(\mathrm{bin}^{\prime}(x)[m])),

where
\mathrm{bin}^{\prime}
was introduced before Remark
9.5
, and
where
\mathrm{num}^{\prime}
is any continuous map from
\{0,1\}_{\bot}^{\mathbb{N}}
to

\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
that extends
\mathrm{num}
, in the sense that

\mathrm{num}^{\prime}(s)=i(\mathrm{num}(s))
for every
s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
; for
example,

\displaystyle\mathrm{num}^{\prime}(s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}i(\mathrm{num}(s))&\text{if }s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\\
{}[x,x+1/2^{k}]&\text{where }x\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}s_{i}/2^{i+1}\\
&\text{if }s_{0},\cdots,s_{k-1}\neq\bot,s_{k}=\bot\end{array}\right.

We are now ready to implement an ISPCF term computing

\lambda_{1}^{\infty}
:

\displaystyle\mathtt{rand\_uniform\_seq}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{r_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits(\underline{\text{mux}}\;r).

This is a term of type
D(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}})
, and we claim that it
draws an infinite sequence of independent, uniformly distributed real
numbers in
[0,1]
.

Theorem 9.10
.

For every environment
\rho
,
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rand\_uniform\_seq}}\right\rrbracket\rho=j[\lambda_{1}^{\infty}]
.

Proof.

Let
\pi_{N}\colon\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket
map
f
to

(f(0),f(1),\cdots,f(N-1))
. We start by fixing
N
, and we
compute
\pi_{N}[\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rand\_uniform\_seq}}\right\rrbracket\rho]
. This is
the valuation that maps every open subset
U
of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}^{N}
to the
probability that a function
f
drawn at random according to

\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rand\_uniform\_seq}}\right\rrbracket\rho
produces a tuple

(f(0),f(1),\cdots,f(N-1))
that falls in
U
. Let us begin by
noting that

\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rand\_uniform\_seq}}\right\rrbracket\rho=\text{mux}[i[\lambda_{1}]]
.

Let
g\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
map
x
to

{(\mathrm{num}(\mathrm{bin}(x)[m]))}_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
. It is easy to see that
g

is measurable. By definition,
g(x)_{m}=\text{mux}(i(x))(m)
for
every non-dyadic number
x
in
[0,1]
, namely for every

x\in\mathbb{R}\setminus E_{0}
where the set
E_{0}
was introduced in
Remark
9.5
, and for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

We claim that
j[g[\lambda_{1}]]=\text{mux}[i[\lambda_{1}]
. This
needs a reminder and a comment. We recall that
j
is the canonical
embedding of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
into
\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket
; and we
have silently promoted
\lambda_{1}
from a continuous valuation to a
measure on
\mathbb{R}
, therefore working with image
measures

rather than image
valuations
.

The claim is proved as follows. For every Borel measurable subset

E
of
\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket
,

j[g[\lambda_{1}]](E)=\lambda_{1}((j\circ g)^{-1}(E))
, while

\text{mux}[i[\lambda_{1}]=\lambda_{1}((\text{mux}\circ i)^{-1}(E))
.
The symmetric difference of
(j\circ g)^{-1}(E)
and of

(\text{mux}\circ i)^{-1}(E)
is included in
E_{0}
, which has

\lambda_{1}
-measure zero, so

j[g[\lambda_{1}]](E)=\lambda_{1}((j\circ g)^{-1}(E))
.

Let us write
i^{(N)}
for the
N
-fold product
i\times\cdots\times i
, and similarly with
\mathrm{num}^{(N)}
. For every
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\pi_{N}(\text{mux}(i(x)))=(i(g(x)_{0}),\cdots,i(g(x)_{N-1}))=(i(\mathrm{num}(\mathrm{bin}(x)[0])),\cdots,i(\mathrm{num}(\mathrm{bin}(x)[N-1])))=(i^{(N)}\circ\mathrm{num}^{(N)}\circ\varpi_{N}\circ\mathrm{bin})(x)
. It follows:

\displaystyle\pi_{N}[\text{mux}[i[\lambda_{1}]]

\displaystyle=i^{(N)}[\mathrm{num}^{(N)}[\varpi_{N}[\mathrm{bin}[\lambda_{1}]]]]

\displaystyle=i^{(N)}[\mathrm{num}^{(N)}[\varpi_{N}[\upsilon]]]

by Lemma
9.4

\displaystyle=i^{(N)}[\mathrm{num}^{(N)}[\upsilon\otimes\cdots\otimes\upsilon]]

by Corollary
9.8
, item 3

\displaystyle=i[\mathrm{num}[\upsilon]]\otimes\cdots\otimes i[\mathrm{num}[\upsilon]]

\displaystyle=i[\lambda_{1}]\otimes\cdots\otimes i[\lambda_{1}]

\displaystyle\text{by Lemma~\ref{lemma:num}}.

This is a statement about measures. Restricting to open sets, we
obtain the following statement about continuous valuations:

(*)

\pi_{N}[\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rand\_uniform\_seq}}\right\rrbracket\rho]
is the
N
-fold
valuation product of
i[\lambda_{1}]
.

We use this to prove that
\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rand\_uniform\_seq}}\right\rrbracket\rho j[\lambda_{1}^{\infty}]
, qua continuous valuations. It suffices to
show that the two sides of the equation coincide on basic open
subsets of
\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket
: as in the proof of
Theorem
4.1
or of Lemma
9.7
,
modularity and Scott-continuity will imply they they also coincide
on every open set at all.

As basic open subsets, Lemma
9.3
suggests that we take the
finite intersections of sets of the form
[n\in V]
. Without loss
of generality, let

U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[0\in V_{0}]\cap[1\in V_{1}]\cap\cdots\cap[N-1\in V_{N-1}]
. (Any set of the form
[n\in V_{n}]
where
V_{n}
is the
whose of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
can be inserted at will, since they will not
modify the intersection.) We note that

U=\pi_{N}^{-1}(V_{0}\times V_{1}\times\cdots\times V_{N-1})
, so:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rand\_uniform\_seq}}\right\rrbracket\rho(U)

\displaystyle=\pi_{N}[\left\llbracket{\mathtt{rand\_uniform\_seq}}\right\rrbracket\rho](V_{0}\times V_{1}\times\cdots\times V_{N-1})

\displaystyle=\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}i[\lambda_{1}](V_{n})\qquad\text{by }(*).

Now, since
j(\vec{x})(n)=i(x_{n})
for all

\vec{x}\in[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

j^{-1}(U)=\pi_{N}^{-1}(i^{-1}(V_{0})\times i^{-1}(V_{1})\times\cdots\times i^{-1}(V_{N-1}))
, so:

\displaystyle j[\lambda_{1}^{\infty}](U)

\displaystyle=\pi_{N}[\lambda_{1}^{\infty}](i^{-1}(V_{0})\times i^{-1}(V_{1})\times\cdots\times i^{-1}(V_{N-1}))

\displaystyle=\prod_{n=0}^{N-1}\lambda_{1}(i^{-1}(V_{n})),

by definition of
\lambda_{1}^{\infty}
, and this concludes the proof.
∎

9.8
More distributions on higher-order objects

At this point, let us be less formal. The construction of

\lambda_{1}^{\infty}
through
\mathtt{rand\_uniform\_seq}
opens up
several avenues, which we mostly leave as programming exercises to the
reader.

For one, we can now implement the Marsaglia-Bray algorithm (see
Section
9.4
) by drawing just
one

random real. We remember that the Marsaglia-Bray algorithm uses
rejection sampling, which may require us to draw arbitrarily many
independent, random,
\lambda_{1}
-distributed real numbers. Instead,
we can just call
\mathtt{rand\_uniform\_seq}
once, obtaining a
random,
\lambda_{1}^{\infty}
-distributed function
f
(modulo the
embedding
j
); each time we need another random real number, we
simply read the next value
f(n)
, for larger and larger values of

n
.

Going further, instead of calling
\mathtt{rand\_uniform\_seq}
, we
may call
\underline{\text{mux}}
on a random
\lambda_{1}
-distributed real number
given as argument. We obtain an ISPCF term

\mathtt{box\_muller\_transform}
of type

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\times\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
(not

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to D(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\times\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}})
, since that term no longer draws
anything at random by itself) that maps a random

\lambda_{1}
-distributed real number to a pair of two independent,
random
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
-distributed real numbers. We reuse
notations and conventions introduced earlier;
\mathtt{find\_pair}

looks for the first pair of consecutive real numbers satisfying
prop

in the infinite list
randnums
,
\mathtt{box\_muller\_engine}

reimplements the
\mathtt{box\_muller^{\prime}}
procedure by using a given
infinite list of real numbers instead of drawing them at random.

\displaystyle\mathtt{find\_pair}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda prop_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}}.\lambda randnums_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}.

\displaystyle\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}(\lambda find_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}.\lambda n_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}}.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}\;{x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}=randnums\;n}\;\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}\;{}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}\;{y_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}=randnums\;(n+\underline{1})}\;\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}\;{}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{if}}(prop\;x\;y)\;\langle x,y\rangle\;find\;(n+\underline{2}))\;\underline{0}

\displaystyle\mathtt{box\_muller\_engine}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda randnums_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}.

\displaystyle\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}\;{\langle x,y\rangle=\mathtt{find\_pair}\;\mathtt{discp}\;randnums}\;\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}\;{}

\displaystyle\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}\;{u_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}=x\times x+y\times y}\;\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}\;{}

\displaystyle\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{let}}\;{m_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}=\underline{\text{sqrt}}(\underline{-2.0}\times\underline{\log}\;u/u)}\;\boldsymbol{\mathtt{in}}\;{}

\displaystyle\qquad\langle m\times x,m\times y\rangle

\displaystyle\mathtt{box\_muller\_transform}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda seed_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}.\mathtt{box\_muller\_engine}\;(\underline{\text{mux}}\;seed).

We let the reader check that the semantics of:

\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{seed\leftarrow\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits(\mathtt{box\_muller\_transform}\;seed)

is
i[\mathcal{N}(0,1)\otimes\mathcal{N}(0,1)]
, just like

\mathtt{box\_muller^{\prime}}
and
\mathtt{box\_muller}
.

The tricks described above suggest the following scheme for
implementing the infinite product
\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\infty}
. We call

\mathtt{rand\_uniform\_seq}
in order to obtain an infinite,
independent sequence of
\lambda_{1}
-distributed real numbers. We then
apply
\mathtt{box\_muller\_transform}
to each. Explicitly, we form
the ISPCF term:

\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{f_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\leftarrow\mathtt{rand\_uniform\_seq}};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits{\lambda n_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}}.\mathtt{box\_muller\_transform}(f\;n)},

of type
D(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}})
.

Again, this can be made into a transformer

\mathtt{box\_muller\_sequence\_transform}\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to D(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}})

that maps any random
\lambda_{1}
-distributed real number to a random

\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{\infty}
-distributed infinite sequence of real
numbers. This can also be iterated. We let the reader explore around
this idea. For example, given two maps

mean,sigma\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
, build a term that maps any
random
\lambda_{1}
-distributed real number to a random infinite
sequence of independent real numbers, whose
n
th entry follows a
normal distribution with mean
mean(n)
and with standard deviation

sigma(n)
.

One can implement infinite independent sequences of random real
numbers following various other distributions in the same way. For
example, do this for infinite products of exponential distributions.

This machinery can also be used to implement non-trivial distributions
over distributions. A typical, and useful, application is the
so-called
Dirichlet process

[
Ferguson, 1973
]
, which is widely
used as a source of Bayesian priors in so-called nonparametric
estimation problems. Given a base distribution
H
over some space

X
, and a parameter
\alpha\in]0,1[
, the Dirichlet process

DP(H)
is a distribution over the space of probability distributions
over
X
, which one may describe as follows. We draw an infinite
sequences of elements
x_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, from
X
. At step
n
,
with probability
\alpha/(\alpha+n)
, we draw
x_{n}
from
X
,
independently from all previous values, using the distribution
H
;
otherwise, we draw
x_{n}
