at random among the previous values, each
having the same probability, in other words with respect to the
distribution
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\delta_{x_{i}}
. (This is
undefined if
n=0
, but then, if
n=0
, that second case happens with
zero probability.) This process yields a random infinite sequence,
which we can use directly, or which we can convert to a distribution,
the
directing measure
, mapping every measurable subset
E
of

X
to the limit of
(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\delta_{x_{i}})(E)
as

n
tends to
+\infty
, which exists
H
-almost surely by de Finetti’s
theorem
[
Kallenberg, 2002
, Chapter 1.1]
, based on the fact that
the sequence
{(x_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a so-called exchangeable
sequence.

In principle, one can compute the directing measure from any so-called
exchangeable sequence, as shown by Daniel Roy

[
Roy, 2011
, Chapter IV]
. In the special case of the Dirichlet
process, there is a popular, and more efficient, implementation of

DP(H)
, using the so-called
stick-breaking algorithm
, see
Figure 2 of
[
Goodman et al., 2008
]
for example. (We also refer to that
paper for an application of the Dirichlet process to an interesting,
stochastic form of memoization.) While the latter relies on memoizing
facilities (hence on global state, possibly hidden), one can implement
it in ISPCF as follows, given
H^{\infty}
(not
H
!) as argument.

We assume that
X
is the denotation
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
of some type
\tau
,
and that the variable
proc_{D(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\tau)}
holds a
representation of
H^{\infty}
. We draw a random function

atoms\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\tau
with respect to
H^{\infty}
,
representing some infinite sequence
{(\theta_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
elements of
X
; this uses
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits
and
proc
. We also draw a random
function
sticks\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
with respect to the
distribution
\text{Beta}(1,\alpha)^{\infty}
(the infinite product of
the so-called
\beta
distribution with parameters
1
and
\alpha
),
representing a random sequence
{(\beta^{\prime}_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. We define
a new random (but no longer independent) sequence

{(\beta_{n})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, where

\beta_{n}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\beta^{\prime}_{n}.\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(1-\beta^{\prime}_{i})
. and we
return the (random) distribution

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\beta_{n}\delta_{\theta_{n}}
.

While that would be possible in ISPCF, we do not compute any
\beta_{n}

explicitly. Instead, the final distribution is obtained by the
following informal procedure: with probability
\beta^{\prime}_{0}
, return

\theta_{0}
; else, with probability
\beta^{\prime}_{1}
, return
\theta_{1}
;
else, with probability
\beta^{\prime}_{2}
, return
\theta_{2}
, and so on. This
terminates with probability
1
.

This can be implemented in ISPCF by drawing a third function

rand\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
at random with respect to

\lambda_{1}^{\infty}
, then returning

atoms\;(\mathtt{pick\_a\_stick}\;\allowbreak sticks\;\allowbreak rand\;0)
, where:

\displaystyle\mathtt{pick\_a\_stick}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda sticks_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}.\lambda rand_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}.\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}(\lambda pick_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}}.

\displaystyle\qquad\lambda j_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}}.

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\boldsymbol{\mathtt{if}}(\underline{\text{pos}}(sticks\;j-rand\;j))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad j

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad(pick\;(j+1))).

We have taken the same procedure names as in

[
Goodman et al., 2008
, Figure 2]
, in the hope that this will ease a
comparison with the Church implementation of the Dirichlet process
given there.

By now, we hope to have provided enough examples in order to convince
the reader that one can write enough useful distributions in a
language with a domain-theoretic semantics such as ISPCF. Let us
proceed with matters of operational semantics.

10
Operational Semantics

The simplest possible operational semantics of ISPCF is one where

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]
draws actual real numbers at random, following the
distribution
\mu
. We follow similar other proposals

[
Vákár et al., 2019
,
Dal Lago and Hoshino, 2019
,
Ehrhard et al., 2018
]
pretty closely. The main
difference is that we will draw
exact
reals, namely elements of

\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
, instead of true reals in
\mathbb{R}
, at random. This is
really not much of a difference, since the probability that

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]
draws an exact real number that is not a true real
number is zero.

In order to describe our operational semantics formally, we consider
generalized ISPCF terms, with extra constants, one for each real
number, and we consider these generalized ISPCF terms as
configurations of an abstract machine. We then define a probabilistic
transition relation on the space of configurations. In order to do
so, we will need to topologize the space of configurations, much as
previous proposals
[
Vákár et al., 2019
,
Dal Lago and Hoshino, 2019
,
Ehrhard et al., 2018
]
defined a

\sigma
-algebra on similar spaces of configurations. The
probabilistic transition relation will be a map from configurations to
measures (rather, continuous valuations) on the space of
configurations. That map will be continuous, and not just measurable.

Notionally, a
generalized ISPCF term
is a closed ISPCF term
built on a set of constants
\Sigma
that contains exactly one
constant
\underline{\mathbf{a}}
for each
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
. We
will equate the already existing constants
\underline{r}
,

r\in\mathbb{R}
, with
\underline{[r,r]}
.

The following alternate definition will be more formal, and will allow
us to give a simple description of the topology we will put on
generalized ISPCF terms. We fix a countable enumeration
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
,
…,
x_{n}
, … of so-called
template
variables of type

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
once and for all, in such a way that we still have an
infinite supply of variables of type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
not in that list. A

template
is an ISPCF term
M
whose free variables, read from
left to right, are
x_{1}
, …,
x_{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
which does not contain any constant of type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
. In particular,
the only free variables of
M
are template variables, they occur only
once in
M
, and are numbered from
1
to
k
consecutively from left
to right. We let
\mathrm{fv}(M)
be the set of free variables of a term

M
. Then we equate a generalized ISPCF term with a

configuration

(M,\theta)
where
M
is a template and

\theta\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}^{\mathrm{fv}(M)}
. Replacing each template variable

x_{i}
that is free in
M
by
\underline{\theta(x_{i})}
yields a
generalized ISPCF term, which we write as
M\theta
. Conversely,
each generalized ISPCF term is represented by a unique configuration

(M,\theta)
:
M
has to be the given generalized ISPCF term where
each constant
\underline{r}
of type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
has been replaced by a
fresh template variable, which is then mapped to
r
by
\theta
. We
call
M
the
shape
of the generalized ISPCF term.
For example, the generalized ISPCF term

\lambda y.\underline{2.0}\times y+\underline{1.0}
is
represented by the configuration

(\lambda y.x_{1}\times y+x_{2},[x_{1}\mapsto 2.0,x_{2}\mapsto 1.0])
.

Definition 10.1

(Space of configurations
\Gamma
)
.

\Gamma
denotes the set of all configurations, ordered by

(M,\theta)\leq(N,\theta^{\prime})
if and only if
M=N
and

\theta\leq\theta^{\prime}
, namely, for every
x\in\mathrm{fv}(M)
,

\theta(x)\leq\theta^{\prime}(x)
.

Remark 10.2
.

\Gamma
is a dcpo, and in fact a continuous dcpo. To see the
latter,
\Gamma
is a coproduct over all possible shapes of finite
products of copies of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
. Each copy of
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
is a
continuous dcpos, and continuous dcpos are closed under finite
products and arbitrary coproducts.

This structure transports to a structure of continuous dcpo on the set
of generalized ISPCF terms through the bijection

(M,\theta)\mapsto M\theta
.

While our algebra of terms was rather open-ended until now, we will
need to restrict it slightly, and we will assume that
\Sigma
has

strictly observable first-order constants
, see below.

Definition 10.3

(Basic and observable types)
.

The algebra of
observable types
is:

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle::=\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}\mid\boldsymbol{\mathtt{void}}\mid\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\mid\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\mid\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}\mid\beta_{1}\times\beta_{2},

The
basic types
are
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{void}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}
, and
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
.

In other words, the observable types are all types that do not contain

\to
or
D
.

Definition 10.4

(Observable element)
.

The
observable elements
of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
, where
\beta
is an
observable type, are defined as follows. The observable elements of

\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{void}}}\right\rrbracket
,
\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}}\right\rrbracket
,
\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}}\right\rrbracket
,

\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket
are all their elements except
\bot
. The
observable elements of
\left\llbracket{\beta_{1}\times\beta_{2}}\right\rrbracket
are the
pairs
(b_{1},b_{2})
where both
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
are observable. The
observable elements of
\left\llbracket{\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}}\right\rrbracket
are the elements

\iota_{1}b
or
\iota_{2}b
, where
b
is observable.

For every observable value
a
of an observable type
\beta
, there is
a generalized ISPCF term
\underline{a}
of type
\beta
such that

\left\llbracket{\underline{a}}\right\rrbracket\rho=a
for every environment
\rho
.
This extends our preexisting underlining notations.

Definition 10.5
.

The constants
\underline{n}\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}
(
n\in\mathbb{Z}
),
\underline{*}\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}
, and
\underline{\mathbf{a}}
(
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
)
are already defined. We let
\underline{(a,b)}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\langle\underline{a},\underline{b}\rangle
,
\underline{(1,a)}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\iota_{1}\underline{a}
,
\underline{(2,b)}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\iota_{2}\underline{b}
.

In order to prevent any conflict of notations, we will require
that the only preexisting constants
\underline{f}
of ISPCF of
observable types are actually of basic types. For example, we do not
allow any constant
\underline{f}
of type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}\times\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
in our syntax.

Definition 10.6

(Strictly observable first-order constants)
.

The set
\Sigma
consists of
strictly observable first-order constants
if
and only if each constant
\underline{f}
in
\Sigma
:

•

has a
first-order type
, namely one of the form

\sigma_{1}\to\cdots\to\sigma_{k}\to\tau
, where each
\sigma_{i}

is a basic type and
\tau
is an observable type; we call
k
the

arity
of
\underline{f}
, and we write it as

\alpha(\underline{f})
;

•

its semantics
f
is
strictly observable
:
f(v_{1})\cdots(v_{k})
is
either observable or equal to
\bot
, and
f(v_{1})\cdots(v_{k})=\bot
if
v_{1}
, …, or
v_{k}
is equal to
\bot
;

•

the only preexisting constants
\underline{f}
of ISPCF of
observable types are of basic types, and their semantics
f
is
observable.

All the constants we have used in our examples are first-order, for
example
\underline{\log}\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
, but also

\underline{\text{pos}}\colon\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}
, where
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{bool}}=\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}+\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}
is
observable but not basic.

Probabilistic rules:

\displaystyle E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]]

\displaystyle\to E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{a}]\quad(a\in\mathbb{R})

(7)

\displaystyle E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a}}]

\displaystyle\to E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}]

(8)

\displaystyle E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x\leftarrow\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits M};N]

\displaystyle\to E^{0}[N[x:=M]]

Deterministic rules:

\displaystyle E[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}\;M]

\displaystyle\to E[M(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}\;M)]

\displaystyle E[(\lambda x.M)N]

\displaystyle\to E[M[x:=N]]

\displaystyle E[\underline{f}\;\underline{a_{1}}\cdots\underline{a_{k}}]

\displaystyle\to E[\underline{f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})}]

(9)

\displaystyle\text{if }k=\alpha(\underline{f})\neq 0\text{ and }f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})\text{ is observable}

\displaystyle E[\pi_{1}\langle M,N\rangle]

\displaystyle\to E[M]

\displaystyle E[\pi_{2}\langle M,N\rangle]

\displaystyle\to E[N]

\displaystyle E[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{case}}(\iota_{1}M)NP]

\displaystyle\to E[NM]

\displaystyle E[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{case}}(\iota_{2}M)NP]

\displaystyle\to E[PM]

Figure 3:
The raw operational semantics

Our operational semantics will require two kinds of evaluation
contexts. The point is that, given a generalized ISPCF term
M
of
type
D\tau
, the computation starting from
M
will in general go
through two phases
M\to^{*}\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits N\to^{*}\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits V
. The first
phase
M\to^{*}\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits N
will be allowed to use all rules, including
rules that operate probabilistic choices, and ends at some term of the
form
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits N
(or loops forever); the second phase reduces
N
to a
value
V
deterministically. The second phase applies (deterministic)
rules under arbitrary evaluation contexts, while the first phase
applies (arbitrary) rules under some so-called weak evaluation contexts.

Definition 10.7

(Evaluation contexts, values)
.

The
evaluation contexts

E
, the
weak evaluation
contexts

E_{0}
, the
values

V
and the
lazy values

V^{0}
are given by the following (pseudo-)grammar:

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle::=E^{0}\mid\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits E\mid\langle E,M\rangle\mid\langle V,E\rangle\mid\iota_{1}E\mid\iota_{2}E

\displaystyle E^{0}

\displaystyle::=[]\mid\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}E^{0}\mid E^{0}N\mid\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x\leftarrow E^{0}};N\mid\underline{f}\;\underline{a}_{1}\;\cdots\;\underline{a}_{i-1}\;E^{0}\quad(i\leq\alpha(\underline{f}))

\displaystyle\quad\mid\boldsymbol{\mathtt{case}}E^{0}NP\mid\pi_{1}E^{0}\mid\pi_{2}E^{0}

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle::=\underline{a}\mid\lambda x.M\mid\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits V\mid\langle V,V\rangle\mid\iota_{1}V\mid\iota_{2}V\mid\underline{f}\;\underline{a}_{1}\;\cdots\;\underline{a}_{i}\quad(i<\alpha(\underline{f}))

\displaystyle V^{0}

\displaystyle::=\underline{a}\mid\lambda x.M\mid\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits M\mid\langle M,N\rangle\mid\iota_{1}M\mid\iota_{2}M\mid\underline{f}\;\underline{a}_{1}\;\cdots\;\underline{a}_{i}\quad(i<\alpha(\underline{f}))

where
\underline{a}
ranges over the zero-ary constants of basic
types,
N
,
P
range over generalized ISPCF terms, and

r\in\mathbb{R}
. The evaluation context
[]
is the
hole
.

Evaluation contexts, weak evaluation contexts, values and lazy values
are typed, although we will often omit mentioning it. The typing
rules are as follows. Every value of the form
\underline{\mathbf{a}}

with
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
is of type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
, and the type of the
other values is their type as generalized ISCPF term. The types of
evaluation contexts
E
(resp.,
E^{0}
) are of the form

\sigma\vdash\tau
, in such a way that for every
M\colon\sigma
,

E[M]
is of type
\tau
. We omit the precise typing rules. The
notation
E[M]
stands for
E
where the hole
[]
is replaced by

M
.

Using this, we define the
raw
operational semantics of ISPCF as
the smallest binary relation
\to
between generalized ISPCF terms
satisfying the clauses of Figure
3
. The raw
semantics does not mention any probabilistic information. This is dealt
with by the following definition. We recall that

i\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
is the embedding
r\mapsto[r,r]
.

Definition 10.8
.

Assume that all constants in
\Sigma
are first-order constants.
The operational semantics of ISPCF is the map
Next\colon\Gamma\to\mathbf{V}\Gamma
defined by:

•

for every instance of a rule
L\to R
of
Figure
3

except (
7
), (
8
) and (
9
),

Next(L)=\delta_{R}
;

•

(case of
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}
)
Next(E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]])
is the
continuous valuation
f_{E^{0}}[\mu]
, where

f_{E^{0}}(a)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{a}]
for every

a\in\mathbb{R}
;

•

(case of
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}
)

Next(E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a}}])\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}|\mathbf{a}|.\delta_{E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}]}
;

•

(case of
\underline{f}
) Given

k=\alpha(\underline{f})\neq 0
,

Next(E[\underline{f}\;\underline{a}_{1}\cdots\underline{a}_{k}])\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\delta_{E[\underline{f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})}]}
if

f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
is observable, the constant zero valuation
otherwise.

We let the reader check that every generalized ISPCF term parses in at
most one way as the left-hand side of a rule of
Figure
3
, so that Definition
10.8
is
non-ambiguous. To see this, one can write any generalized ISPCF term
as
E[M_{1}]
, where
E
has maximal depth, in a unique way. If

M_{1}=\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]
, then only rule (
7
) applies,
and only if
E
is weak. If
M_{1}=\underline{\mathbf{a}}
, then
only rule (
8
) applies, and only if
E
is of the form

E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}[]]
. If
M_{1}=\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits M
and

E=E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x\leftarrow[]};N]
, then only the third rule
applies. And so on, and we see that all the cases are mutually
exclusive.

Proposition 10.9
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of strictly observable first-order constants. The map

Next
is Scott-continuous from
\Gamma
to
\mathbf{V}\Gamma
.

Proof.

Let
L
be any generalized ISPCF term,
\mathcal{V}
be any
Scott-open subset of
\mathbf{V}\Gamma
, and let us assume that

Next(L)\in\mathcal{V}
. We will show that there is a Scott-open
neighborhood
U
of
L
such that

U\subseteq Next^{-1}(\mathcal{V})
.
U
will consist of terms of
the same shape
P
as
L
.

If
L
is of the form
E[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}\;M]
, then
Next(L)=\delta_{R}

where
R\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}E[M(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}\;M)]
is computed as follows: writing

L
as
P\theta
for some unique configuration
(P,\theta)
,
R

is obtained as
Q\theta^{\prime}
for some unique configuration

(Q,\theta^{\prime})
, where
Q
is obtained from
P
alone (independently
of
\theta
), and
\theta^{\prime}=\theta\circ\sigma
for some function

\sigma\colon\mathrm{fv}(Q)\to\mathrm{fv}(P)
that is determined from
P
alone,
again. For example, if

L=\underline{1.0}+\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}(\lambda x.x\times\underline{2.0})(\underline{3.0})
, then

P=x_{1}+\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}(\lambda x.x\times x_{2})(x_{3})
,

Q=x_{1}+(\lambda x.x\times x_{2})(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}(\lambda x.x\times x_{3}))(x_{4})
, and
\sigma
maps
x_{1}
to
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
and
x_{3}
to
x_{2}
,
and
x_{4}
to
x_{3}
.

Since
\delta_{R}
is in
\mathcal{V}
,
R
is in

V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\eta_{\Gamma}^{-1}(\mathcal{V})
, and
\theta^{\prime}
is in the
open subset
\{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{fv}(Q)}\mid Q\theta^{\prime}\in V\}
.
The map
S\colon\theta\mapsto\theta\circ\sigma
is clearly
Scott-continuous. It follows that

U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{P\theta\mid\theta\in S^{-1}(\{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{fv}(Q)}\mid Q\theta^{\prime}\in V\})\}
is Scott-open in
\Gamma
.
Moreover, by definition, for every generalized ISPCF term
P\theta

in
U
,
Next(P)
is in
\mathcal{V}
.

We reason similarly for all other rules except (
7
),
(
8
), and (
9
). The case of (
9
)
is not that different. In the final step,
U
is equal to the
intersection of

\{P\theta\mid\theta\in S^{-1}(\{\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{fv}(Q)}\mid Q\theta^{\prime}\in V\})\}
with the set
U^{\prime}
of generalized ISPCF terms
of shape
P
on which rule (
9
) applies; then we will be
able to conclude as above, once we prove that
U^{\prime}
is open. In
order to do so, and since we are in the case of (
9
),
P

is of the form
E[\underline{f}\;M_{1}\cdots M_{k}]
, where each
M_{i}

is either a constant (if its type is not
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
) or a template
variable. Let
x_{j+1}
, …,
x_{j+p}
be the template
variables occurring among the terms
M_{i}
, listed from left to
right, and say that
x_{j+1}=M_{i_{1}}
, …,

x_{j+p}=M_{i_{p}}
, where
1\leq i_{1}<\cdots i_{p}\leq k
. Let
also
M_{i}
be the constant
\underline{a}_{i}
for each
i
not among

i_{1}
, …,
i_{p}
. Then

U^{\prime}=\{P\theta\mid\theta\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{fv}(P)},f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{i_{1}-1})(\theta(x_{j+1}))(a_{i_{1}+1})\cdots(a_{i_{2}}-1)(\theta(x_{j+2}))(a_{i_{2}+1})\cdots(a_{i_{p}-1})(\theta(x_{k+p}))(a_{i_{p}+1})\cdots(a_{k})\in Obs_{\beta}\}
, where
\beta
is the
(observable) type of
f\;M_{1}\cdots M_{k}
, and
Obs_{\beta}
is the
subset of observable elements of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
. It is easy to see
that
Obs_{\beta}
is Scott-open. Since
f
is Scott-continuous,
U^{\prime}

is open.

In the case of (
8
),

L=E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a}}]
. We write
L
as

P\theta
for some unique configuration
(P,\theta)
, once again.
Then

Next(L)=|\mathbf{a}|.\delta_{E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}]}

where
E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}]
can be written as

Q\theta^{\prime}
for some configuration
(Q,\theta^{\prime})
. Once again,
Q

is determined as a function of
P
alone. In fact, if
\mathbf{a}

is the
m
th occurrence of a constant of type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
in
L
(so
that
\theta(x_{m})=\mathbf{a}
; recall that
x_{m}
is the
m
th
template variable), and
\mathrm{fv}(P)=\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}\}
with

n\geq m
, then
\mathrm{fv}(Q)=\{x_{1},\cdots,x_{n-1}\}
, and

\theta^{\prime}=\theta\circ\sigma
, where
\sigma
maps
x_{1}
to
x_{1}
,
…,
x_{m-1}
to
x_{m-1}
, and
x_{m}
to
x_{m+1}
, …,

x_{n-1}
to
x_{n}
. The map
\mathbf{a}\mapsto|\mathbf{a}|
is
Scott-continuous from
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Product is a
Scott-continuous map on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, from which we obtain easily that

(a,\nu)\mapsto a\nu
is Scott-continuous from

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times\mathbf{V}\Gamma
to
\mathbf{V}\Gamma
. Using that

\eta_{\Gamma}
is also Scott-continuous, the map

(\mathbf{a},\theta^{\prime})\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}\times\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{fv}(Q)}\mapsto|\mathbf{a}|.\delta_{Q\theta^{\prime}}
is Scott-continuous, and therefore

S\colon\theta\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{fv}(P)}\mapsto|\theta(x_{m})|.\delta_{Q(\theta\circ\sigma)}
is also Scott-continuous. We
then define
U
as

\{P\theta\mid\theta\in S^{-1}(\mathcal{V})\}
. This is open,
contains
L
, and its image by
Next
in included in
\mathcal{V}
by
definition.

In the case of (
7
), finally,

L=E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]]
. This time,
L=P\theta
for some unique
configuration
(P,\theta)
. For every
a\in\mathbb{R}
,

f_{E^{0}}(a)=E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{a}]
can be written as

Q\theta^{\prime}(a)
, where
(Q,\theta^{\prime}(a))
is a configuration such
that
Q
is obtained from
P
alone, as in previous cases.
Additionally, there is a number
m
(the number of the template
variable that is replaced by
\underline{a}
in
Q
), the template
variables of
P
are
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
with
n\geq m
, the
template variables of
Q
are
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n+1}
, and
\theta^{\prime}

is obtained as
S(\theta,i(a))
, where the map
S
is defined by:
for all
\theta\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{fv}(P)}
and
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
,

S(\theta,\mathbf{a})
maps
x_{m}
to
\underline{\mathbf{a}}
,
every
x_{i}
with
i<m
to
\theta(x_{i})
, and every
x_{i}
with

i>m
to
\theta(x_{i-1})
.
S
is clearly Scott-continuous. In
particular,
f_{E^{0}}=Q\;S(\theta,i(\_))
is lower semicontinuous,
so
f_{E^{0}}[\mu]
makes sense. Moreover, the map

\theta\mapsto f_{E^{0}}(a)=Q\;S(\theta,i(a))
is also
Scott-continuous for every
a\in\mathbb{R}
, so the map that sends
every
\theta
to

f_{E^{0}}[\mu]=\lambda V\in{\mathcal{O}}\Gamma.\mu\{a\in\mathbb{R}\mid Q\;S(\theta,i(a))\in V\}
is also Scott-continuous. Therefore

U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\{P\theta\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{fv}(P)}\mid f_{E^{0}}[\mu]\in\mathcal{V}\}
is open. By construction,
L
is in
U
, and every element of

U
maps to an element of
\mathcal{V}
by
Next
.
∎

As a corollary,
Next
is Borel measurable from
\Gamma
to
\mathbf{V}\Gamma
, and is therefore a
kernel
in the sense of

[
Vákár et al., 2019
]
; not just a measurable kernel, but a continuous kernel.

10.1
Soundness

Definition 10.10

(Redex, blocked term, normal form)
.

A
redex
is a generalized ISPCF term that is of any of the ten
forms found at the left of the rules of
Figure
3
. A
blocked term
is a
generalized ISPCF term of the form

E[\underline{f}\;\underline{a_{1}}\cdots\underline{a_{k}}]
where

E
is an evaluation context and
f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
is not
observable. A
normal form
is a generalized ISPCF term that
is neither a redex nor a blocked term.

All three categories are disjoint. For every blocked term or normal
form
L
,
Next(L)
is the zero valuation.

We will also need the following notions.

Definition 10.11

(Weak reduction, weak normal forms)
.

A
weak
instance of a rule of Figure
3
is
one where
E
, not just
E^{0}
, is a weak evaluation context. A

weak redex
is a left-hand side of a weak instance of a rule.
A
weak blocked term
is a generalized ISPCF term of the form

E^{0}[\underline{f}\;\underline{a_{1}}\cdots\underline{a_{k}}]

where
E^{0}
is a weak evaluation context and
f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})

is not observable. A
weak normal form
is a generalized ISPCF
term that is neither a weak redex nor a weak blocked term.

Lemma 10.12
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of strictly observable first-order constants.
The weak normal forms are exactly the lazy values.

Proof.

It is clear that every lazy value is in weak normal form.
Conversely, we show that every weak normal form
M
is a lazy value

V^{0}
, by induction on the size of
M
.

We start with the case where
M
is a constant
\underline{f}
. If

\alpha(\underline{f})=0
, then
M
is a zero-ary constant of
observable type. By the last condition of
Definition
10.6
, it is of basic type, and
therefore it is a lazy value. If
\alpha(\underline{f})\neq 0
,
then it is a lazy value of the form

\underline{f}\;V_{1}\;\cdots\;V_{i}
, with

i<\alpha(\underline{f})
(namely with
i=0
).

If
M
is of the form
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;N
, then
N
is weakly normal, since

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}[]
is a weak evaluation context and
M
is weakly normal.
We apply the induction hypothesis to
N
, and we realize that
because of typing,
N
must be of the form
\underline{\mathbf{a}}

for some zero-ary constant
\underline{\mathbf{a}}
of type

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
. However,
M=\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a}}
is not
weakly normal, so
M
cannot be of the form
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;N
after all.

M
cannot be of the form
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}\;N
or
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]
, which are
not weakly normal. Lambda-abstractions, and terms of the form

\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits N
,
\langle N,P\rangle
,
\iota_{1}N
, or
\iota_{2}N
are
all lazy values.

If
M
is of the form
\pi_{1}N
, then
N
is weakly normal, so
by induction hypothesis and typing,
N
is a pair, which would
contradict the normality of
M
; similarly if
M=\pi_{2}N
. If
M

is of the form
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{case}}NPQ
, then
N
is weakly normal, so by
induction hypothesis and typing it is of the form
\iota_{1}N_{1}
or

\iota_{2}N_{2}
, and that would contradict the normality of
M
as well.

If
M
is of the form
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x\leftarrow N};P
, then by induction
hypothesis and typing
N
is of the form
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits P
, which would
contradict the fact that
M
is weakly normal.

If
M
is an application
NP
, then by induction hypothesis and
typing, either
N
is a
\lambda
-abstraction (which is impossible
since
M
is weakly normal), or
N
is of the form

\underline{f}\;M_{1}\;\cdots\;M_{i}
with
i<\alpha(\underline{f})

and where
M_{1}
, …,
M_{i}
are weakly normal. Then

M=\underline{f}\;M_{1}\;\cdots\;M_{i}P
, where
M_{1}
, …,

M_{i}
and
P
are weakly normal, hence are lazy values. By the
first condition of Definition
10.6
, they are lazy
values of basic types, hence are constants of arity
0
. If

i+1=\alpha(\underline{f})
, either rule (
9
) would
apply or
M
would be blocked. Hence
i+1<\alpha(\underline{f})
,
showing that
M
is a lazy value.
∎

Let
Norm_{\tau}
be the set of normal forms of type
\tau
.

Lemma 10.13
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of strictly observable first-order constants.
For every type
\tau
,
Norm_{\tau}
is exactly the set of values of
type
\tau
.

Proof.

It is clear that every value is in normal form. Conversely, we
argue that every element
M
of
Norm_{\tau}
is a value, by induction
on
M
. Every normal form is weakly normal, since every weak
evaluation context
E^{0}
is a context
E
; so
M
is a lazy value.
In order to show that it is a value, we only need to show that if it
is of the form
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits N
,
\langle N,P\rangle
,
\iota_{1}N
or

\iota_{2}N
, then
N
and
P
are values. If
M=\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits N
, this
is because
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits[]
is an evaluation context, so that
N
is a
value by induction hypothesis. Similarly when
M=\iota_{1}N
or

M=\iota_{2}N
. When
M=\langle N,P\rangle
, since

\langle[],P\rangle
is an evaluation context, by induction
hypothesis
N
is a value
V
. Since
\langle V,[]\rangle
is a
context,
P
is a value.
∎

The following lemma will apply to
\kappa\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}Next
, where

X\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\Gamma
, and where
Norm
is the set of normal forms. For
every
x\in X
, for every open subset
U
of
Norm
,

Next^{\leq n}(x)(U)
is the ‘probability’ that we will reach
U
,
starting from
x
, in at most
n

Next
steps, and
Next^{*}(x)(U)

is the ‘probability’ that we will reach
U
starting from
x
in any
number of steps. (The quotes around ‘probability’ reflect the fact
that those probabilities need not be bounded by
1
.) This way of
defining ‘probabilities’ of reaching
U
is inspired from Lemma 3.9
and Equation (6) of
[
Ehrhard et al., 2018
]
. Let us also recall the
restriction
\mu_{|U}
of a continuous valuation to an open subset

U
, defined by
\mu_{|U}(V)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mu(U\cap V)
.

Lemma 10.14
.

Let
X
be a topological space, and let
Norm
and
\overline{Norm}

be two complementary open subsets of
X
. Let
\kappa\colon X\to\mathbf{V}X
be
a continuous map, and let us assume that for every
x\in Norm
,
\kappa(x)
is the zero valuation. We define:

•

\kappa^{\leq 0}
as mapping every
x\in Norm
to

\delta_{x}
, and every
x\in\overline{Norm}
to the zero valuation;

•

\kappa^{\leq 1}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\kappa+\kappa^{\leq 0}
;

•

\kappa^{\leq n+1}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(\kappa^{\leq n})^{\dagger}\circ\kappa^{\leq 1}
, for every
n\geq 1
.

Then:

1.

the maps
\kappa^{\leq n}
are continuous from
X
to
\mathbf{V}X
;

2.

for every
x\in X
, the family

{(\kappa^{\leq n}(x)_{|Norm})}_{n\geq 1}
is monotonically
increasing;

3.

the formula

\kappa^{*}(x)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\sup_{n\geq 1}\kappa^{\leq n}(x)_{|Norm}
defines a
continuous map
\kappa^{*}
from
X
to
\mathbf{V}X
.

Proof.

1. Since
X
is homeomorphic to the topological coproduct
\overline{Norm}+Norm
, the continuity of
\kappa^{\leq 0}
reduces to that
the unit
\eta_{Norm}
and of the constant zero map. Then

\kappa^{\leq 1}
is continuous since
+
is Scott-continuous on

\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and by induction on
n\geq 1
,
\kappa^{\leq n+1}
is
continuous as a composition of continuous maps.

2. We show that
\kappa^{\leq n}(x)(U)\leq\kappa^{\leq n+1}(x)(U)
for every
x\in X
and every open subset
U
of

Norm
by induction on
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

In the base case
n=0
, this is obvious. Otherwise,
n\geq 1
, and

\kappa^{\leq n+1}(x)(U)=\int_{y\in X}\kappa^{\leq n}(y)(U)d\kappa^{\leq 1}(x)
. For every
y\in X
,

\kappa^{\leq n}(y)(U)\geq\kappa^{\leq n-1}(y)(U)
by
induction hypothesis, so
\kappa^{\leq n+1}(x)(U)\geq\int_{y\in X}\kappa^{\leq n-1}(y)(U)d\kappa^{\leq 1}(x)=\kappa^{\leq n}(x)(U)
.

3.
\mathbf{V}X
is a dcpo, and suprema in the dcpo of continuous maps
from
X
to
\mathbf{V}X
are computed pointwise.
∎

From now on, we let
Norm
be the subset of configurations

(M,\theta)
such that
M\theta
is a normal form. Being a normal
form is a property of shapes, namely any two generalized ISPCF terms
with the same shape will both be normal, or neither of them will
be. It follows that
Norm
and its complement
\overline{Norm}
are
open in
\Gamma
. Let us give another characterization of

Next^{\leq n}
.

Lemma 10.15
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of strictly observable first-order constants. For every
generalized ISPCF term
M
, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we have:

1.

if
M\in Norm
, then
Next^{\leq n}(M)=\delta_{M}
;

2.

if
M=E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]]
, and
n\geq 1
, then for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}\Gamma
,

Next^{\leq n}(M)(U)=\int_{a\in\mathbb{R}}Next^{\leq n-1}(E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{a}])(U)d\mu
;

3.

if
M=E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a}}]
and
n\geq 1
,
then

Next^{\leq n}(M)=|\mathbf{a}|.Next^{\leq n-1}(E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}])
;

4.

if

M=E[\underline{f}\;\underline{a_{1}}\cdots\underline{a_{k}}]

and
n\geq 1
, then
Next^{\leq n}(M)
is equal to

Next^{\leq n-1}\allowbreak(E[\underline{f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})}])
if
f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
is observable, to the zero
valuation otherwise;

5.

for every instance of a rule
L\to R
of
Figure
3
except (
7
),
(
8
) and (
9
), if
n\geq 1
then

Next^{\leq n}(L)=Next^{\leq n-1}(R)
.

Proof.

1. By induction on
n
. If
n=0
, then
Next^{\leq 0}(M)=\delta_{M}
since
M\in Norm
. If
n=1
, then
Next(M)=0
, so

Next^{\leq 1}(M)=Next(M)+Next^{\leq 0}(M)=\delta_{M}
. If

n\geq 2
, then

Next^{\leq n}(M)=(Next^{\leq n-1})^{\dagger}(Next^{\leq 1}(M))=(Next^{\leq n-1})^{\dagger}(\delta_{M})=Next^{\leq n-1}(M)=\delta_{M}
, by induction hypothesis.

2. Since
M=E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]]\in\overline{Norm}
,

Next^{\leq 1}(M)=Next(M)
is equal to
f_{E^{0}}[\mu]
, where

f_{E^{0}}(a)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{a}]
for every

a\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore

Next^{\leq n}(M)(U)=(Next^{\leq n-1})^{\dagger}(f_{E^{0}}[\mu])(U)=\int_{N\in\Gamma}Next^{\leq n-1}(N)(U)df_{E^{0}}[\mu]
.
By the change-of-variables formula, this is equal to

\int_{a\in\mathbb{R}}Next^{\leq n-1}(f_{E^{0}}(a))\allowbreak(U)d\mu
, hence to

\int_{a\in\mathbb{R}}Next^{\leq n-1}(E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{a}])(U)d\mu
.

3. Since

M=E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a}}]\in\overline{Norm}
,

Next^{\leq 1}(M)=Next(M)=|\mathbf{a}|.\allowbreak\delta_{E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}]}
. Then, for every

U\in{\mathcal{O}}\Gamma
,

Next^{\leq n}(M)(U)=(Next^{\leq n-1})^{\dagger}(|\mathbf{a}|.\allowbreak\delta_{E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}]})(U)=\int_{N\in\Gamma}Next^{\leq n-1}(N)(U)d|\mathbf{a}|.\allowbreak\delta_{E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}]}
, and this is equal to
|\mathbf{a}|.Next^{\leq n-1}\allowbreak(E^{0}\allowbreak[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}])(U)
.

4. Let

M=E[\underline{f}\;\underline{a_{1}}\cdots\underline{a_{k}}]
. If
f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
is not observable, then
M
is
blocked, so
Next^{\leq 1}(M)=0
. For every
U\in{\mathcal{O}}\Gamma
,

Next^{\leq n}(M)(U)=(Next^{\leq n-1})^{\dagger}(0)=\int_{N\in\Gamma}Next^{\leq n-1}(N)(U)d0=0
, so

Next^{\leq n}(M)=0
.

If
f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
is observable, then

Next^{\leq 1}(M)=Next(M)=\delta_{E[\underline{f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})}]}
. It follows that

Next^{\leq n}(M)=(Next^{\leq n-1})^{\dagger}(\eta_{\Gamma}(E[\underline{f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})}]))=Next^{\leq n-1}\allowbreak(E[\underline{f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})}])
.

5. This is similar to the latter case.
∎

We extend the denotational semantics of Figure
2
to
generalized ISPCF terms by positing:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}\right\rrbracket\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathbf{a},

for every
\mathbf{a}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
. We note that
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho
is
independent of
\rho
. In the sequel, we will therefore simply write

\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket
for the semantics of the generalized ISPCF term
M
,
disregarding the useless
\rho
. Alternatively, every generalized
ISPCF term
M
can be written as
P\theta
for some unique configuration

(P,\theta)
, and
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket
is also equal to
\left\llbracket{P}\right\rrbracket\theta
,
reading
\theta
as an environment. The Scott-continuity of
\left\llbracket{P}\right\rrbracket

immediately entails the following.

Lemma 10.16
.

Let
\Gamma_{\tau}
be the subspace of
\Gamma
consisting of
generalized ISPCF terms of type
\tau
.
The map
\left\llbracket{\_}\right\rrbracket\colon\Gamma_{\tau}\to\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
is
Scott-continuous.

It follows that, given any Scott-open subset
U
of
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
, for
any type
\tau
, the set
\overline{U}
of normal forms
M
of type

\tau
such that
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\in U
is Scott-open in
\Gamma_{\tau}
.
We make that into a definition, and we also introduce another open set

\overline{\overline{U}}
.

Definition 10.17

(The open sets
\overline{U}
,
\overline{\overline{U}}
)
.

For every type
\tau
, for every Scott-open subset
U
of

\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
, let
\overline{U}
be the Scott-open set of normal forms

V
of type
\tau
such that
\left\llbracket{V}\right\rrbracket\in U
, and
\overline{\overline{U}}
be the
Scott-open set of terms of the form
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits V
with

V\in\overline{U}
.

It is clear that
\overline{\overline{U}}
is Scott-open: by definition of the
topology of
\Gamma
, the map
M\mapsto\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits M
is a homeomorphism
of
\Gamma
onto the subspace of those configurations starting with

\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits
.

Lemma 10.18
.

The only weak evaluation contexts
E^{0}
whose type is of the
form
D\sigma\vdash\tau
are those of the form:

\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits x_{n}\leftarrow(\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits x_{n-1}\leftarrow\cdots(\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits x_{1}\leftarrow[];\allowbreak N_{1});\cdots;N_{n-1});N_{n},

and then
\tau
is a distribution type
D\tau^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By induction on the size of
E^{0}
. This is obvious if
E^{0}=[]
,
otherwise we see that
E^{0}
must be of the form

{E^{\prime}}^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x\leftarrow[]};N]
, by inspection of the
evaluation context formation rules, using typing to rule out all the
other possibilities.
∎

The following linearity property is crucial for soundness. The
importance of such linearity properties have already been made in

[
Goubault-Larrecq, 2019
, Proposition 5.1]
, where probabilistic choice was
discrete, and in
[
Ehrhard et al., 2018
, Lemma 7.6]
. Given any evaluation
context
E
, we write
\left\llbracket{E}\right\rrbracket
for
\left\llbracket{\lambda x.E[x]}\right\rrbracket
.

Lemma 10.19
.

Assume that all constants in
\Sigma
are first-order constants.
For every weak evaluation context
E^{0}
of type

D\sigma\vdash D\tau
,
\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket
is
linear
:

1.

for all
\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in\mathbf{V}{\left\llbracket{\sigma}\right\rrbracket}
, for every

a\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket(a.\mu)=a\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket(\mu)
and

\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket(\mu+\mu^{\prime})=\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket(\mu)+\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket(\mu^{\prime})
;

2.

for every dcpo
X
, for every Scott-continuous map

f\colon X\to\mathbf{V}{\left\llbracket{\sigma}\right\rrbracket}
,

\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket\circ f^{\dagger}=(\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket\circ f)^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
10.18
,
E^{0}
is of the form

\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits x_{n}\leftarrow(\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits x_{n-1}\leftarrow\cdots(\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits x_{1}\leftarrow[];\allowbreak N_{1});\cdots;N_{n-1});N_{n}
. We prove
both claims by induction on
n
, the
length
of
E^{0}
.

1. If
n=0
, this is obvious. Otherwise, we can write
E^{0}
as

\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{n}\leftarrow{E^{\prime}}^{0}};N_{n}
where
{E^{\prime}}^{0}
has length

n-1
. We note that, for every continuous map

f\colon X\to\mathbf{V}Y
,
f^{\dagger}
is linear, in the sense that

f^{\dagger}(a.\mu)=a.f^{\dagger}(\mu)
and

f^{\dagger}(\mu+\mu^{\prime})=f^{\dagger}(\mu)+f^{\dagger}(\mu^{\prime})
. This
is clear from the definition of
f^{\dagger}
, see formula
(
1
). Then:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket(a.\mu)

\displaystyle=(\left\llbracket{\lambda x_{n}.N_{n}}\right\rrbracket)^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket{{E^{\prime}}^{0}}\right\rrbracket(a.\mu))

\displaystyle=(\left\llbracket{\lambda x_{n}.N_{n}}\right\rrbracket)^{\dagger}(a.\left\llbracket{{E^{\prime}}^{0}}\right\rrbracket(\mu))

by induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=a.(\left\llbracket{\lambda x_{n}.N_{n}}\right\rrbracket)^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket{{E^{\prime}}^{0}}\right\rrbracket(\mu))=a.\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket(\mu)\mskip-40.0mu

and similarly for
\left\llbracket{E}\right\rrbracket(\mu+\mu^{\prime})
.

2. Again, this is clear when
n=0
. When
n\geq 1
, we have:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket\circ f^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=(\left\llbracket{\lambda x_{n}.N_{n}}\right\rrbracket)^{\dagger}\circ\left\llbracket{{E^{\prime}}^{0}}\right\rrbracket\circ f^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=(\left\llbracket{\lambda x_{n}.N_{n}}\right\rrbracket)^{\dagger}\circ\left(\left\llbracket{{E^{\prime}}^{0}}\right\rrbracket\circ f\right)^{\dagger}

by induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=\left((\left\llbracket{\lambda x_{n}.N_{n}}\right\rrbracket)^{\dagger}\circ\left\llbracket{{E^{\prime}}^{0}}\right\rrbracket\circ f\right)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\text{by the equation }(g^{\dagger}\circ f)^{\dagger}=g^{\dagger}\circ f^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=(\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket\circ f)^{\dagger}.

∎

Proposition 10.20

(Soundness)
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of strictly observable first-order constants. For every
generalized ISPCF term
M\colon D\tau
, where
\tau
is any type,
for every open subset
U
of
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
,

\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(U)\geq Next^{*}(M)(\overline{\overline{U}})
.

Proof.

We show that
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho(U)\geq Next^{\leq n}(M)(\overline{\overline{U}})

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
. If
M\in Norm
, then

Next^{\leq n}(M)=\delta_{M}
by Lemma
10.15
, item 1.
By Lemma
10.13
(and typing),
M=\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits V
for some
value
V
of type
\tau
. Hence
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(U)=\delta_{\left\llbracket{V}\right\rrbracket}(U)
,
which is equal to
1
if
\left\llbracket{V}\right\rrbracket\in U
, and to
0
otherwise.
Since
\left\llbracket{V}\right\rrbracket\in U
if and only if
V\in\overline{U}
if and only
if
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits V\in\overline{\overline{U}}
,
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(U)=Next^{\leq n}(M)(\overline{\overline{U}})
.

Henceforth, we assume that
M
is not normal, and we prove the claim
by induction on
n
.
If
n=0
, then
Next^{\leq 0}(M)=0
, so the claim is clear. Let
therefore
n\geq 1
, and let us look at the possible shapes of
M
.

IF
M=E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]]
, then:

\displaystyle Next^{\leq n}(M)(\overline{\overline{U}})

\displaystyle=\int_{a\in\mathbb{R}}Next^{\leq n-1}(E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{a}])(\overline{\overline{U}})d\mu

by Lemma
10.15
, item 2

\displaystyle\leq\int_{a\in\mathbb{R}}\left\llbracket{E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{a}]}\right\rrbracket(U)d\mu

by induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}}\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket(\eta_{\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket}(\mathbf{a}))(U)di[\mu]

by the change-of-variables formula

\displaystyle=(\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket\circ\eta_{\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket})^{\dagger}(i[\mu])(U)

\displaystyle\text{by definition of }^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket(\eta_{\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\right\rrbracket}^{\dagger}(i[\mu]))(U)

by Lemma
10.19
, item 2

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket(i[\mu])(U)

since
\eta_{X}^{\dagger}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbf{V}X}

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket{{E^{0}}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]]}\right\rrbracket(U)=\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(U).

If
M=E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a}}]
, then:

\displaystyle Next^{\leq n}(M)(\overline{\overline{U}})

\displaystyle=|\mathbf{a}|.Next^{\leq n-1}(E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}])(\overline{\overline{U}})d\mu

by Lemma
10.15
, item 3

\displaystyle\leq|\mathbf{a}|.\left\llbracket{E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}]}\right\rrbracket(U)

by induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=|\mathbf{a}|.\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket(\delta_{*})(U)

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket{E^{0}}\right\rrbracket(|\mathbf{a}|.\delta_{*})(U)

by Lemma
10.19
, item 1

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket{E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;{\underline{\mathbf{a}}}]}\right\rrbracket(U)=\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(U).

If

M=E[\underline{f}\;\underline{a_{1}}\cdots\underline{a_{k}}]
,
then we consider two cases. If
f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
is not
observable, then
Next^{\leq n}(M)(\overline{\overline{U}})=0
by
Lemma
10.15
, item 4, and the claim is clear. If

f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
is observable, then:

\displaystyle Next^{\leq n}(M)(\overline{\overline{U}})

\displaystyle=Next^{\leq n-1}(E[\underline{f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})}])(\overline{\overline{U}})

by Lemma
10.15
, item 4

\displaystyle\leq\left\llbracket{E[\underline{f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})}]}\right\rrbracket(U)

by induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket{E[\underline{f}\;\underline{a_{1}}\cdots\underline{a_{k}}]}\right\rrbracket(U)=\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(U).

Finally, if
M
an instance
L
of a rule
L\to R
of
Figure
3
except (
7
),
(
8
) and (
9
), then by inspection

\left\llbracket{L}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket{R}\right\rrbracket
, so:

\displaystyle Next^{\leq n}(M)(\overline{\overline{U}})

\displaystyle=Next^{\leq n-1}(R)(\overline{\overline{U}})

by Lemma
10.15
, item 5

\displaystyle\leq\left\llbracket{R}\right\rrbracket(U)

by induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=\left\llbracket{L}\right\rrbracket(U)=\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(U).

∎

10.2
Adequacy

Adequacy means that the inequality of Proposition
10.20
can
be reinforced to an equality, provided that
\tau
is an observable
type.

We recall that the
deterministic rules
are all those of
Figure
3
except the first three, and that the

weak rules
are those where the evaluation context is weak.

Lemma 10.21
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of strictly observable first-order constants. For every
string of rewrite steps

M=M_{0}\to M_{1}\to\cdots\to M_{n}\to\cdots
by deterministic
rules,
Next^{*}(M)=Next^{*}(M_{i})
for every
i
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that if
M\to N
by some deterministic rule,
then
Next^{*}(M)=Next^{*}(N)
. This follows from the fact that,
for every
n\geq 1
,
Next^{\leq n}(M)=Next^{\leq n-1}(N)

(Lemma
10.15
, items 4 and 5), and by taking suprema over

n
.
∎

We say that
M\to^{*}N

by deterministic rules
(resp.,
by
weak deterministic rules
) if and only if one can connect
M
to
N

by a finite string of instances of deterministic rules (resp., weak
deterministic rules).

Lemma 10.22
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of strictly observable first-order constants.

1.

For every generalized ISPCF term
M
of type
\tau
, there is
at most one maximal string of instances of weak deterministic
rules
M_{0}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}M\to M_{1}\to\cdots\to\cdots M_{n}\cdots
.
Every
M_{i}
has type
\tau
. If
\tau
is an observable type, and
if that string stops at rank
n
, then
M_{n}
is a weak value

V^{0}
.

2.

For every generalized ISPCF term
M
of type
\tau
, there is
at most one maximal string of instances of deterministic rules

M_{0}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}M\to M_{1}\to\cdots\to\cdots M_{n}\cdots
. Every

M_{i}
has type
\tau
. If
\tau
is an observable type, either
that string stops at rank
n
, then
M_{n}
is a value
V
, and

Next^{*}(M)=\delta_{V}
; or else
Next^{*}(M)=0
.

Proof.

The unicity of any maximal string of (weak) deterministic rules
starting from
M
is clear, as well as the fact that every
M_{i}
has
type
\tau
. We now assume that
\tau
is an observable type.

1. We consider a maximal string of weak deterministic rules stopping
at rank
n
, namely
M=M_{0}\to^{*}M_{n}
. We claim that
M_{n}
is
weakly normal. If
M_{n}
is of the form
E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]]
or

E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a}}]
or

E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x\leftarrow\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits M};N]
, then
E^{0}
has a type of
the form
D\sigma\vdash\tau
. By Lemma
10.18
, that
would imply that
\tau
is of the form
D\tau^{\prime}
for some type

\tau^{\prime}
, which is impossible since
\tau
is observable. Since no
weak deterministic rule applies to
M_{n}
, no weak rule at all applies, so

M_{n}
is weakly normal. By Lemma
10.13
, it is a weak
value
V^{0}
.

2. We reason similarly, assuming a maximal string of deterministic
rules stopping at rank
n
,
M=M_{0}\to^{*}M_{n}
. Then
M_{n}
is a
value
V
. By Lemma
10.21
,

Next^{*}(M)=Next^{*}(M_{n})=Next^{*}(V)
, and this is equal to

\delta_{V}
by Lemma
10.15
, item 1.

If instead the maximal string
M=M_{0}\to M_{1}\to\cdots
does not
stop, then for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have

Next^{\leq m}(M)=Next^{\leq m-1}(M_{1})=\cdots=Next^{\leq 0}(M_{m})
, by Lemma
10.15
, items 4 and 5. Since
M_{m}
is
not normal, this is equal to
0
. Taking suprema over
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
we obtain that
Next^{*}(M)=0
.
∎

In order to establish adequacy, we will use a logical relation

{(\mathrel{R}_{\tau})}_{\tau\text{ type}}
in the same style as those used in

[
Goubault-Larrecq, 2015
,
Goubault-Larrecq, 2019
]
. Before we do this, we need the
following notion.

Definition 10.23

(Observable open set)
.

An
observable open subset
of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
is a Scott-open
subset of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
consisting of observable elements.

For each type
\tau
,
\mathrel{R}_{\tau}
will be
a binary relation between generalized ISPCF terms of type
\tau
and
elements of
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
. We define it with the help of auxiliary
relations
\mathrel{R}_{\tau\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}
. The latter relates
evaluation contexts of type
D\tau\vdash D\beta
and
Scott-continuous maps from
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
to
\left\llbracket{D\beta}\right\rrbracket
, where

\beta
is an observable type. We will use short phrases such as
“for all
E^{0}\mathrel{R}_{\tau\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}h
” instead of “for
every observable type
\beta
, for every weak evaluation context
E^{0}

of type
D\tau\vdash D\beta
, for every Scott-continuous map
h

from
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
to
\left\llbracket{D\beta}\right\rrbracket
, if

E^{0}\mathrel{R}_{\tau\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}h
then”.

Definition 10.24

(Logical relation,
\mathrel{R}_{\tau}
,
\mathrel{R}_{\tau\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}
)
.

We define:

•

for every
basic
type
\tau
,
M\mathrel{R}_{\tau}a
if and only
if either
a=\bot
, or
M\to^{*}\underline{b}
by weak
deterministic rules, for some zero-ary constant
\underline{b}

such that
b\geq a
.

•

M\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}}a
if and only if
a=\bot
, or

a=(a_{1},a_{2})
where

M\to^{*}\langle M_{1},M_{2}\rangle
by weak deterministic rules,
for some
M_{1}\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}}a_{1}
and
M_{2}\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{2}}a_{2}
.

•

M\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}}a
if and only if
a=\bot
, or

M\to^{*}\iota_{i}N
by weak deterministic rules and
a=(i,b)
,
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
and some
N\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{i}}b
with

b\neq\bot
.

•

M\mathrel{R}_{\sigma\to\tau}g
if and only if for all

N\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}a
,
MN\mathrel{R}_{\tau}g(a)
.

•

M\mathrel{R}_{D\tau}\mu
if and only if for all

E^{0}\mathrel{R}_{\tau\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}h
, for every observable open
subset
U
of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
,

Next^{*}(E^{0}[M])(\overline{\overline{U}})\geq h^{\dagger}(\mu)(U)
;

•

E^{0}\mathrel{R}_{\tau\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}h
if and only if for all

M\mathrel{R}_{\tau}a
, for every observable open subset
U
of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
,

Next^{*}(E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits M])(\overline{\overline{U}})\geq h(a)(U)
.

This is a well-founded definition by induction on the size of
\tau
.
Note that
\mathrel{R}_{D\tau}
is defined in terms of

\mathrel{R}_{\tau\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}
, which itself depends on
\mathrel{R}_{\tau}
;
there is no dependency on, say,
\mathrel{R}_{D\beta}
, which would make the
definition ill-founded.

Lemma 10.25
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of strictly observable first-order constants. Let
\tau
be
any type. If
M\to^{*}M^{\prime}
by weak deterministic rules, then

M\mathrel{R}_{\tau}a
if and only if
M^{\prime}\mathrel{R}_{\tau}a
.

Proof.

It suffices to show this when
M\to M^{\prime}
by one weak deterministic
rule, and we show this by induction on
\tau
.

When
\tau
is a basic type, and if
M^{\prime}\mathrel{R}_{\tau}a
, then either

a=\bot
, or
M^{\prime}\to^{*}\underline{b}
by weak deterministic rules,
with
b\geq a
. In the second case,
M\to M^{\prime}\to^{*}b
by weak
deterministic rules, so
M\mathrel{R}_{\tau}a
. In the reverse direction, if

M\mathrel{R}_{\tau}a
, either
a=\bot
, or
M\to^{*}\underline{b}
by
weak deterministic rules, with
b\geq a
. Since
M\to M^{\prime}
by a weak
deterministic rule, since
\underline{b}
is (weakly) normal, and
since weak deterministic rules are deterministic by construction,
the string of reduction steps
M\to^{*}\underline{b}
is of the form

M\to M^{\prime}\to^{*}\underline{b}
. Therefore
M\mathrel{R}_{\tau}a
.

The argument is similar
when
\tau
is a product type
\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}
.
The key is that, if
M\to^{*}\langle M_{1},M_{2}\rangle
by weak
deterministic rules, and if
M\to M^{\prime}
by a weak deterministic rule,
then
M
is not weakly normal, so the first string of rewrite steps
must be of the form
M\to M^{\prime}\to^{*}\langle M_{1},M_{2}\rangle
.
Hence, if
M\to M^{\prime}
by a weak deterministic rule, then

M\to^{*}\langle M_{1},M_{2}\rangle
by weak deterministic rules if
and only if
M^{\prime}\to^{*}\langle M_{1},M_{2}\rangle
by weak
deterministic rules. We reason similarly if
\tau
is a sum type

\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}
, where if
M\to M^{\prime}
by a weak deterministic rule,
then
M\to^{*}\iota_{i}N
by weak deterministic rules if and only if

M^{\prime}\to^{*}\iota_{i}N
by weak deterministic rules.

For arrow types
\sigma\to\tau
, we assume
M
and
M^{\prime}
of type

\sigma\to\tau
, such that
M\to M^{\prime}
by a weak deterministic
rule. Then, for every
N\colon\sigma
,
MN\to M^{\prime}N
by the same
weak deterministic rule. If
M\mathrel{R}_{\sigma\to\tau}f
, then for
all
N\mathrel{R}_{\tau}a
,
MN\mathrel{R}_{\tau}f(a)
, and since
MN\to M^{\prime}N
, by
induction hypothesis
M^{\prime}N\mathrel{R}_{\tau}f(a)
; therefore

M^{\prime}\mathrel{R}_{\sigma\to\tau}f
. The fact that

M^{\prime}\mathrel{R}_{\sigma\to\tau}f
implies
M\mathrel{R}_{\sigma\to\tau}f
is
similar.

For distribution types
\tau\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}D\sigma
, we use a variant of
that argument. We assume
M,M^{\prime}\colon D\sigma
such that

M\to M^{\prime}
by some weak deterministic rule. Then, for every weak
evaluation context
E^{0}
,
E^{0}[M]\to^{*}E^{0}[M^{\prime}]
by the same weak
deterministic rule. By Lemma
10.21
,

Next^{*}(E^{0}[M])=Next^{*}(E^{0}[M^{\prime}])
, from which it follows
immediately that
M\mathrel{R}_{D\sigma}\mu
if and only if
M^{\prime}\mathrel{R}_{D\sigma}\mu
, for any
\mu
.
∎

We let
M\mathrel{R}_{\tau}
abbreviate the set of all values
a\in\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket

such that
M\mathrel{R}_{\tau}a
.

Lemma 10.26
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of strictly observable first-order constants. For every
type
\tau
, for every generalized ISPCF term
M
of type
\tau
,

M\mathrel{R}_{\tau}
is a Scott-closed subset of
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
that contains

\bot
.

Proof.

By induction on
\tau
. That
M\mathrel{R}_{\tau}
contains
\bot
is clear
except perhaps when
\tau
is a distribution type
D\tau^{\prime}
. In
that case, the
\bot
element of
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
is the zero valuation

0
, and
h^{\dagger}(0)(U)
is equal to
0
for every

h\in\left\llbracket{\tau\vdash D\beta}\right\rrbracket
, which trivially entails the
statement.

If
\tau
is a basic type, then
M\mathrel{R}_{\tau}
is the closed set

\{\bot\}
if the maximal string of weak deterministic rewriting
steps starting from
M
does not stop, or
\mathop{\downarrow}b
if

M\to^{*}\underline{b}
by weak deterministic rules. Then,
\mathop{\downarrow}b

is closed because downward closures of points are closed.

In the case of product types, either
M\to^{*}V^{0}
by weak
deterministic rules for no lazy value
V^{0}
, and then

M\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}}
is equal to
\{\bot\}
; or

M\to^{*}V^{0}
for some uniquely determined lazy value
V^{0}
by
Lemma
10.22
, item 1. By typing and
Lemma
10.13
,
V^{0}=\langle M_{1},M_{2}\rangle
for some

M_{1}\colon\tau_{1}
and
M_{2}\colon\tau_{2}
. Then

M\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}}
is equal to

(M_{1}\mathrel{R}_{\sigma})\times(M_{2}\mathrel{R}_{\sigma})
union
\{\bot\}
. The
latter is a finite union of Scott-closed sets, hence is
Scott-closed.

In the case of sum types, we reason similarly. Either

M\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}}
is equal to
\{\bot\}
, or

M\to^{*}\iota_{i}N
by weak deterministic rules, and then

M\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}}
is the set of elements

\{\bot\}\cup\{(i,b)\mid b\in N\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{i}}\}
. It is easy to
see that the latter is downwards closed and closed under directed
suprema, hence is Scott-closed.

In the case of arrow types
\sigma\to\tau
,

M\mathrel{R}_{\sigma\to\tau}
is equal to the intersection over all

N\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}a
of the sets
\mathrm{App}(\_,a)^{-1}(MN\mathrel{R}_{\tau})
.
(
\mathrm{App}
is the application morphism
(g,a)\mapsto g(a)
.) This
set is closed because
\mathrm{App}
and therefore
\mathrm{App}(\_,a)
is
continuous for every
a
, and intersections of closed sets are closed.

In the case of distribution types
D\tau
,
M\mathrel{R}_{D\tau}
is the
intersection of the sets

{(h^{\dagger}(\_)(U))}^{-1}(\mathop{\downarrow}Next^{*}(E^{0}[M])(\overline{\overline{U}}))
over all

E^{0}\mathrel{R}_{\tau\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}h
and all observable open subsets
U

of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
. We conclude because downward closures of points
are closed, and since
h^{\dagger}
is Scott-continuous, hence also

h^{\dagger}(\_)(U)
for every
U
.
∎

The fundamental lemma of logical relations states that terms are
related to their semantics. We prove the variant suited to our case
below. Given an ISPCF term
M
, a
substitution

\theta
for

M
is a map from the free variables of
M
to generalized ISPCF terms
of the same type, and
M\theta
denotes the result of application

\theta
to
M
. Given a substitution
\theta
and an environment

\rho
with the same domain
D
, we write
\theta\mathrel{R}_{*}\rho
to mean
that
\theta(x_{\sigma})\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}\rho(x_{\sigma})
for every

x_{\sigma}\in D
.

Lemma 10.27
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of strictly observable first-order constants. For every
observable type
\beta
, for every observable element
a
of

\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
,
\underline{a}\mathrel{R}_{\beta}a
.

Proof.

We recall that
\underline{a}
was defined in
Definition
10.5
. We show this by induction on

\beta
. If
\beta
is a basic type, this follows from the fact
that
\underline{a}\to^{*}\underline{a}
(in
0
step) and

\left\llbracket{\underline{a}}\right\rrbracket=a
.
If
\beta
is a product type
\beta_{1}\times\beta_{2}
,
then
a
is of the form
(a_{1},a_{2})
with
a_{1}
observable in
\left\llbracket{\beta_{1}}\right\rrbracket
and
a_{2}
observable in
\left\llbracket{\beta_{2}}\right\rrbracket
. Then

\underline{a}\to^{*}\langle\underline{a}_{1},\underline{a}_{2}\rangle

(in
0
step), while
\underline{a}_{1}\mathrel{R}_{\beta_{1}}a_{1}
and

\underline{a}_{2}\mathrel{R}_{\beta_{2}}a_{2}
by induction hypothesis. The case
of sum types is similar.
∎

Proposition 10.28
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of strictly observable first-order constants.
For every ISPCF term
M\colon\tau
, for all
\theta\mathrel{R}_{*}\rho
,
M\theta\mathrel{R}_{\tau}\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho
.

Proof.

By induction on the size of
M
.

\bullet
If
M
is a constant
\underline{f}
, say of type

\sigma_{1}\to\cdots\to\sigma_{k}\to\tau
where every
\sigma_{i}
is
a basic type and
\tau
is an observable type, then we must show
that for all
N_{1}\mathrel{R}_{\sigma_{1}}a_{1}
, …,

N_{k}\mathrel{R}_{\sigma_{k}}a_{k}
,

\underline{f}\;N_{1}\cdots N_{k}\mathrel{R}_{\tau}f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
. If

f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})=\bot
, this is clear, so let us assume

f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})\neq\bot
. By the second condition of
Definition
10.6
,
f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
is
observable, and every
a_{i}
is different from
\bot
. Since every

\sigma_{i}
is a basic type, we have
N_{i}\to^{*}\underline{a^{\prime}}_{i}
by
weak deterministic rules for some zero-ary constant

\underline{a^{\prime}}_{i}
such that
a^{\prime}_{i}\geq a_{i}
, for every
i
with

1\leq i\leq k
. It is easy to see that

\underline{f}\;N_{1}\cdots N_{k}\to^{*}\underline{f}\;\underline{a^{\prime}}_{1}\;\cdots\;\underline{a^{\prime}}_{k}\to\underline{f(a^{\prime}_{1})\cdots(a^{\prime}_{k})}
by weak deterministic rules; the last step is justified
by the fact that
f(a^{\prime}_{1})\cdots(a^{\prime}_{k})\geq f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
,
that
f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
is observable, and that every element
larger than an observable element is itself observable. By
Lemma
10.27
,

\underline{f(a^{\prime}_{1})\cdots(a^{\prime}_{k})}\mathrel{R}_{\tau}\left\llbracket{\underline{f(a^{\prime}_{1})\cdots(a^{\prime}_{k})}}\right\rrbracket\rho=f(a^{\prime}_{1})\cdots(a^{\prime}_{k})
. Using
Lemma
10.25
, we deduce that

\underline{f}\;N_{1}\cdots N_{k}\mathrel{R}_{\tau}f(a^{\prime}_{1})\cdots(a^{\prime}_{k})
.
Since
f(a^{\prime}_{1})\cdots(a^{\prime}_{k})\geq f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
, and since

\underline{f}\;N_{1}\cdots N_{k}\mathrel{R}_{\tau}
is Scott-closed, hence
downwards-closed (Lemma
10.26
), we obtain that

\underline{f}\;N_{1}\cdots N_{k}\mathrel{R}_{\tau}f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
, as
desired.

\bullet
If
M
is a variable, this is by the assumption

\theta\mathrel{R}_{*}\rho
.

\bullet
If
M=\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]
, we must show that for all

E^{0}\mathrel{R}_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to D\beta}^{\perp}h
, for every observable open
subset
U
of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
,

Next^{*}(E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]])(\overline{\overline{U}})\geq h^{\dagger}(\mu)(U)
.
By Lemma
10.15
, item 2, and taking suprema over

n\geq 1
,
Next^{*}(E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]])(\overline{\overline{U}})
is equal to

\int_{a\in\mathbb{R}}Next^{*}(E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{a}])(\overline{\overline{U}})d\mu
. By Lemma
10.27
,

\underline{\mathbf{a}}\mathrel{R}_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\mathbf{a}
for every

\mathbf{a}\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
. In particular,

\underline{a}\mathrel{R}_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}i(a)
for every
a\in\mathbb{R}
. Since

E^{0}\mathrel{R}_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}\to D\beta}^{\perp}h
,

Next^{*}(E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{a}])(\overline{\overline{U}})\geq h(a)(U)
for
every
a\in\mathbb{R}
. From this, we conclude that

Next^{*}(E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]])(\overline{\overline{U}})\geq\int_{a\in\mathbb{R}}h(a)(U)d\mu=h^{\dagger}(\mu)(U)
.

\bullet
If
M=\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;N
, we must show that for all

E^{0}\mathrel{R}_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}\to D\beta}^{\perp}h
, for every observable open subset

U
of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
,

Next^{*}(E^{0}[M\theta])(\overline{\overline{U}})\geq h^{\dagger}(|\mathbf{a}|.\delta_{*})(U)
where
\mathbf{a}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho
. We observe
that
h^{\dagger}(|\mathbf{a}|.\delta_{*})=|\mathbf{a}|.h(*)
.
Hence the claim is clear if
|\mathbf{a}|=0
, notably if

\mathbf{a}=\bot
. Let us assume that
\mathbf{a}\neq\bot
. Then

N\theta\mathrel{R}_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\mathbf{a}
, by induction hypothesis. By
definition of
\mathrel{R}_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}
,

N\theta\to^{*}\underline{\mathbf{a^{\prime}}}
by weak deterministic
rules, for some zero-ary constant
\underline{\mathbf{a^{\prime}}}
such
that
\mathbf{a^{\prime}}\geq\mathbf{a}
.

Since
E^{0}\mathrel{R}_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}\to D\beta}^{\perp}h
, and since

\underline{*}\mathrel{R}_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}}*
(by the case of zero-ary constants,
already treated),

Next^{*}(E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits{\underline{*}}])(\overline{\overline{U}})\geq h(*)(U)
.
We have
N\theta\to^{*}\underline{\mathbf{a^{\prime}}}
by weak
deterministic rules, so

E^{0}[M\theta]=E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;N\theta]\to^{*}E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a^{\prime}}}]
by weak deterministic rules. By
Lemma
10.21
,

Next^{*}(E^{0}[M\theta])(\overline{\overline{U}})=Next^{*}\allowbreak(E^{0}[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a^{\prime}}}])(\overline{\overline{U}})
, which is equal
to
|\mathbf{a^{\prime}}|.Next^{*}(E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}])(\overline{\overline{U}})

by Lemma
10.15
, item 3, hence is larger than or equal to

|\mathbf{a^{\prime}}|.h(*)(U)
. We verify that

|\mathbf{a^{\prime}}|\geq|\mathbf{a}|
, owing to the fact that

\mathbf{a^{\prime}}\geq\mathbf{a}
. Hence

Next^{*}(E^{0}[M\theta])(\overline{\overline{U}})\geq|\mathbf{a}|.h(*)(U)=h^{\dagger}(|\mathbf{a}|.\delta_{*})(U)
.

\bullet
If
M=\lambda x.N\colon\sigma\to\tau
, then we
must show that for all
P\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}a
,

(M\theta)P\mathrel{R}_{\tau}\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho(a)
. By induction hypothesis, we
have
N\theta[x:=P]\mathrel{R}_{\tau}\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho[x\mapsto a]
. Since

(M\theta)P\to N\theta[x:=P]
, we conclude by
Lemma
10.25
.

\bullet
The case where
M
is an application is immediate.

\bullet
If
M=\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}\;N
, where
N\colon\sigma\to\sigma
,
then let
f\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho
. By induction hypothesis,

N\mathrel{R}_{\sigma\to\sigma}f
, so:
(*)
for all
P\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}a
, we
have
(N\theta)P\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}f(a)
. We show that

M\theta\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}f^{n}(\bot)
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, by induction
on
n
. If
n=0
, then
f^{0}(\bot)=\bot
is in

M\theta\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}
by Lemma
10.26
. If
n\geq 1
,
then
N\theta(M\theta)\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}f(f^{n-1}(\bot))
, using
(*)

and the induction hypothesis. Now

M\theta=\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}(N\theta)\to N\theta(M\theta)
by a
weak deterministic rule, so
M\theta\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}f^{n}(\bot)
, by
Lemma
10.25
. Now that we have shown that

M\theta\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}f^{n}(\bot)
, namely that
f^{n}(\bot)
is in

M\theta\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}
for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we use the fact that

M\theta\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}
is Scott-closed (Lemma
10.26
)
and we conclude that

\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho=\mathop{\text{lfp}}(f)=\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}f^{n}(\bot)
is in

M\theta\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}
.

\bullet
If
M=\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits N
, with
N\colon\sigma
, then we must
show that for all
E^{0}\mathrel{R}_{\sigma\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}h
, for
every observable open subset
U
of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
,

Next^{*}(E^{0}[M\theta])(\overline{\overline{U}})\geq h^{\dagger}(\delta_{\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho})(U)
. By induction hypothesis,

N\theta\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho
, so by definition of

\mathrel{R}_{\sigma\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}
, we obtain

Next^{*}(E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits N\theta])(\overline{\overline{U}})\geq h(\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho)(U)
; this is exactly what we want to prove.

\bullet
If
M=\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\sigma}\to N};P\colon D\tau
, then we
must show that for all
E^{0}\mathrel{R}_{\tau\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}h
, for
every observable open subset
U
of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
,

Next^{*}(E^{0}[M\theta])(\overline{\overline{U}})\geq h^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho)(U)
. We note that

h^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho)(U)=h^{\dagger}((\left\llbracket{\lambda x_{\sigma}.P}\right\rrbracket\rho)^{\dagger}\allowbreak(\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho))(U)=(h^{\dagger}\circ\left\llbracket{\lambda x_{\sigma}.P}\right\rrbracket\rho)^{\dagger}\allowbreak(\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho))(U)
, where the last equality is by the monad equation

g^{\dagger}\circ f^{\dagger}=(g^{\dagger}\circ f)^{\dagger}
.

We consider the weak evaluation context

{E^{\prime}}^{0}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\sigma}\to[]};P\theta]
, and we claim
that

{E^{\prime}}^{0}\mathrel{R}_{\sigma\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}h^{\dagger}\circ\left\llbracket{\lambda x_{\sigma}.P}\right\rrbracket\rho
. This means showing that for all

Q\mathrel{R}_{\sigma}b
, for every observable open subset
V
of

\left\llbracket{D\beta}\right\rrbracket
,

Next^{*}({E^{\prime}}^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits Q])(\overline{\overline{V}})\geq h^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket{\lambda x_{\sigma}.P}\right\rrbracket\rho(b))(V)
. The right-hand side is equal
to
h^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket{P}\right\rrbracket\rho[x\mapsto b])(V)
. Since

{E^{\prime}}^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits Q]=E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x_{\sigma}\to\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits Q};P\theta]\to E^{0}[P\theta[x:=Q]]
by a weak deterministic rule, and by
Lemma
10.21
, the left-hand side

Next^{*}({E^{\prime}}^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits Q])(\overline{\overline{V}})
is equal to

Next^{*}(E^{0}[P\theta[x:=Q]])(\overline{\overline{V}})
. The latter is larger
than or equal to
h^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket{P}\right\rrbracket\rho[x\mapsto b])(V)
since

P\theta[x:=Q]\mathrel{R}_{D\tau}\left\llbracket{P}\right\rrbracket\rho[x\mapsto b]
by
induction hypothesis and since

E^{0}\mathrel{R}_{\tau\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}h
.

Since

{E^{\prime}}^{0}\mathrel{R}_{\sigma\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}h^{\dagger}\circ\left\llbracket{\lambda x_{\sigma}.P}\right\rrbracket\rho
and since by induction hypothesis

N\theta\mathrel{R}_{D\sigma}\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho
, we have

Next^{*}({E^{\prime}}^{0}[N\theta])(\overline{\overline{U}})\geq(h^{\dagger}\circ\left\llbracket{\lambda x_{\sigma}.P}\right\rrbracket\rho)^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho)(U)
, and this
is exactly what we had to prove.

\bullet
If
M=\langle M_{1},M_{2}\rangle
, with

M_{1}\colon\tau_{1}
and
M_{2}\colon\tau_{2}
, then

M\theta\to^{*}\langle M_{1}\theta,M_{2}\theta\rangle
by weak
deterministic rules (vacuously), and

M_{1}\theta\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}}\left\llbracket{M_{1}}\right\rrbracket\rho
,

M_{2}\theta\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{2}}\left\llbracket{M_{2}}\right\rrbracket\rho
, by induction hypothesis.

\bullet
If
M=\pi_{1}N
, with
N\colon\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}
,
then
N\theta\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}}\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho
by induction
hypothesis. If
\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho=\bot
, then
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho=\bot
,
and therefore
M\theta\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}}=\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho
,
by Lemma
10.26
. Otherwise,
\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho
is of the
form
(a_{1},a_{2})
with
a_{1}\neq\bot
or
a_{2}\neq\bot
. By
definition of
\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2}}
,

N\theta\to^{*}\langle N_{1},N_{2}\rangle
by weak deterministic
rules in such a way that
N_{1}\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}}a_{1}
and

N_{2}\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{2}}a_{2}
. We note that

M\theta=\pi_{1}N\theta\to^{*}\pi_{1}\langle N_{1},N_{2}\rangle\to N_{1}
by weak deterministic rules, so

M\theta\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}}a_{1}=\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho
, using
Lemma
10.25
.

The case
M=\pi_{2}N
is similar.

\bullet
If
M=\iota_{1}N\colon\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}
, then vacuously

M\theta\to^{*}\iota_{1}N\theta
by weak deterministic rules. By
induction hypothesis,
N\theta\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}}\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho
, so

M\theta\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}}(1,\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho)=\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho
.

The case
M=\iota_{2}N
is similar.

\bullet
If
M=\boldsymbol{\mathtt{case}}NP_{1}P_{2}\colon\tau
, with

N\colon\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}
, then by induction hypothesis

N\theta\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}}\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho
. If

\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho=\bot
, then
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho=\bot
, so

M\theta\mathrel{R}_{\tau}\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho
, by Lemma
10.26
.
Otherwise,
\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\rho=(i,b)
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
and

b\in\left\llbracket{\tau_{i}}\right\rrbracket
. By definition of

\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}+\tau_{2}}
,
N\theta\to^{*}\iota_{i}N^{\prime}
by weak
deterministic rules, in such a way that
N^{\prime}\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{i}}b
. Then

M\theta\to^{*}\boldsymbol{\mathtt{case}}{(\iota_{i}N^{\prime})}{(P_{1}\theta)}{(P_{2}\theta)}\to P_{i}\theta N^{\prime}
by weak deterministic rules. By induction
hypothesis,
P_{i}\theta\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{i}\to\tau}\left\llbracket{P_{i}}\right\rrbracket\rho
, so

P_{i}\theta N^{\prime}\mathrel{R}_{\tau}\left\llbracket{P_{i}}\right\rrbracket(\rho)(b)
. By
Lemma
10.25
,

M\theta\mathrel{R}_{\tau}\left\llbracket{P_{i}}\right\rrbracket(\rho)(b)=\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho
.
∎

We obtain the generalized ISPCF terms as
M\theta
, where

(M,\theta)
ranges over
\Gamma
. In that case, for every variable

x
in the domain of
\theta
,
x
has type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
, and if we write

\underline{\mathbf{a}}
for
\theta(x)
, then we may define

\rho(x)
as
\mathbf{a}
. Then
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\rho
is the semantics

\left\llbracket{M\theta}\right\rrbracket
of the generalized ISPCF term
M\theta
. By
Lemma
10.27
,

\underline{\mathbf{a}}\mathrel{R}_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}\mathbf{a}
, so
\theta\mathrel{R}_{*}\rho
.
Proposition
10.28
then implies the following.

Corollary 10.29
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of strictly observable first-order constants. For every
generalized ISPCF term
M\colon\tau
,
M\mathrel{R}_{\tau}\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket
.

Lemma 10.30
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of strictly observable first-order constants. For every
observable type
\beta
, for every generalized ISPCF term

N\colon\beta
, for every
b\in\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
, if
N\mathrel{R}_{\beta}b

then either
b
is not observable, or
b
is observable and

N\to^{*}V
by deterministic rules for some value
V\colon\beta

such that
\left\llbracket{V}\right\rrbracket\geq b
.

Note that
N\to^{*}V
by deterministic rules, but not necessarily weak
rules.

Proof.

We show that
N\mathrel{R}_{\beta}b
and the fact that
b
is observable
together imply the existence of a value
V\colon\beta
such that

\left\llbracket{V}\right\rrbracket\geq b
, by induction on
\mathrel{R}_{\beta}
. If
\beta
is a basic
type, then this is by definition of
\mathrel{R}_{\beta}
. If
\beta
is a
product type
\beta_{1}\times\beta_{2}
, then let us write
b
as

(b_{1},b_{2})
, where
b_{1}\neq\bot
and
b_{2}\neq\bot
since
b
is
observable. Since
b\neq\bot
, by definition of

\mathrel{R}_{\beta_{1}\times\beta_{2}}
,
M\to^{*}\langle M_{1},M_{2}\rangle
by
weak deterministic rules, for some
M_{1}\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{1}}b_{1}
and

M_{2}\mathrel{R}_{\tau_{2}}b_{2}
. By induction hypothesis,
M_{1}\to^{*}V_{1}
and

M_{2}\to^{*}V_{2}
by weak deterministic rules, for some values
V_{1}

and
V_{2}
such that
\left\llbracket{V_{1}}\right\rrbracket\geq b_{1}
,
\left\llbracket{V_{2}}\right\rrbracket\geq b_{2}
.
Then

M\to^{*}\langle M_{1},M_{2}\rangle\to^{*}\langle V_{1},M_{2}\rangle\to^{*}\langle V_{1},V_{2}\rangle
by (non-weak) deterministic rules.
The term
V\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\langle V_{1},V_{2}\rangle
is a value, and

\left\llbracket{V}\right\rrbracket\geq(b_{1},b_{2})=b
. The case of sum types is similar.
∎

We finally obtain the promised adequacy theorem.

Theorem 10.31

(Adequacy)
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of strictly observable first-order constants. For every
observable type
\beta
, for every generalized ISPCF term

M\colon D\beta
, for every observable open subset
U
of

\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
,
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(U)=Next^{*}(M)(\overline{\overline{U}})
.

Proof.

We consider the empty weak evaluation context
E^{0}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}[]
, and
we claim that

E^{0}\mathrel{R}_{\beta\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}\eta_{\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket}
. In order
to show this, we need to show that for all
N\mathrel{R}_{\beta}b
, for every
observable open subset
U
of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
,

Next^{*}(E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits N])(\overline{\overline{U}})\geq\delta_{b}(U)
.

If
b\not\in U
, then
\delta_{b}(U)=0
, and this is obvious.

Otherwise,
\delta_{b}(U)=1
. Since
b\in U
and since
U
is
observable,
b
is an observable element of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
. Since

N\mathrel{R}_{\beta}b
, Lemma
10.30
tells us that
N\to^{*}V
by
deterministic rules for some value
V\colon\beta
such that

\left\llbracket{V}\right\rrbracket\geq b
. Since
U
