is upwards closed,
\left\llbracket{V}\right\rrbracket
is in
U
.
Therefore
V
is in
\overline{U}
, and
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits V
is in
\overline{\overline{U}}
.

We note that since
N\to^{*}V
by deterministic rules,

\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits N\to^{*}\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits V
by deterministic rules as well. By
Lemma
10.21
,

Next^{*}(E^{0}[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits N])=Next^{*}\allowbreak(\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits N)
is equal
to
Next^{*}(\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits V)
. By Lemma
10.15
, item 1, (and
taking suprema over all
n
,) since
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits V
is a normal form,

Next^{*}(\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits V)(\overline{\overline{U}})=\delta_{\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits V}(\overline{\overline{U}})=1=\delta_{b}(U)
.

Now that we have proved

E\mathrel{R}_{\beta\vdash D\beta}^{\perp}\eta_{\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket}
, we apply it
to
M\mathrel{R}_{D\beta}\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket
(Corollary
10.29
), and,
noting that
E=[]
is a weak evaluation context, we obtain that

Next^{*}(E[M])(\overline{\overline{U}})\geq(\eta_{\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket})^{\dagger}(\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket)(U)=\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(U)
. The converse inequality is by soundness
(Proposition
10.20
).
∎

Remark 10.32
.

Why do we restrict
M
to be of type
D\beta
in
Theorem
10.31
? Restricting it to be of observable type

\beta
would be silly, because only deterministic computations
happen at type
\beta
. In order to be able to do any probabilistic
computation at all,
M
has to be of distribution type. A similar
choice was made in
[
Goubault-Larrecq, 2015
,
Goubault-Larrecq, 2019
]
.

Remark 10.33
.

The reason why Theorem
10.31
only considers

observable
open subsets
U
is that adequacy is concerned
with
terminating
computations, and terms of observable type

\beta
terminate on values
V
(if they do terminate at all), whose
semantics is observable. For non-observable open subsets
U
, the
denotational semantics gives us more information. For example, when

U
is the whole of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
,
Next^{*}(M)(\overline{\overline{U}})
is the
‘probability’ that
M
terminates at all, while
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(U)
is
larger in general, and represents the ‘probability’ of all
executions starting from
M
, including those that do not terminate.
For example, let
M\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits(\boldsymbol{\mathtt{rec}}(\lambda n_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}}.n))
,
then
Next^{*}(M)(\overline{\overline{\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}}\right\rrbracket}})=0
, while

\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}}\right\rrbracket)=\delta_{\bot}(\left\llbracket{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}}\right\rrbracket)=1
. For a
product type
\beta_{1}\times\beta_{2}
, the Scott-open set

U\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\left\llbracket{\beta_{1}}\right\rrbracket\times U^{\prime}
, where
U^{\prime}
is a non-empty
observable subset of
\left\llbracket{\beta_{2}}\right\rrbracket
, is not observable either,
but is smaller than
\left\llbracket{\beta_{1}\times\beta_{2}}\right\rrbracket
. In that case,

Next^{*}(M)(\overline{\overline{U}})
is the ‘probability’ that
M
terminates on
a value
\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\langle V_{1},V_{2}\rangle
with

V_{2}\in\overline{U^{\prime}}
, but
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(U)
is the ‘probability’
that
\pi_{2}M
terminates with a value in
U^{\prime}
, thereby also
counting all computations for which
\pi_{1}M
does not terminate.
Another view to the question is given by Lemma
10.35
below.

Remark 10.34
.

The reader interested in extending the adequacy theorem to
non-observable open subsets
U
should be warned of the following
difficulty. Let
U
be the whole of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
. While

\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(U)
is perfectly well-defined, what would be the
operational meaning of “the ‘probability’ of all executions
starting from
M
”? This is difficult, already for executions not
involving
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]
and rule (
7
), but
involving
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}
and rule (
8
). Imagine an infinite
execution starting for
M
and calling
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}
on real values

a_{1}
,
a_{2}
, …, in succession. One would imagine that the
‘probability’ of the unique execution starting from
M
is

\prod_{i=1}^{+\infty}a_{i}
. However, that is ill-defined in
general. For an example, consider
a_{i}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}2
for
i

odd,
1/2
for
i
even.

We say that
\Sigma
is
terse
if and only if for every

n\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is a unique zero-ary constant
\underline{n}
of type

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{int}}
such that
\left\llbracket{\underline{n}}\right\rrbracket=n
, and
\underline{*}
is
the only zero-ary constant of type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{unit}}
. In this case, we can
avoid the use of sets of the form
\overline{\overline{U}}
in the definition of

\precsim^{app}
and
\cong^{app}
, because of the following lemma.
Note that we do not need to require a similar condition of terseness
on
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{void}}
(if
\Sigma
consists of strictly observable first-order constants,
then there can be no zero-ary constant of type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{void}}
), or on

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
(because of the way we defined generalized ISPCF terms, see
Section
10
).

Lemma 10.35
.

Let
\Sigma
be terse and consist of strictly observable first-order constants.
For every observable type
\beta
, the map
M\mapsto\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket
is an
order isomorphism from
Norm_{\beta}
to the subdcpo
Obs_{\beta}
of
observable elements in
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
. The open subsets of

Norm_{\beta}
are exactly the sets of the form
\overline{U}
, where

U
is a observable Scott-open subset of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
. Those of

Norm_{D\beta}
are exactly the sets of the form
\overline{\overline{U}}
, where

U
is a observable Scott-open subset of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
.

Proof.

The first claim is clear. The inverse map is the map

a\mapsto\underline{a}
of Definition
10.5
. For
the second claim,
Obs_{\beta}
is a Scott-open subset of

\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
, and is in particular a subdcpo (i.e., directed
suprema are computed as in
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
). Let
U^{\prime}
be any open
subset of
Norm_{\beta}
. Its image by the map

a\mapsto\underline{a}
is an open subset of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
included
in
Obs_{\beta}
, hence is Scott-open in
Obs_{\beta}
, since

Obs_{\beta}
is a subdcpo of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
. The third claim follows
from the fact that
M\mapsto\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits M
is an isomorphism between

Norm_{\beta}
and
Norm_{D\beta}
.
∎

10.3
Contextual equivalence and the applicative preorder

An important, and expected, consequence of adequacy, is that equality
of denotations implies contextual equivalence, namely that for any two
generalized ISPCF terms
M
,
N
of any type
\tau
, if

\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket
then
M
and
N
are contextually equivalent,
meaning that every observable type
\beta
, for every context

\mathcal{E}
of type
\tau\vdash D\beta
, the ‘probability’ that

\mathcal{E}[M]
evaluates to a value in any given open set
U
of
normal forms is equal to the ‘probability’ that
\mathcal{E}[N]

evaluates to a value in
U
.

We define the required contexts
\mathcal{E}
as generalized ISPCF
terms with arbitrarily many holes. This represents the fact that one
may replace
M
at any number of positions in a program by a
contextually equivalent program
N
and not change the observable
behavior of the program.

In order to formalize this, there is no need to invent a new notion of
multi-hole context. Indeed, replacing all the holes in
\mathcal{E}

with a term
M
gives a term with the same semantics as the
application
QM
, where
Q\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\lambda x.\mathcal{E}[x]
. Hence
the notion of contextual equivalence we are looking is the following
one, usually called
applicative equivalence
. This is the
equivalence relation associated with a more primitive notion of

applicative preorder
, which we now define.

Definition 10.36

(Applicative preorder
\precsim^{app}
and equivalence)
.

For any two generalized ISPCF terms
M
,
N
of the same type

\tau
,
M\precsim^{app}N
(resp.,
M\cong^{app}N
) if and only
if, for every observable type
\beta
, for every generalized ISPCF
term
Q\colon\tau\to D\beta
, for every open subset
U
of

Norm_{D\beta}
,
Next^{*}(QM)(U)\leq Next^{*}(QN)(U)
(resp.,

=
).

The adequacy Theorem
10.31
allows us to simplify the
definition of
\precsim^{app}
and
\cong^{app}
as follows. Note
that, if
Q=\lambda x_{\tau}.\mathcal{E}[x]
, where
\mathcal{E}
is
one of our purported multi-hole contexts, then

\left\llbracket{QM}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket{\mathcal{E}[M]}\right\rrbracket
, justifying the view that the
applicative preorder is really about comparing programs with zero,
one, or several occurrences of
M
replaced by another term
N
.

Proposition 10.37
.

Let
\Sigma
be terse and consist of strictly observable first-order constants.
For all generalized ISPCF terms
M
,
N
of the same type
\tau
,

M\precsim^{app}N
(resp.,
M\cong^{app}N
) if and only if, for
every observable type
\beta
, for every generalized ISPCF term

Q\colon\tau\to D\beta
, for every observable Scott-open subset
U

of
\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
,
\left\llbracket{QM}\right\rrbracket(U)\leq\left\llbracket{QN}\right\rrbracket(U)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
10.35
, one may replace the quantification over
open subsets
U
of
Norm_{D\beta}
by a quantification over open
subsets of the form
\overline{\overline{U}}
, where
U
ranges over the observable
Scott-open subsets. Then, by Theorem
10.31
,
Next^{*}(QM)(\overline{\overline{U}})=\left\llbracket{QM}\right\rrbracket(U)
, and similarly with
QN
.
∎

Importantly, we obtain the following easy result.

Proposition 10.38
.

Let
\Sigma
be terse and consist of strictly observable first-order constants. Let
M
and

N
be two generalized ISPCF terms of the same type
\tau
. The
following implications hold:

1.

\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\leq\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\mathrel{\Rightarrow}M\precsim^{app}N
;

2.

\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket\mathrel{\Rightarrow}M\cong^{app}N
.

Proof.

If
\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket\leq\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket
, then for every observable type
\beta
,
for every generalized ISPCF term
Q\colon\tau\to D\beta
,

\left\llbracket{QM}\right\rrbracket=\left\llbracket{Q}\right\rrbracket(\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket)\leq\left\llbracket{Q}\right\rrbracket(\left\llbracket{N}\right\rrbracket)=\left\llbracket{QN}\right\rrbracket
. Using Proposition
10.37
,
M\precsim^{app}N
. The
second part of the theorem is an easy consequence of the first part.
∎

The converse implications would be a form of full abstraction, and are
quite probably hopeless. Therefore we will not bother with them.

Remark 10.39
.

Following up on Remark
8.1
, it follows that when
x
is
not free in
N
and
y
is not free in
M
, the terms

\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x\leftarrow M};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{y\leftarrow N};P
and

\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{y\leftarrow N};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{x\leftarrow M};P
are contextually
equivalent.

Remark 10.40
.

Similarly, the terms
\mathtt{normal}
,

\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{\langle x,y\rangle\leftarrow\mathtt{box\_muller}};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits x
, and

\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{do}}}\nolimits{\langle x,y\rangle\leftarrow\mathtt{box\_muller}};\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits y
(see Section
9.4
) are contextually
equivalent. The terms
\mathtt{box\_muller}
and

\mathtt{box\_muller^{\prime}}
are contextually equivalent, and so are the
terms
\mathtt{expo}
,
\mathtt{expo}^{\prime}
and
\mathtt{von\_neumann}

of Section
9.6
.

11
A Precise Operational Semantics

We give a second operational semantics that is closer to the intent of

[
Vákár et al., 2019
,
Dal Lago and Hoshino, 2019
,
Ehrhard et al., 2018
]
. This requires slightly
stricter assumptions than simply having strictly observable first-order constants.
Those assumptions are always met in practice, as far as we know. Note
that
\exp
,
\sin
,
\cos
, but also
pos
and
\log
, are
precise, for example. One instance of a non-precise constant would be
the sign map, if we decide to define it as
\hat{g}
(see
Section
8
), where
g
maps every negative number to

0
and every non-negative number to
1
; indeed,
\hat{g}
maps every
interval
[a,b]
with
a\leq 0\leq b
to
[0,1]
. We can define
the (precise) sign map as

\lambda x_{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}}.\boldsymbol{\mathtt{if}}(\underline{\text{pos}}\;x)\;\underline{1.0}\;\underline{0.0}
instead.

Definition 11.1
.

The set
\Sigma
consists of
precise first-order constants
if
and only if
\Sigma
has strictly observable first-order constants, and
additionally, for each constant
\underline{f}\colon\sigma_{1}\to\cdots\to\sigma_{k}\to\tau
of arity
k
in
\Sigma
,
f
is

precise
, namely:

•

for all maximal elements
v_{1}\in\left\llbracket{\sigma_{1}}\right\rrbracket
, …,

v_{k}\in\left\llbracket{\sigma_{k}}\right\rrbracket
,
f(v_{1})\cdots(v_{k})
is either
maximal in
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
or equal to
\bot
.

When
\sigma_{1}=\cdots=\sigma_{k}=\tau=\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}
, this means that
f

encodes a partial continuous map from
\mathbb{R}^{k}
to
\mathbb{R}
. If we
equate
i(v)=[v,v]
with the real number
v
, the domain

\{(v_{1},\cdots,v_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\mid f(v_{1})\cdots(v_{k})\neq\bot\}
of
f
is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
f
is required to
be continuous on that domain.

A
precise
configuration
(M,\theta)
is a configuration where
for every
x\in\mathrm{fv}(M)
,
\theta(x)
is a maximal element of

\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
, namely a real number.

A
precise
generalized ISPCF term is a term
M\theta
obtained
from a precise configuration
(M,\theta)
. Given a shape
M
, one
can equate
\theta
with an element of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{fv}(M})
, namely with
a finite tuple of real numbers.
\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{fv}(M)}
, and by extension
the set of precise configurations with shape
M
, is equipped with its
usual metric topology, which we will call its
Euclidean topology
.

Definition 11.2

(
\Gamma^{0}
)
.

We topologize

\Gamma^{0}
as the set of all precise configurations, topologized as
the coproduct over all shapes
M
of the spaces of precise
configurations with shape
M
, each being given the Euclidean topology.

On the one hand,
\Gamma^{0}
is a much more familiar space to the
ordinary topologist. It is a countable coproduct of Polish spaces,
hence it is itself Polish. On the other hand,
\Gamma^{0}
is also a
topological subspace of
\Gamma
. We write
\iota
for the subspace
embedding; we have
\iota(M,\theta)=(M,i\circ\theta)
, where

i
is the familiar subspace embedding
r\mapsto[r,r]
of
\mathbb{R}

into
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
.

Lemma 11.3
.

Assume that
\Sigma
consists of precise first-order constants.
There is a unique continuous map
Next^{0}\colon\Gamma^{0}\to\mathbf{V}\Gamma^{0}
such that, for every
(M,\theta)\in\Gamma^{0}
,
\iota[Next^{0}(M,\theta)]=Next(\iota(M,\theta))
.

Proof.

By inspection of Definition
10.8
. The key point is the
case of
\underline{f}
, where the result holds because
\Sigma
has
precise first-order constants.
∎

Remark 11.4
.

In general,
Next(M\theta)
is a minimal valuation on
\Gamma
.
However,
Next^{0}(M,\theta)
need not be a minimal valuation. For
example,
Next^{0}(x,\allowbreak[x:=\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda]])
is, up to
isomorphism, the Lebesgue valuation on
\mathbb{R}
, which is not minimal
by Lemma
6.1
.

Next^{0}
defines a kernel. Using Lemma
10.14
, we obtain a
continuous map
Next^{0,*}\colon\Gamma^{0}\to\mathbf{V}\Gamma^{0}
,
provided we replace
Norm
by the set

Norm^{0}\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}Norm\cap\Gamma^{0}
. By induction on
n
, it is easy
to see that

\iota[Next^{0,\leq n}(M,\theta)]=Next^{\leq n}(\iota(M,\theta))
. It follows:

Lemma 11.5
.

Assume that
\Sigma
consists of precise first-order constants.
For every precise configuration
(M,\theta)
,

\iota[Next^{0,*}(M,\theta)]=Next^{*}(\iota(M,\theta))
.

Proposition
10.20
and Theorem
10.31
then
immediately imply the following. We equate
\Gamma_{0}
with a
subspace of
\Gamma
, and omit any mention of the embedding
\iota
.

Theorem 11.6
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of precise first-order constants. For every
precise generalized ISPCF term
M\colon D\tau
, where
\tau
is
any type, for every open subset
U
of
\left\llbracket{\tau}\right\rrbracket
,

\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(U)\geq Next^{0,*}(M)(\overline{\overline{U}}\cap\Gamma^{0})
. This
inequality is an equality if
\tau
is an observable type and
U
is
observable.

12
A Sampling-Based Operational Semantics

An operational semantics is usually an abstract view of an
implementation. However, operational semantics such as those of
previous sections (and such as those of

[
Vákár et al., 2019
,
Dal Lago and Hoshino, 2019
,
Ehrhard et al., 2018
]
, which even go as far as
manipulating
true
real numbers) are far from an implementation.
Park, Pfenning and Thrun
[
Park et al., 2008
]
, and later Dal Lago and
Hoshino
[
Dal Lago and Hoshino, 2019
]
introduce a more concrete

sampling-based
operational semantics. Roughly speaking, it is
more concrete in the sense that it no longer has to sample from
arbitrary measures on
\mathbb{R}
, and instead draws arbitrarily long
strings of independent, uniform random bits, which are drawn at random
prior to execution.

We use a similar idea here. This will apply under some reasonable
assumptions, the most important one being that we only have one term
of the form
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]
, namely
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]
. As we have
hinted in Section
9
, one can define quite a number of
other distributions from that one alone.

12.1
Random strings

Our sampling-based operational semantics will be a

deterministic
semantics, parameterized by an infinite string of
bits, which we can think as having been drawn at random, uniformly, in
advance. This trick is routinely used in complexity theory, for
example, where a popular definition of a randomized Turing machine is
a Turing machine with an additional read-only
random tape
on
which the head can only move right. It is also at the heart of the
definition of computable probability theory, where a
computable
random variable
on a computable metric space
S
is a measurable
map
X\colon\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to S
such that
X
is computable on a
subset of
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
of
\upsilon
-measure one

[
Roy, 2011
, Definition II.12]
. We have also used similar tricks in
Section
9.8
, too.

We will reuse several notions and notations from
Section
9.7
. Notably,
\upsilon
is the
uniform measure
\mathrm{bin}[\lambda_{1}]
on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. We also recall
the maps
\mathrm{bin}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
,

\mathrm{num}\colon\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\mathbb{R}
,

split\colon\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to(\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}})^{2}
, the notation

s[m]\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}{(s_{\langle m,n\rangle})}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and the shift

\mathop{\uparrow}s
.

12.2
The sampling-based operational semantics

Our next semantics will be a partial map

Next^{\prime}(s)\colon\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma^{\prime}
, parameterized by a fixed
random string
s
, and where
\Gamma^{\prime}
is a space of so-called

enriched configurations
. This is somewhat similar to the
sampling-based operational semantics of

[
Dal Lago and Hoshino, 2019
, Section 3.3]
. An enriched configuration is a
tuple
(M,i,r)
where
M
is a generalized ISPCF term,
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
and
r\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
:
i
is the number of the next random string

s[i]
to use in order to implement rule (
7
), and
r

is the product of the score values produced by rule (
8
)
so far. We let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be the space of all indexed configurations.
This is a product dcpo
\Gamma\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
, where

\mathbb{N}
is ordered by equality and
\mathbb{R}_{+}
by its usual ordering. The
Scott topology on the product coincides with the product topology,
since this is a finite product of continuous posets

[
Goubault-Larrecq, 2013
, Proposition 5.1.54]
; as
such,
\Gamma^{\prime}
is also a continuous poset. Random strings are
elements of the topological product
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, where
\{0,1\}

has the discrete topology.

Definition 12.1

(Sampling-based operational semantics)
.

Assume that all constants in
\Sigma
are first-order constants, and
that the only term of the form
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]
is

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]
, where
\lambda_{1}
is Lebesgue measure on

[0,1]
. The
sampling-based operational semantics
of ISPCF
is the partial map

Next^{\prime}\colon\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\to\Gamma^{\prime}\to\Gamma^{\prime}
defined by:

•

for every instance of a rule
L\to R
of
Figure
3

except (
7
), (
8
) and (
9
),

Next^{\prime}(s)(L,i,r)=(R,i,r)
;

•

(case of
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}
)

Next^{\prime}(s)(E[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]],i,r)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{\mathrm{num}(s[0])}],i+1,r)
;

•

(case of
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}
)

Next^{\prime}(s)(E[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a}}],i,r)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}],i,|\mathbf{a}|.r)
;

•

(case of
\underline{f}
) If

k=\alpha(\underline{f})\neq 0
, then

Next^{\prime}(s)(E[\underline{f}\;\underline{a}_{1}\cdots\underline{a}_{k}],i,r)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}(E[\underline{f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})}],i,r)
if
f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
is observable;

•

Next^{\prime}(s)(M,i,r)
is undefined otherwise.

Let us write
(M,i,r)\to_{s}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime},r^{\prime})
for
“
Next^{\prime}(s)(M,i,r)
is defined and equal to
(M^{\prime},i^{\prime},r^{\prime})
”. Let
also
\to^{*}_{s}
be the reflexive-transitive closure of
\to_{s}
. For
every generalized ISPCF term
M
, and every open set
U
of normal
forms, there is at most one enriched configuration
(M^{\prime},i^{\prime},r^{\prime})
such
that
(M,0,1)\to^{*}_{s}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime},r^{\prime})
and
M^{\prime}\in U
. If it exists,
we call that
r^{\prime}
the
score

\sigma[U](M,s)
of
M
relative
to
s
and
U
; otherwise we let
\sigma[U](M,s)\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}0
. Note
that the score depends on
M
, on
U
, and on
s
.

One can refine this as follows. This will be needed below. Let

\to^{\leq k}
denote reachability in at most
k

Next^{\prime}
steps.

Definition 12.2
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\sigma_{k}[U](M,s)
be the unique value

r^{\prime}
such that
(M,0,1)\allowbreak\to^{\leq k}_{s}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime},r^{\prime})

for some
M^{\prime}\in U
, if that exists, and
0
otherwise.

Then
\sigma[U]
is the supremum of the directed family of maps

{(\sigma_{k}[U])}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Lemma 12.3
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, for all
i,i^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
,

r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, for all generalized ISPCF terms
M
and
M^{\prime}
,

(M,i,r)\to_{\mathop{\uparrow}s}^{\leq k}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime},r^{\prime})
if and only if

(M,i+1,r)\to_{s}^{\leq k}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime}+1,r^{\prime})
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
(M,i,r)\to_{\mathop{\uparrow}s}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime},r^{\prime})
if
and only if
(M,i+1,r)\to_{s}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime}+1,r^{\prime})
, which is an easy
verification, and to induct on
k
.
∎

The following is easily proved, too.

Lemma 12.4
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, for all
i,i^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
,

r,r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, for all generalized ISPCF
terms
M
and
M^{\prime}
, if
(M,i,0)\to_{s}^{\leq k}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime},r^{\prime})
then

r^{\prime}=0
. If
(M,i,r)\to_{s}^{\leq k}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime},r^{\prime})
then

(M,i+1,\alpha.r)\to_{s}^{\leq k}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime}+1,\alpha.r^{\prime})
. The
converse implication holds if
\alpha\neq 0
.

Lemma 12.5
.

Under the assumptions of Definition
12.1
, for every
open subset
U
of
Norm
, the maps
\sigma_{k}[U]
(
k\in\mathbb{N}
)
and
\sigma[U]
are lower semicontinuous from

\Gamma\times\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
to
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Proof.

We recall that lower semicontinuous simply means continuous,
provided that we equip
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with its Scott topology, which we
now assume.

In order to simplify the proof, we use the following result by
Ershov
[
Ershov, 1997
, Proposition 2]
: given a c-space
X

(an
\alpha
-space in Ershov’s terminology, e.g., a continuous poset
with its Scott topology), and two topological spaces
Y
and
Z
, a
function
f\colon X\times Y\to Z
is separately continuous if and
only if it is jointly continuous. Joint continuity is ordinary
continuity from
X\times Y
with the product topology. Separate
continuity means that
f(x,\_)
and
f(\_,y)
are continuous for
fixed
x
and
y
respectively. This applies here, as
\Gamma^{\prime}
is
a continuous poset.

Since suprema of lower semicontinuous maps are lower semicontinuous,
it suffices to show that
\sigma_{k}[U]
is lower semicontinuous for
every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, which we do by induction on
k
.

We fix a template
M_{0}
, and we show that
\sigma_{k}[U]
is
continuous from the product of the space of generalized ISPCF terms
of shape
M_{0}
with
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. We only need
to prove separate continuity.

If
k=0
, then
\sigma_{0}[U](M,s)=1
if
M
is normal,
0

otherwise. For
M
fixed, this is constant hence continuous in
s
.
For
s
fixed, we note that normality only depends on the shape
(here,
M_{0}
). Hence
\sigma_{0}[U](\_,s)
is constant, hence
continuous, on the subspace of generalized ISPCF terms of shape

M_{0}
.

Let us assume
k\geq 1
. If the unique
\to_{s}
step from

(M,i,r)
is by applying a rule
L\to R
of
L\to R
of
Figure
3
except (
7
),
(
8
) and (
9
), then we note that the same
rule would apply if we replaced
M
by any other term with the same
shape
M_{0}
. Then
\sigma_{k}[U](M,s)=\sigma_{k-1}[U](R,s)
,
and we apply the induction hypothesis.

In the case of
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}
,
M=E[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]]
, and if

(M,0,1)\to^{\leq k}_{s}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime},r^{\prime})
for some
M^{\prime}\in U
, then

(M,0,1)\to_{s}(E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{\mathrm{num}(s[0])}],1,1)\to^{\leq k-1}_{s}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime},r^{\prime})
, where
r^{\prime}=\sigma_{k}[U](M,s)
.
We then have

(E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{\mathrm{num}(s[0])}],\allowbreak 0,1)\to^{\leq k-1}_{\mathop{\uparrow}s}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime}-1,r^{\prime})
by Lemma
12.3
, hence

r^{\prime}=\sigma_{k}[U](M,\mathop{\uparrow}s)=\sigma_{k-1}[U](E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{\mathrm{num}(s[0])}],\mathop{\uparrow}s)
. For
M
fixed, we see that the
composition of the maps
s\mapsto s[0]\mapsto\mathrm{num}(s[0])
is
continuous from
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
to
\mathbb{R}
, using
Lemma
9.6
; also,
s\mapsto\mathop{\uparrow}s
is continuous. As
in the proof of Proposition
10.9
, the map

a\in\mathbb{R}\mapsto E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{a}]
is continuous, so

\sigma_{k}[U](M,s)
defines a continuous function of
s
. For
s

fixed, this is the composition of the continuous function that maps
every term
M=E[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]]
of shape
M_{0}
to

E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{a}]
(where
a\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\mathrm{num}(s[0])
) with

s_{k-1}[U](\_,\mathop{\uparrow}s)
, hence is continuous as well.

In the case of
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}
, we have

M=E[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a}}]
, and assuming that

(M,0,1)\to^{\leq k}_{s}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime},r^{\prime})
for some
M^{\prime}\in U
, we have

(M,0,1)\to_{s}(E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}],0,\allowbreak|\mathbf{a}|)\to^{\leq k-1}_{s}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime},r^{\prime})
, and
r^{\prime}=\sigma_{k}[U](M,s)
.
In particular, if
|\mathbf{a}|\neq 0
then

(E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}],0,1)\to^{\leq k-1}_{s}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime},r^{\prime}/|\mathbf{a}|)
by Lemma
12.4
(final part), so

\sigma_{k-1}[U](E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}],s)=r^{\prime}/|\mathbf{a}|
.
Therefore

\sigma_{k}[U](M,s)=|\mathbf{a}|.\sigma_{k-1}[U](E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}],s)
. We see that this holds also when

|\mathbf{a}|=0
, using the first part of Lemma
12.4
,
and also when
(M,0,1)\to^{\leq k}_{s}(M^{\prime},i^{\prime},r^{\prime})
for no

M^{\prime}\in U
, in which case both sides of the equality are
0
. By
induction hypothesis,
\sigma_{k-1}
is continuous. The map

M=E[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a}}]\mapsto E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}]
is continuous, by a similar argument as in
Proposition
10.9
. The map

M=E[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a}}]\mapsto\mathbf{a}
is also
clearly continuous, the map
\mathbf{a}\mapsto|\mathbf{a}|
is
continuous from
\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}_{\bot}
to
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, and product is
Scott-continuous on
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
, so
\sigma_{k}[U](\_,s)
is
continuous, for every fixed
s
. For

M=E[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a}}]
fixed,

\sigma_{k}[U](M,\_)=|\mathbf{a}|.\sigma_{k-1}[U](E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}],\_)
is obviously continuous. Hence
\sigma_{k}[U]

itself is continuous.

In case

M=E[\underline{f}\;\underline{a}_{1}\cdots\underline{a}_{k}]
where

k=\alpha(\underline{f})\neq 0
, then either

f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
is observable and

\sigma_{k}[U](M,s)=\sigma_{k-1}[U](E[\underline{f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})}],s)
, or else
\sigma_{k}[U](M,s)=0
. For
M

fixed, this defines a continuous map in
s
. We now consider
s

fixed. The function that maps every term
M
as above, of shape

M_{0}
, to the tuple of (denotations of) constants of type
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{real}}

among
\underline{a}_{1}
, …,
\underline{a}_{k}
is continuous,
hence also the function that maps
M
(of given shape
M_{0}
) to

f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
. In particular the set
V
of generalized
ISPCF terms
M
of shape
M_{0}
such that
f(a_{1})\cdots(a_{k})
is
observable is open. Let
g
be the function that maps every

M=E[\underline{f}\;\underline{\mathbf{a}_{1}}\cdots\underline{\mathbf{a}_{k}}]
in
V
to

E[\underline{f(\mathbf{a}_{1})\cdots(\mathbf{a}_{k})}]
. This is
continuous. The inverse image of every non-trivial Scott-open
subset
]a,+\infty]
of
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
(namely,
a>0
) by

\sigma_{k}[U](\_,s)
is equal to

g^{-1}(\sigma_{k-1}[U](\_,s)^{-1}]a,+\infty])
is then open,
showing that
\sigma_{k}[U](\_,s)
is continuous.
∎

We define the ‘probability’ of reaching a normal form in some open
subset
U
of
Norm
through the sampling-based operational semantics
as:

\displaystyle P[M\to U]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\text{def}}}}\int_{s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\sigma[U](M,s)d\upsilon.

In other words,
P[M\to U]
is the average score of all strings of
rewrite steps starting from
M
, where
s
is drawn uniformly at
random. The integral makes sense by
Proposition
12.5
. We now verify that
P[M\to U]

is exactly the ‘probability’
Next^{*}(M)(U)
that
M
eventually
evaluates to a normal form in
U
according to the operational
semantics of Section
10
.

Proposition 12.6
.

Assume that all constants in
\Sigma
are first-order constants, and
that the only available term of the form
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]
is

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]
, where
\lambda_{1}
is Lebesgue measure on

[0,1]
. For every open subset
U
of
Norm
, for every
generalized ISPCF term
M
,
P[M\to U]=Next^{*}(M)(U)
.

Proof.

We show that

\int_{s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\sigma_{k}[U](M,s)d\upsilon=Next^{\leq k}(M)(U)
by induction on
k\in\mathbb{N}
. The result
will follow by taking suprema over
k
.

When
k=0
,
Next^{\leq 0}(M)(U)
is equal to
\delta_{M}(U)
if

M\in Norm
, to
0
otherwise, hence to
\chi_{U}(M)
in all cases.
For every
s\in[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
,
\sigma_{0}[U](M,s)
is equal to

\chi_{U}(M)
, and since
\upsilon
is a probability distribution,

\int_{s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\sigma_{k}[U](M,s)d\upsilon=\chi_{U}(M)
.

When
k\geq 1
, we look at the shape of
M
, and we use
Lemma
10.15
to evaluate
Next^{\leq k}(M)(U)
.

If
M\in Norm
, then

Next^{\leq k}(M)(U)=\delta_{M}(U)=\chi_{U}(M)
. Also,

\sigma_{k}[U](M)=\chi_{U}(M)
, so

\int_{s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\sigma_{k}[U](M,s)d\upsilon=\chi_{U}(M)
.

We arrive at the main case of the proof. If

M=E[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]]
, where necessarily
\mu=\lambda_{1}
, then,
as in the proof of Lemma
12.5
,

\sigma_{k}[U](M,\mathop{\uparrow}s)=\sigma_{k-1}[U](E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{\mathrm{num}(s[0])}],\mathop{\uparrow}s)
, so:

\displaystyle\int_{s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\sigma_{k}[U](M,s)d\upsilon

\displaystyle=\int_{s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\sigma_{k-1}[U](E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{\mathrm{num}(s[0])}],\mathop{\uparrow}s)d\upsilon.

We now use the change-of-variables formula with respect to the
homeomorphism

split\colon s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\mapsto(s[0],\mathop{\uparrow}s)\in(\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}})^{2}
(see Lemma
9.7
). Since
split[\upsilon]=\upsilon\otimes\upsilon
, and using Proposition
4.1
:

\displaystyle\int_{s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\sigma_{k}[U](M,s)d\upsilon

\displaystyle=\int_{t\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\left(\int_{(s^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\sigma_{k-1}[U](E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{\mathrm{num}(t)}],s^{\prime})d\upsilon\right)d\upsilon

\displaystyle=\int_{t\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}Next^{\leq k-1}(E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{\mathrm{num}(t)}])(U)d\upsilon

by induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=\int_{a\in\mathbb{R}}Next^{\leq k-1}(E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{a}])(U)d\lambda_{1}

by the change-of-variables formula and
Lemma
9.6

\displaystyle=Next^{\leq k}(M)(U),

by Lemma
10.15
, item 2.

If
M=E[\boldsymbol{\mathtt{score}}\;\underline{\mathbf{a}}]
, then, as in the proof
of Lemma
12.5
,

\sigma_{k}[U](M,s)=|\mathbf{a}|.\sigma_{k-1}[U](E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}],s)
. Hence:

\displaystyle\int_{s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}\sigma_{k}[U](M,s)d\upsilon

\displaystyle=\int_{s\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}}|\mathbf{a}|.\sigma_{k-1}[U](E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}],s)d\upsilon

\displaystyle=|\mathbf{a}|.Next^{\leq k-1}(E[\mathop{\boldsymbol{\mathtt{ret}}}\nolimits\underline{*}],s)(U)=Next^{\leq k}(M)(U),

by induction hypothesis and Lemma
10.15
, item 3. The
remaining cases are equally easy, and left to the reader.
∎

Combining Proposition
12.6
with Theorem
10.31
, it
follows that the denotational, operational, and sampling-based operational
semantics all agree at observable types.

Theorem 12.7
.

Let
\Sigma
consist of strictly observable first-order constants, and let us
assume that the only available term of the form
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\mu]
is

\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]
, where
\lambda_{1}
is Lebesgue measure on

[0,1]
. For every observable type
\beta
, for every generalized
ISPCF term
M\colon D\beta
, for every observable open subset
U
of

\left\llbracket{\beta}\right\rrbracket
,

\left\llbracket{M}\right\rrbracket(U)=Next^{*}(M)(\overline{\overline{U}})=P[M\to\overline{\overline{U}}].

12.3
A few notes on implementation

We finish this section by briefly mentioning that the deterministic
relation
\to_{s}
is easily implemented. One only needs to be able to
compute on exact real numbers. There are quite a number of proposals
in order to do this

[
Boehm et al., 1986
,
Lester, 1992
,
Sünderhauf, 1995
,
Escardó, 1996
,
Escardó, 1996
,
Edalat et al., 1997
,
Plume, 1998
,
Marcial-Romero, 2004
,
Di Gianantonio and Lanzi, 2004
,
Lambov, 2005
,
Ho, 2013
]
.
The only modification one needs to make is that some of the exact real
numbers
\theta(x)\in\mathbf{I}\mathbb{R}
we have to consider were drawn by
sampling from
\boldsymbol{\mathtt{sample}}[\lambda_{1}]
, i.e., were obtained as

\mathrm{num}(s[i])
for some
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Reading that number up to a
specified accuracy
\epsilon>0
means reading its first
k
bits,
where
1/2^{k}<\epsilon
. In an actual implementation, we would
therefore only need (arbitrarily large) finite prefixes of an
(arbitrarily large) finite number of substrings
s[i]
, and its bits
can be drawn at random, and stored, on demand.

Explicitly, in the redundant signed-digit representation once proposed
by Boehm and Cartwright in
[
Boehm et al., 1986
]
and later by di
Gianantonio and Lanzi
[
Di Gianantonio and Lanzi, 2004
]
, and by Ho
[
Ho, 2013
]
, real
numbers between
-1
and
1
are infinite streams of digits in

\{-1,0,1\}
, to be read in base
2
. Those infinite streams are
read on demand, and can represent
s[i]
verbatim. In Boehm and
Cartwright’s second representation, real numbers
x
are encoded as
functions
\overline{x}
mapping each natural number
k
to an integer

\overline{x}(k)
such that
\overline{x}(k)/4^{k}
is at distance at
most
1/4^{k}
from
x
. One can then represent
s[i]
by maintaining
a table
T_{i}
of the first few bits of
s[i]
in mutable store. When

\overline{s[i]}(k)
is required, we make sure that at least the
first
2k
entries of
T_{i}
are populated, possibly drawing fresh
bits, uniformly and independently at random, and storing them in

T_{i}
; then we return
\sum_{j=0}^{2k-1}T_{i}[j]/2^{j+1}
.

The other primitives working on real numbers that we have considered
are well covered in the literature, except perhaps for
\mathrm{bin}^{\prime}
and

\mathrm{num}^{\prime}
, mentioned in Section
9.7
. We have
already described how
\mathrm{bin}^{\prime}
can be implement, right before
Remark
9.5
. In a redundant signed-digit
representation, Ho explains how one can compute series of the form

\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}x_{k}/2^{k+1}
, where

(x_{0},x_{1},\cdots,x_{k},\cdots)
is a stream of exact real numbers in

[-1,1]

[
Ho, 2013
, Section 3.1.2]
, based on unpublished work by
Adam Scriven. (This is also the denotation of Simpson’s

\mathtt{coerce}
operation
[
Simpson, 1998
, Figure 3]
.) From this,

\mathrm{num}^{\prime}
is easily implemented. In Boehm and Cartwright’s second
representation, one can implement
\mathrm{num}^{\prime}(s)
as the function that
maps each
k\in\mathbb{N}
to the partial, finite sum

\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{2k-1}s_{k^{\prime}}/2^{k^{\prime}+1}
if
s_{0}
, …,
s_{2k-1}
are
all different from
\bot
, and as
\bot
otherwise.
While this definition of
\mathrm{num}^{\prime}
differs slightly from
(
9.7
), it shares with it the property that
\mathrm{num}^{\prime}(s)

computes the exact real representation of
\mathrm{num}(s)
if no bit
s_{k}

is equal to
\bot
, which is the only thing we require from it.

13
Related work

We have already cited a few papers on statistical programming
languages
[
Goodman et al., 2008
,
Wood et al., 2014
,
Goodman and Stuhlmüller, 2014
,
Mansinghka et al., 2014
]

and, what is of more interest to us, on
semantics
of such
languages
[
Vákár et al., 2019
,
Heunen et al., 2017
,
Dal Lago and Hoshino, 2019
,
Ehrhard et al., 2018
]
. We
will not cite the even larger body of literature that deals with the
simpler case of probabilistic, higher-order languages without real
numbers, continuous distributions, or soft constraints. As only
exception, we mention
[
Park et al., 2008
]
, which presents a
sampling-based operational semantics for a higher-order probabilistic
call-by-value language with continuous distributions and true real
numbers, and which can be seen as a precursor of some of the
previously mentioned papers.

We have insisted on obtaining a commutative monad of continuous

R
-valuations, and we have noted that this amounts to forms of the
Fubini-Tonelli theorem. There are several such commutative monads in
the literature already, including some that predate the novel
commutative monad of
[
Vákár et al., 2019
]
. On the category of measurable
spaces, the monad of spaces of measures, popularized by M. Giry

[
Giry, 1982
]
, is commutative, as first proved by L. Tonelli

[
Tonelli, 1909
]
. (Tonelli’s theorem applies to non-negative
measurable maps, while the Fubini-Tonelli theorem applies to
measurable maps whose absolute value is integrable.) Vickers
introduced a valuation monad on the category of locales, and proved
that it is commutative, too
[
Vickers, 2011
]
. The topological
counterpart of that result is Proposition
4.1
. We
have argued in Section
4
why this does not
imply a corresponding Fubini-Tonelli theorem on the category
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}

of dcpos.

We get around this problem by considering
minimal
valuations
instead of general continuous valuations. The minimal

subprobability
valuations are an instance of a more general
construction of Jia and Mislove
[
Jia and Mislove, 2020
]
, based on Keimel and
Lawson’s
K
-completions
[
Keimel and Lawson, 2008
]
. Heckmann’s point-continuous
valuations
[
Heckmann, 1995
]
are another instance of that
construction. It was realized independently by the first and second
authors in 2020 that such constructions provide for commutative monads
on
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
. Explicitly, Jia realized that the constructions of

[
Jia and Mislove, 2020
]
all produced commutative monads, while Goubault-Larrecq
realized that the monad of point-continuous valuations was commutative
on
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
, and made it an exercise in a book in preparation

[
Goubault-Larrecq, 2020
, Exercise 12.8.11]
. The two groups decided to write up
on this, in different settings and with different applications in
mind. This led to the paper
[
Jia et al., 2021
]
by the Tulane
group, and to the present paper.

The general idea of using a form of completion of simple objects
(simple valuations, here) in order to obtain objects with better
properties is, naturally, a very common idea. A recent and topical
example is the construction of the commutative monad
T
of

[
Vákár et al., 2019
, Section 4]
. There, the authors consider the
continuation monad
J
with answers in
[0,+\infty]
. The analogue
in our case would be to define
JX
as

[[X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}]\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}]=[\mathcal{L}X\to\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}]
, and one should
realize that
\mathbf{V}X
embeds into
JX
by mapping each continuous
valuation
\nu
to its integration functional
\int\_d\nu
. Then,
they consider the so-called randomisable expectation operators, which
are obtained as the (integration functionals of) the image measures of
Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
. Finally,
T
is the smallest full
submonad of
J
that contains all the randomisable expectation
operators. A simpler, and more classical example of completion is the
notion of
s-finite
measure

[
Sharpe, 1988
,
Getoor, 1990
]
, which are the suprema of
countable chains of bounded measures; s-finite measures satisfy
Fubini-Tonelli, but are not known to form a monad on the category of
measurable sets and measurable maps. Instead, s-finite kernels form a
commutative monad on that category, which Staton used to give
semantics to a simple first-order probabilistic language

[
Staton, 2017
]
.

14
Conclusion

We have given a simple domain-theoretic, denotational semantics for a
simple statistical programming language, based on the novel notion of

minimal
valuation, forming a commutative monad over
{\mathbf{Dcpo}}
.
We believe that the notion is natural, and that the resulting
denotational semantics is simple. Through an extensive list of
examples, we hope to have demonstrated that a language based on this
semantics is able to implement a rich set of distributions, including
some non-trivial distributions on higher-order objects. We have given
three operational semantics, which are all sound and adequate, under
various, natural sets of assumptions on the semantics of available
constants. Further work include formally verified efficient
implementations, for example extending the particle Monte Carlo Markov
chain algorithm of Anglican
[
Wood et al., 2014
]
.
